The Complex Gradient Flow Equation and Seidel’s Spectral Sequence
Donghao Wang

Abstract
. Following the proposals of Donaldson-Thomas, Haydys and Gaiotto-Moore-Witten, we give a construction of Fukaya-Seidel categories for a suitable class of Morse Landau-Ginzburg models using the complex gradient flow equation, which has the potential for generalization to some infinite dimensional examples. In the course of this construction, we give an alternative proof to Seidel’s spectral sequence for Lagrangian Floer cohomology, which can be viewed as a finite dimensional model for a potential bordered monopole Floer theory. The key observation is that under a neck-stretching limit, this complex gradient flow equation produces a natural geometric filtration on the Floer cochain complex. The resulting spectral sequence is then identified with Seidel’s original one.



Dedicated to Professor Tomasz Mrowka on occasion of his 60th birthday

Part I
Introduction

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation: towards an infinite dimensional Picard-Lefschetz theory

It is the theme of Morse theory to understand the algebraic topology of a finite dimensional closed oriented manifold
N
in terms of a Morse function
f:N\to\mathbb{R}
. There are several approaches towards this goal, with different flavors and scopes of generality. The one we are most interested in is via the so-called Morse-Smale-Witten complex
[
Wit82
]
, which is freely generated by critical points of
f
as a vector space and graded by the Morse index. Let
\nabla f
denote the gradient vector field of
f
with respect to a given Riemannian metric. If the flow of
-\nabla f
satisfies certain generic conditions, then the differential map on this complex is defined by counting flowlines of
-\nabla f
connecting critical points of adjacent Morse indices:

(1.1)

p:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to N,\ \partial_{t}p(t)+\nabla f(p(t))=0.

It was Floer’s original idea
[
Flo88b
,
Flo89b
]
that this approach can be generalized to some infinite dimensional manifolds such as the action functional on the loop space of a symplectic manifold, resolving the Arnold conjecture
[
Flo89a
]
for a wide-ranging family. On the gauge theory side, Floer
[
Flo88a
]
exploited this idea to define the instanton Floer cohomology for integral homology 3-spheres which categorifies Casson’s invariant and leads to a gluing theorem for Donaldson’s invariants. Nowadays Floer cohomology is referred to any type of invariants defined as an infinite dimensional Morse cohomology and has been a powerful tool in tackling many important problems in low dimensional topology and symplectic topology.

As the complex analogue of Morse theory, it is the theme of Picard-Lefschetz theory to understand the symplectic topology of a complete Kähler manifold
M
in terms of a holomorphic Morse function
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
(called superpotential). Such a pair
(M,W)
is called a Landau-Ginzburg model and is used extensively in mirror symmetry to formulate the so-called Calabi-Yau/Landau-Ginzburg correspondence
[
VW89
,
Mar90
,
Wit93
,
FJR13
]
. A holomorphic Morse function exhibits very different properties from the real ones. If all critical points of
W
are non-degenerate and finite, then they must concentrate on the same cohomological grading due to the holomorphicity of
W
. For a generic
e^{i\theta}\in S^{1}
, the Morse-Smale-Witten complex of
\re(e^{-i\theta}W)
is well-defined but boring, as the differential map on this complex is always trivial. The correct symplectic invariant to be defined for
(M,W)
, due to the work of Seidel
[
Sei08
,
Sei12
]
, is the so-called Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
, which in our context is a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category whose objects are in bijection with the critical set of
W
, and as such is a more refined invariant than the Morse cohomology of
\re(e^{-i\theta}W)
.

The theme of this paper is to extend Floer’s idea further to the case of holomorphic Morse functions and develop a prototype for an infinite dimensional Picard-Lefschetz theory. This allows us to transport existing techniques/results from symplectic topology (as finite dimensional toy models) to understand gauge theory (the infinite dimensional case). There are several instances in the literature where infinite dimensional Landau-Ginzburg models are considered.

Example 1.1
.

Let
M
be the space of
\bar{\partial}
-operators on a given complex vector bundle
E
on a Calabi-Yau 3-fold
\mathcal{Y}
, and
W
the holomorphic Chern-Simons functional; a critical point of
W
corresponds to an integrable operator giving rise to a genuine holomorphic structure
E
. This example motivates the construction of Donaldson-Thomas invariants
[
Tho00
]
as a counting of critical points of
W
.
∎

Example 1.2
.

Let
M
be the space of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connections on a given closed oriented 3-manifold
Z
, and
W
the complex Chern-Simons functional; a critical point of
W
corresponds to a flat
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-connection, which gives a representation of
\pi_{1}(Z)
into
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. This example motivates the work of Abouzaid-Manolescu
[
AM20
]
on a sheaf theoretic model of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-Floer homology.
∎

Example 1.3
.

Let
M
be the Seiberg-Witten configuration space on a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
, and
W
the Dirac functional
[
Wan20a
]
. This example motivates the author’s earlier work on monopole Floer cohomology for 3-manifolds with toroidal boundary
[
Wan20b
,
Wan20c
]
.
∎

Example 1.4
.

Take a holomorphic symplectic manifold
\mathcal{N}
with a pair of holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds
\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Let
M
be the space of paths
\gamma:[0,1]_{s}\to\mathcal{N}
with
\gamma(j)\in\mathcal{L}_{j},j=0,1
, and
W
the complex action functional. This example motivates the ongoing project by Kontsevich-Soibelman on holomorphic Floer theory; see
[
KS22
]
.
∎

The idea of developing Picard-Lefschetz theory in the infinite dimensional case originates with the work of Donaldson-Thomas
[
DT98
]
, with more detailed plans proposed by Haydys
[
Hay15
]
and Gaiotto-Moore-Witten
[
GMW15
,
GMW17
]
. The main obstacle in this line of thoughts involves generalizing Lagrangian Floer cohomology for infinite dimensional Lagrangian submanifolds
[
Ngu12
,
Ngu18
]
. As the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
comprises only a very special class of Lagrangian submanifolds called thimbles, this obstacle may be circumvented by considering the complex analogue of (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
with the real operator
\partial_{t}
replaced by the complex operator
\partial_{t}+i\partial_{s}
and
f
by the Hamiltonian function
\im(e^{-i\theta}W)
:

(1.2)

P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M,\ \partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla\im(e^{-i\theta}W)(P(t,s))=0.

The
\theta=0
version of (
1.2
) has been studied in the literature under many names:
the complex gradient flow equation

[
DT98
]
, or the Witten equation
[
FJR13
]
as used extensively in the quantum singularity theory (FJRW theory). In this paper, (
1.2
) will be called
the
\theta
-instanton equation
following the convention of
[
GMW15
, Chapter 14]
(note that they used the Greek letter
\zeta
instead of
\theta
). The moral to take away from this paper is that (
1.2
) seems to be a more natural equation to consider in the realm of Landau-Ginzburg models, as opposed to the Cauchy-Riemann equation in the traditional symplectic literature, though (
1.2
) is only a perturbed version of the latter. As the first hint, this
\theta
-instanton equation (
1.2
) is the toy model to understand the 2-dimensional reduction of many gauge theoretic equations, as summarized in the list below.

\eqref{Intro.E.2}\text{ with }(M,W)\text{ as in }\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{Example }\ref{Intro.EX.1}&\Rightarrow\text{ the Spin(7)-instanton equation on }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{Y},\\
\text{Example }\ref{Intro.EX.2}&\Rightarrow\text{ the Haydys-Witten equations on }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times Z,\\
\text{Example }\ref{Intro.EX.3}&\Rightarrow\text{ the Seiberg-Witten equations on }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\Sigma,\\
\text{Example }\ref{Intro.EX.4}&\Rightarrow\text{ the Fueter equation on }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,1].\end{array}\right.

Donaldson-Thomas’ original proposal
[
DT98
]
is to construct a Fukaya-Seidel category for the holomorphic Chern-Simons functional by counting Spin(7)-instantons on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{Y}
and define more refined deformation invariants of the Calabi-Yau 3-fold
\mathcal{Y}
. The Donaldson-Thomas invariant is then interpreted as the number of objects of this directed
A_{\infty}
-category. The work of Haydys
[
Hay15
]
and Gaiotto-Moor-Witten
[
GMW15
,
GMW17
]
is motivated by Witten’s proposal
[
Wit12
]
to define Khovanov homology for knots in a general 3-manifold by studying the Kapustin-Witten equations
[
KW07
]
, which is the dimensional reduction of the Haydys-Witten equations on a 4-manifold.

Following this line of thoughts, this paper is devoted to a detailed construction of Fukaya-Seidel categories for Landau-Ginzburg models
(M,W)
satisfying a tameness condition (see Definition
2.1
; this condition is inspired by
[
FJY18
]
), which has the potential for generalization to the infinite dimensional Example
1.3
; other examples remain difficult to tackle due to a compactness issue: a local compactness property (Lemma
3.1
) which is well-known for the Seiberg-Witten equations fails in general for other equations in this list
[
Tau13
]
. The construction of the Fukaya-Seidel category for the Dirac superpotential along with some concrete computations of examples will appear in a companion paper in the future.

The present paper focuses on the finite dimensional case and explains in detail the geometric input required for such a construction. We remark that in the finite dimensional case there have been at least two approaches to construct Fukaya-Seidel categories for Morse Landau-Ginzburg models: either via vanishing cycles in the fiber of
W:M\to\mathbb{C}

[
Sei08
]
or via the partially wrapped Fukaya category
[
Sei12
,
Syl19
,
GPS20
]
. The geometric inputs required in these approaches, however, are not quite available in the infinite dimension case. On the other hand, our construction, though inspired by their works, is targeted specifically at the infinite dimensional generalization and is somewhat inconvenient for finite dimensional applications, as some formal structures are lost along this line. Nevertheless, as a concrete application, we give an alternative proof to Seidel’s spectral sequence within our framework, which serves as a finite dimensional model for a potential bordered monopole Floer theory.

1.2.
Towards a bordered monopole Floer theory

The Seiberg-Witten Floer cohomology is a powerful invariant introduced by Kronheimer-Mrowka
[
KM07
]
for any closed oriented 3-manifold
Z
, which fits into a (3+1) TQFT and encodes important topological information about
Z
. This paper is motivated by an attempt to develop a bordered monopole Floer theory which computes a version of this Floer cohomology in terms of a splitting of
Z
. Suppose that
Z=Z_{0}\cup_{\Sigma}Z_{1}
is separated by a connected closed surface
\Sigma
, where
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
are 3-manifolds with boundary
\partial Z_{0}\cong\Sigma\cong(-\partial Z_{1})
. Let
M(\Sigma)
denote the infinite dimensional Kähler manifold in Example
1.3
and
W_{\mathcal{D}}
the Dirac superpotential. The Seiberg-Witten gauge theory is then tied to symplectic topology by a theorem of Nguyen.

Theorem 1.5

(
[
Ngu12
]
)
.

The solution space of the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations on
Z_{0}
(resp.
Z_{1}
) restricts to an infinite dimensional (possibly immersed) Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}_{0}
(reps.
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) in
M(\Sigma)
on which
\re W_{\mathcal{D}}
is bounded above (resp. below).

In this theorem one has to fix a cylindrical metric on
Z_{0}
(resp. on
Z_{1}
). Let
g_{R}
be the metric on
Z
obtained by gluing
Z_{0}
,
Z_{1}
with a long neck
[0,R]_{s}\times\Sigma
:

(Z,g_{R})=Z_{0}\cup[0,R]_{s}\times\Sigma\cup Z_{1}.

The dictionary between symplectic topology and the Seiberg-Witten gauge theory is then summarized by the table below.

Symplectic Topology

the Seiberg-Witten Gauge Theory

(M(\Sigma),W_{\mathcal{D}})

\Sigma

\mathcal{L}_{0}

Z_{0}

\mathcal{L}_{1}

Z_{1}

the space of paths
\gamma:[0,R]_{s}\to M

with
\gamma(0)\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\gamma(R)\in\mathcal{L}_{1}

the Seiberg-Witten

configuration space on
(Z,g_{R})

the symplectic action

functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
perturbed by
W

the Chern-Simons-Dirac

functional on
(Z,g_{R})

the complex gradient

flow equation
(
1.2
)
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R]_{s}

the Seiberg-Witten

equations on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(Z,g_{R})

the Floer cochain complex

\Ch^{*}_{R}(\mathcal{L}_{0},\mathcal{L}_{1})

the Seiberg-Witten Floer

cochain complex of
(Z,g_{R})

In contrast with the Atiyah-Floer conjecture
[
Ati88
]
which assigns to each oriented surface
\Sigma
the Fukaya category of a compact symplectic manifold, the picture we advertise here assigns to each
\Sigma
the Fukaya-Seidel category of an infinite dimensional Landau-Ginzburg model. The main result of this paper suggests that under the neck-stretching limit as
R\to\infty
,
the energy filtration induced by the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
on the Seiberg-Witten Floer cochain complex of
(Z,g_{R})

has a particularly nice structure
, which may be understood concretely in terms of
A_{\infty}
-invariants associated to
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
. We remark that the Dirac superpotential
W_{\mathcal{D}}
is not Morse, and one has to perturb the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional by a closed 2-form on
Z_{R}
in order to see this special structure, so only a variant of the reduced monopole Floer cohomology can be recovered through this approach. In next section we explain in some detail the geometric origin of this filtration and why Seidel’s result is viewed as a toy model for this bordered theory.

1.3.
Main Results: Seidel’s Spectral Sequence

From now on we focus on the finite dimensional case and review the basic setup. Throughout this paper we work with a base field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
2
. Let
(M,W)
be any tame Landau-Ginzburg model in the sense of Definition
2.1
, so
M
is exact with
c_{1}(TM,J_{M})=0
. Let
L=\re W
and
H=\im W
denote the real and imaginary part of
W
respectively. Assume that the critical set
\Crit(W)=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}\}
is finite and ordered such that

(1.3)

H(x_{1})<H(x_{2})<\cdots<H(x_{m}).

Denote by
S_{n}
(resp.
U_{n}
) the stable (resp. unstable) submanifold of
L
associated to
x_{n}\in\Crit(W)
, then
W(S_{n})\subset\mathbb{C}
(resp.
W(U_{n})
) is a ray emanating from the critical value
W(x_{n})
parallel to the real axis; see Figure
1
below.
S_{n}
(resp.
U_{n}
) is also the Lefschetz thimble along this ray, which can be made into a graded exact Lagrangian submanifold. Then Seidel’s theorem says the following.

Theorem 1.6

(
[
Sei08
, Corollary 18.27]
)
.

For any pair of compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifolds
X,Y\subset M
, there is a spectral sequence:

(1.4)

\bigoplus_{n=1}^{m}\HFF^{*}(U_{n},Y)\otimes\HFF^{*}(X,S_{n})\rightrightarrows\HFF^{*}(X,Y).

Seidel’s original proof of Theorem
1.6
relies on his deep generating theorem
[
Sei08
, Corollary 18.25]
for the Fukaya category of
(M,W)
, so (
1.4
) is only constructed algebraically (though this proof only works over a field of char
\neq 2
, we shall ignore this technical point in this exposition). The advantage of this approach is that all higher differentials of this spectral sequence can be computed step by step using his algebraic recipes, so (
1.4
) is viewed as a computational tool for the Floer cohomology group
\HFF^{*}(X,Y)
. In the sequel we shall refer to (
1.4
) as the algebraic spectral sequence.

\begin{overpic}[Pic57.png]
\put(-10.0,19.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$U_{1}$}
\put(-10.0,29.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$U_{2}$}
\put(-10.0,46.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$U_{3}$}
\put(-10.0,59.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$U_{4}$}
\put(105.0,19.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$S_{1}$}
\put(105.0,29.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$S_{2}$}
\put(105.0,46.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$S_{3}$}
\put(105.0,59.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$S_{4}$}
\put(47.0,23.0){$W(x_{1})$}
\put(66.0,33.0){$W(x_{2})$}
\put(37.0,50.0){$W(x_{3})$}
\put(57.0,63.0){$W(x_{4})$}
\put(8.0,2.0){$Y$}
\put(88.0,2.0){$X$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1
.
The images of thimbles under
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
.

We now explain the geometric origin of (
1.4
) from the complex gradient flow equation (
1.2
). The Floer cohomology group
\HFF^{*}(X,Y)
can be defined by counting
\theta
-instantons (with
\theta=0
) on an infinite strip
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R]_{s}
with Lagrangian boundary conditions, where the stretching parameter
R>0
is allowed to vary:

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R]_{s}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla H&=0,\\
P(t,R)&\in Y,\\
P(t,0)&\in X.\end{array}\right.

Denote the resulting Floer complex by
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
. We may scale the domain to be
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}
so that (
1.5
) takes the form

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\widetilde{P}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}\widetilde{P}+J\partial_{s}\widetilde{P}+R\cdot\nabla H&=0,\\
\widetilde{P}(t,1)&\in Y,\\
\widetilde{P}(t,0)&\in X.\end{array}\right.

with
\widetilde{P}(t,s)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P(R\cdot t,R\cdot s),(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}
. In the second case (
1.6
), one may even set
R=0
and return to the classical realm of
J
-holomorphic curves.

However, the secret of the spectral sequence (
1.4
) is revealed by taking
R\gg 1
in (
1.5
). To this end, we wish to understand the energy filtration on
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
. A generator of
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
(also called a soliton) is a path
p_{R}:[0,R]_{s}\to M
following the flow of
-\nabla L
, since
\nabla H=J\nabla L
:

(1.7)

J\partial_{s}p_{R}+\nabla H=J(\partial_{s}p_{R}+\nabla L)=0\text{ with }p_{R}(0)\in X\text{ and }p_{R}(R)\in Y.

The Hamiltonian function
H
is constant along
p_{R}
. For
R\gg 1
, any soliton
p_{R}
must approximate a broken flowline of
-\nabla L
, and the condition (
1.3
) ensures that only one break is possible for this approximation (think of the red and blue curves associated to the same critical point in Figure
1
as a concatenation of such a broken flowline projected on
\mathbb{C}
). Thus the Floer complex
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
is a direct sum

(1.8)

\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{m}V_{R}^{n},\ R\gg 1

where
V_{R}^{n}
is the subspace generated by solitons approximating the
n
-th critical point
x_{n}\in\Crit(W)
in the middle of the interval
[0,R]_{s}
.

Our next step is to understand the Floer differential
\partial
acting on the direct sum (
1.8
).
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
is the Morse-Smale-Witten complex of a perturbed symplectic action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
on the path space between
X
and
Y
, and each soliton is a critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
. A short computation (Lemma
9.5
) shows that

(1.9)

\mathcal{A}_{W,R}(p_{R})=R\cdot H(x_{n})+\mathcal{O}(1),R\gg 1,

if
p_{R}
is a generator of
V_{R}^{n}
, so this value is blowing up linearly as
R\to\infty
with the leading order term given by
H(x_{n})
. Meanwhile, the Floer differential
\partial
on
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
can only increase the value of
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
and hence the value of
H(x_{n})
. This implies that the differential map
\partial
acting on (
1.8
) is a lower triangular matrix for
R\gg 1
. Thus the complex
\Ch_{R}^{*}(X,Y)
is endowed with an decreasing filtration:

\Ch_{R}(X,Y)=\Ch_{R}^{(1)}(X,Y)\supset\cdots\supset\Ch_{R}^{(m+1)}(X,Y)=\{0\},

with

(1.10)

\Ch_{R}^{(n)}(X,Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{j=n}^{m}V^{j}_{R},\ 1\leq n\leq m+1,

and which induces a spectral sequence

(1.11)

\bigoplus_{n=1}^{m}H(V_{R}^{n})\rightrightarrows H(\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y))\cong\HFF^{*}(X,Y).

We refer to (
1.11
) as the geometric spectral sequence. Using a vertical gluing theorem (Section
11
), one verifies that
H(V_{R}^{n})
is isomorphic to the tensor product
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(U_{n},Y)\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,S_{n})
, where
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
is a variant of
\HFF^{*}
(see Section
1.5
) defined by counting
\theta
-instantons on the upper/lower half planes. Another application of the vertical gluing theorem proves that in fact
\HFF^{*}\cong\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
(Section
13.9
), so the geometric spectral sequence (
1.11
) has the same
E_{1}
-page as the algebraic spectral sequence (
1.4
). The main result of this paper then says that their higher pages are also isomorphic.

Theorem 1.7

(The main result: the preliminary version; Theorem
13.11
)
.

The geometric spectral sequence (
1.11
) is isomorphic to Seidel’s algebraic spectral sequence (
1.4
).

Remark 1.8
.

The spectral sequence (
1.4
) was first suggested by Donaldson based on the following observation
[
Sei08
, Remark 18.28]
: if one considers the product manifold
M^{-}\times M
, where the sign of the symplectic form is reversed on the first factor, as well as the Hamiltonian flow of the function
\widetilde{H}(x,y)=H(x)+H(y)
, then part of the diagonal
\Delta_{M}\subset M\times M
may diverge within finite time, while the rest will approximate the disjoint union
\coprod_{n}S_{n}\times U_{n}
, suggesting a Lagrangian cobordism:

(1.12)

\Delta_{M}\xrightarrow[\text{Hamiltonian Flow}]{\text{Lag. Cob.}}\coprod_{n=1}^{m}S_{n}\times U_{n},

The general framework of Biran-Cornea
[
BC13
]
will then produce a spectral sequence by pairing (
1.12
) with the product Lagrangian submanifold
X\times Y\subset M^{-}\times M
. However, this Lagrangian cobordism is never constructed explicitly in the literature. In our story this Hamiltonian flow is replaced by taking a large stretching parameter
R\gg 1
in the equation (
1.5
), and Biran-Cornea’s framework is replaced by the geometric spectral sequence (
1.11
).
∎

To prove Theorem
1.7
, one has to construct Seidel’s algebraic spectral sequence (
1.4
) within our framework. To this end, we construct in this paper using a variant of the
\theta
-instanton equation (
1.2
):

•

a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}
using all stable thimbles
S_{1},\cdots,S_{m}
,
m=|\Crit(W)|
, called the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
;

•

a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{B}
using all unstable thimbles
U_{m},\cdots,U_{1}
;

•

a diagonal bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
with the property

(1.13)

\Delta(S_{k},U_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{K}&\text{ if }j=k,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

For any compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifold
X
, we construct

•

a left
\mathcal{A}
-module
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
and a right
\mathcal{B}
-module
X_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Theorem
1.7
is then refined as follows.

Theorem 1.9

(The main result: the complete version)
.

For any finite dimensional tame Landau-Ginzburg model
(M,W)

(
so
M
is exact with
2c_{1}(TM,J)=0)
and any compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifolds
X,Y
, the following holds:

(1)

(\text{Theorem }\ref{FS.T.3})

{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
induces a cohomological full and faithful embedding
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
is the dg-category of right
\mathcal{B}
-modules;

(2)

(\text{Theorem }\ref{SS.T.2})
for
R\gg 1
, there is a quasi-isomorphism

(1.14)

\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}}),

which identifies the geometric filtration on the left with the algebraic filtration on the right;

(3)

(
Corollary
13.5
; the wall-crossing formula
)
there is a quasi-isomorphism in the dg-category
\mathcal{Q}_{r}

(1.15)

X_{\mathcal{B}}\to\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}X,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})

where
\mathcal{P}_{l}
denotes the dg-category of left
\mathcal{A}
-modules.

Theorem
1.9

(
1
)

and the property (
1.13
) imply that
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
forms Koszul-duality pair, so one determines the other. The algebraic filtration on the complex
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}})
is induced from an increasing filtration on
Y_{\mathcal{B}}
(see
[
Sei08
, Remark 5.25]
or Section
6.6
), which defines the algebraic spectral sequence (
1.4
). Thus Theorem
1.7
follows from Theorem
1.9

(
2
)

.

Remark 1.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{c}(M)
denote the Fukaya category of compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifolds in
M
. Theorem
1.9

(
2
)

may be used to show that there is a cohomologically full and faithful functor

(1.16)

\mathcal{F}_{c}(M)\to\mathcal{Q}_{r},

reproving Seidel’s result
[
Sei08
, Corollary 18.25]
. The author confesses that the analysis of this paper is not enough to construct this
A_{\infty}
-functor (
1.16
). This failure may be remedied by assuming further that
(M,\omega_{M})
is a Liouville manifold (this is not a requirement of tameness) and by combining the compactness argument of this paper with that in
[
Sei08
, Section 7]
. Nevertheless, this is considered as a relatively minor drawback comparing to our potential infinite dimensional application.
∎

Composing (
1.14
) with a quasi-inverse of (
1.15
), we obtain that

Corollary 1.11

(Corollary
13.6
)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.9
, we have a quasi-isomorphism for all
R>0
:

(1.17)

\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}X,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}),Y_{\mathcal{B}}).

Remark 1.12
.

In light of Remark
1.8
, one may think of the diagonal bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
as the algebraic invariant associated to
\Delta_{M}\subset M\times M
, and the right hand side of (
1.17
) should be viewed as the algebraic pairing between
\Delta_{M}
and
X\times Y
. Thus the quasi-isomorphism (
1.17
) can be read as: the geometric pairing of
\Delta_{M}
and
X\times Y
is “equal” to their algebraic pairing.
∎

1.4.
The idea of Theorem
1.9

Although the analogue of Theorem
1.9
is well-known in the literature, we emphasize that our strategy is new and independent of any existing proof of the generating theorem
[
Sei08
,
GPS20
]
, which we now describe. As in the case of
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
, the right
\mathcal{B}
-module
X_{\mathcal{B}}
and the diagonal bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
can be deformed (up to quasi-isomorphisms) into a filtered
\mathcal{B}
-module and a filtered
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule respectively under a neck-stretching limit with
R\gg 1
:

\displaystyle 0=X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(0)}\subset\cdots\subset X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(n)}\subset X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(n+1)}\subset\cdots\subset X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=X_{\mathcal{B},R},

\displaystyle 0=\Delta_{R}^{(0)}\subset\cdots\subset\Delta_{R}^{(n)}\subset\Delta_{R}^{(n+1)}\subset\cdots\subset\Delta_{R}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{R}.

These increasing filtrations are induced by the symplectic action functionals and depend a priori on the stretching parameter
R\gg 1
(one has to work with the
\theta
-instanton equation (
1.2
) with
\theta=\pi
here). The
n
-th filtered submodule/sub-bimodule involves the first
n
critical points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
of
W
in (
1.3
). The chain map and
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism defined naturally from the geometry preserve these filtrations, so (
1.14
) and (
1.15
) are refined respectively as

\displaystyle\Ch_{R}^{(n+1)}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B},R}/X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(n)},Y_{\mathcal{B}}),

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{B},R}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}X,\Delta_{R}^{(n)}),\ 0\leq n\leq m.

The idea behind this proof is the simple algebraic fact that a map between spectral sequences is an isomorphism between the
E_{\infty}
-page if and only if it is on the
E_{1}
-page. Thus one may pass to the associated graded complexes to verify that these maps are quasi-isomorphisms. In the latter case, only one critical point of
W
is involved in the picture, and these maps can be understood concretely using a vertical gluing theorem by taking
R\to\infty
.

Remark 1.13
.

Since
S_{j}\cap S_{k}=\emptyset
for
j\neq k
, we have to perturb the stable and unstable thimbles by some small angles in the construction of
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
and work with a perturbed version of (
1.2
), called the
\alpha
-instanton equation, where the angle
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
is allowed to change and hence replaced by a function
\alpha:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
that is constant when
|s|\gg 1
:

(1.18)

P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M,\ \partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)(P(t,s))=0.

With suitable asymptotic conditions at infinity, counting
\alpha
-instantons gives an alternative route to define the Floer cohomology for a pair of thimbles without using the Lagrangian boundary conditions, which refines the earlier proposals of Haydys and Gaiotto-Moore-Witten.

The bulk of this paper is devoted to a detailed construction of these
A_{\infty}
-invariants. We emphasize that with a few analytic results in place, Theorem
1.9
follows rather formally from the existence of the geometric filtration (
1.10
). In some sense, the complex gradient flow equation (
1.2
) is “smart” enough to establish the algebraic spectral sequence in its own right.
∎

1.5.
Relation with Gaiotto-Moore-Witten’s proposal: a vertical gluing theorem

In the last step of the proof of Theorem
1.9
, we have to use an analytic gluing theorem to compute the
E_{1}
-pages of the spectral sequences and the map between them. We explain how this is done for the Floer complex
\Ch_{R}^{*}(X,Y)
. First, introduce a Floer cohomology of
(X,S_{n})
by counting
\theta
-instantons on the upper half planes (with
\theta=0
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,+\infty)_{s}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla H&=0,\\
P(t,0)&\in X,\\
\lim_{s\to\infty}P(t,s)&=x_{n}\text{ exponentially and uniformly in $t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}$},\end{array}\right.

where the convergence is understood as in (
2.13
) below. A generator of the Floer complex
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(X,S_{n})
is a path
p_{X}:[0,+\infty)_{s}\to M
following the flow of
-\nabla L
with
p_{X}(0)\in X\cap S_{n}
; the subscript
\natural
is to distinguish
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}
from the complex defined by counting
\theta
-instantons on a strip. Meanwhile, define a Floer cohomology of
(U_{n},Y)
by counting instantons on the lower half planes:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(-\infty,0]_{s}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla H&=0,\\
P(t,0)&\in Y,\\
\lim_{s\to-\infty}P(t,s)&=x_{n}\text{ exponentially and uniformly in $t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}$}.\end{array}\right.

A generator of
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{n},Y)
is a path
p_{Y}:(-\infty,0]_{s}\to M
following the flow of
-\nabla L
with
p_{Y}(0)\in U_{n}\cap Y
. The standard gluing theorem in Morse-Smale-Witten theory implies that any soliton
p_{R}\in V_{R}^{n}\subset\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
(cf. (
1.7
)) is obtained by gluing some
p_{X}
with
p_{Y}
at
x_{n}
, and hence gives an isomorphism between vector spaces,

(1.19)

\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{n},Y)\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(X,S_{n})\to V_{R}^{n}\subset\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y).

The upshot is that (
1.19
) is also an isomorphism between chain complexes for
R\gg 1
: one can also glue instantons vertically to identify the differential maps on both sides. This observation was first suggested by Gaiotto-Moore-Witten
[
GMW15
]
and is verified in Section
11
. This gluing result is possible because by (
1.9
) the drop of the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
along a Floer differential on
V_{R}^{n}
is uniformly bounded as
R\to\infty
. Any instanton with this property on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R]_{s}
approximates the critical point
x_{n}\in\Crit(W)
exponentially as
(t,s)
tends to the middle line
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{\frac{R}{2}\}
— it is then compared with a solution when the neck is completely stretched, i.e., when
R=+\infty
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic67.png]
\put(-18.0,9.0){\small$\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R]_{s}$}
\put(-3.0,1.0){\small$X$}
\put(5.0,9.0){\small$x_{n}$}
\put(-3.0,16.0){\small$Y$}
\put(92.0,20.0){\small$Y$}
\put(92.0,0.0){\small$X$}
\put(75.0,13.0){\small$x_{n}$}
\put(75.0,6.0){\small$x_{n}$}
\put(40.0,12.0){\small converge}
\put(44.0,6.0){\small glue}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2
.
Gluing Floer differentials vertically.

In fact, a general framework is proposed in
[
GMW15
]
to understand all differentials on the Floer complex
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
as
R\to\infty
which may go between different filtration levels with the drop of
\mathcal{A}_{W,R}
blowing up linearly. However, as we have followed Seidel’s algebraic framework, their full proposal is not implemented in this paper.

1.6.
Future direction I: Non-compact Lagrangian submanifolds

The
A_{\infty}
-modules
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
and
Y_{\mathcal{B}}
can be defined more generally for suitable non-compact
X,Y,
so one expects Corollary
1.11
to hold in greater generality. The optimal result we obtain is the following.

Theorem 1.14

(In preparation)
.

Suppose that
(M,W)
is tame, and
X,Y\subset M
are any exact graded Lagrangian submanifold with bounded geometry and such that for some
C>0
,

\displaystyle\re(e^{-i\beta_{X}}W)|_{X}<C\text{ for some }\beta_{X}\in[-\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{\pi}{3}],

\displaystyle\re(e^{-i\beta_{Y}}W)|_{Y}<C\text{ for some }\beta_{Y}\in[\frac{2\pi}{3},\frac{4\pi}{3}].

Then the chain map (
1.17
) is a quasi-isomorphism. Figure
1
illustrates the case when
\beta_{X}=0
and
\beta_{Y}=\pi
.

Remark 1.15
.

Theorem
1.14
explains why different
A_{\infty}
-modules are used for
X,Y
in Corollary
1.11
— the projections
W(X),W(Y)\subset\mathbb{C}
are bounded above in different directions. In this context the Floer cohomology of
(X,Y)
is not well-defined, and the complex
\Ch_{R}^{*}(X,Y)
in (
1.17
) is defined by counting a special class of solitons and instantons, and whose invariance is only verified via Theorem
1.14
. These solitons are required to approximate some critical points of
W
when
R\gg 1
, so the decomposition (
1.8
) remains valid. In light of Nguyen’s Theorem
1.5
, Theorem
1.14
is the more relevant version for our infinite dimensional application.
∎

Remark 1.16
.

(Warning to the reader) Theorem
1.14
is not proved in this Arxiv preprint, as it requires more sophisticated analysis to establish the compactness theorem. An appendix (
\sim
20 pages) is in preparation to address this in detail. The version proved in Section
13
is also for non-compact
X,Y
but assumes in addition that
H|_{X}>C
and
H|_{Y}<C
(so the intersection
W(X)\cap W(Y)\subset\mathbb{C}
remains compact), and its proof is identical to the case when
X,Y
are both compact.
∎

Remark 1.17
.

Although we have advertised this paper mainly through Seidel’s spectral sequence, (
1.17
) is the gluing formula we wish to generalize for the Dirac superpotential in Example
1.3
. Returning to our discussion of the bordered monopole Floer theory in Section
1.2
, as our Lagrangian submanifolds originate with Nguyen’s Theorem
1.5
, the Lagrangian boundary condition will be removed completely in our final application in gauge theory. To develop a bordered theory along this line, one has to construct:

•

directed
A_{\infty}
-categories
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
along with a diagonal bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
for the Dirac superpotential;

•

a left
\mathcal{A}
-module for
Z_{0}
and a right
\mathcal{B}
-module for
Z_{1}
.

The geometric filtration on the monopole Floer cochain complex of
Z_{R}=Z_{0}\cup[0,R]_{s}\times\Sigma\cup Z_{1}
with
R\gg 1
is induced by the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional in the same ways as in (
1.8
). One would hope that a formula similar to (
1.17
) will recover this Floer cohomology along with this spectral sequence in terms of the
A_{\infty}
-invariants of
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
, where the contribution of
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
is separated. In the genus
g(\Sigma)=1
case, the underlying cochain complexes of these
A_{\infty}
-modules have been constructed in the author’s earlier paper
[
Wan20b
]
. We hope more will be explored in the future.
∎

1.7.
Future direction II: a Picard-Lefschetz-Novikov theory

One technical point, however, complicates the discussion of Remark
1.17
: the Dirac superpotential
W_{\mathcal{D}}
, once perturbed into a Morse function, is not single-valued on the quotient configuration space on
\Sigma
. In the finite dimensional case, this is saying that
W:M\to\mathbb{T}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z})
is a holomorphic map onto a 2-torus with finitely many critical points. Thus a Picard-Lefschetz-Novikov theory is needed in order to develop a complete bordered theory along this line (as the complex analogue of the Morse-Novikov theory
[
Nov81
,
Nov82
]
for
S^{1}
-valued Morse functions).

As an intermediate step, we should first understand the case when
W:M\to\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
is valued in a cylinder. Suppose that
X,Y\subset M
are compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifolds, then the Hamiltonian function
H=\im W:M\to S^{1}\cong\mathbb{R}/2\pi
used in (
1.5
) is circle-valued, and hence the complex
\Ch^{*}_{R}(X,Y)
is only defined over a Novikov ring
\mathbb{K}[[U]]
, where the exponent of the
U
-variable keeps track of how many times an instanton goes around the circle factor (or equivalently one equips a local system on
M
using
H:M\to S^{1}
here).

\begin{overpic}[Pic66.png]
\put(21.0,51.0){$\widetilde{M}$}
\put(79.0,51.0){$M$}
\put(82.0,35.0){$W=L+iH$}
\put(25.0,42.0){$\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{L}+i\widetilde{H}$}
\put(75.0,5.0){$X,Y\subset M$}
\put(39.0,0.0){$\widetilde{X}$}
\put(0.0,0.0){$\widetilde{Y}$}
\put(-5.0,13.0){$\Omega$}
\put(50.0,18.0){$\pi$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3
.
A superpotential
W
valued in
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
.

Consider the pull-back of
W:M\to\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
via the quotient map
\pi:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}/2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
, and denote this new fibration by
\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{L}+i\widetilde{H}:\widetilde{M}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
\widetilde{X}
(resp.
\widetilde{Y}
) be the lift of
X
(resp.
Y
) in this covering space,
\Omega=\mathbb{R}\times[0,2\pi]\subset\mathbb{C}
a fundamental domain of
\pi
, and
\Crit(\widetilde{W})=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}\}
the set of critical points of
\widetilde{W}
lying over
\Omega
, which are ordered by

\widetilde{H}(x_{1})<\widetilde{H}(x_{2})<\cdots<\widetilde{H}(x_{m}).

To each critical point
x_{n}\in\Crit(\widetilde{W})
is associated a pair of thimbles
(U_{n},S_{n})
, and the Floer cohomology group
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(U_{n},\widetilde{Y})
and
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\widetilde{X},S_{n})
are well-defined over
\mathbb{K}
. The geometric spectral sequence (
1.11
) is then generalized to this case to give

\bigg(\bigoplus_{n=1}^{m}\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(U_{n},\widetilde{Y})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\widetilde{X},S_{n})\bigg)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{K}[[U]]\rightrightarrows H(\Ch_{R}^{*}(X,Y)).

To the best of our knowledge, Seidel’s generating theorem is not available yet in this context. The proof of Theorem
1.9
or Theorem
1.14
has a local nature, which may be generalized to understand any quotient subcomplex of
\Ch_{R}^{*}(X,Y)
with finitely many filtration levels, truncating the coefficient ring from
\mathbb{K}[[U]]
into
\mathbb{K}[U]/U^{n}
for any
n\geq 1
. A complete description may be then obtained by algebraically taking
n\to\infty
. The author wishes to return to this topic in the near future.

Remark 1.18
.

Another motivation for this development is for Example
1.2
: the complex Chern-Simons functional is a superpotential valued in
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
. Although the analysis of Haydys-Witten equations is difficult, it might be helpful to understand first the type of the algebraic invariants one should associate to any closed 3-manifold via this superpotential.
∎

1.8.
Outline of the paper

This paper is organized as follows.

Part
II
is devoted to the construction of Floer cohomology of thimbles by counting the
\alpha
-instantons (
1.18
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
. The analytic foundation of this Floer theory has been developed in the context of the Seiberg-Witten theory in
[
Wan20b
,
Wan20b
]
, so some arguments might be sketchy here. The invariance of this Floer cohomology is then verified using the continuation method.

Part
III
is devoted to the construction of the Fukaya-Seidel category
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
. The idea is to attach lower half planes to the boundary of pointed disks and count instantons on these complete Riemann surfaces. Their invariance is then verified using categorical localization in Section
8
. The filtered version of
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
is constructed in Section
9
. The Koszul duality pattern between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is explained in Section
10
, which also proves Theorem
1.9

(
1
)

. The vertical gluing theorem, which is the main analytic input of this work, is proved in Section
11
.

Part
IV
is devoted to the proof of Seidel’s spectral sequence, i.e., Theorem
1.9

(
2
)

and

(
3
)

. With all analytic tools developed in previous sections, they follow from a simple algebraic argument. Finally, some applications of the vertical gluing theorem is collected in Section
13
, including a special case of Seidel’s long exact sequence
[
Sei03
]
and an isomorphism of our Floer cohomology with the classical one defined by counting solutions on strips.

The author has tried to reconcile the conflict of interests for readers from different background by making this paper more or less self-contained. This explains partly why this paper is much longer than expected. Experts should feel free to skip the expository materials in these sections.

Acknowledgment.
The author would like thank his thesis advisor, Tom Mrowka, for his guidance and constant encouragement throughout this project. The author would also like to thank Shaoyun Bai, Simon Donaldson, Andrew Hanlon, Andriy Haydys, Runjie Hu, Jianfeng Lin, Paul Seidel, Guangbo Xu and Yongquan Zhang for their suggestions on various technical points in this paper. This work is partially supported by NSF through his thesis advisor’s award DMS-2105512 and by a MathWorks fellowship.

Part II
Thimbles

This part is devoted to the analytic theory of the
\alpha
-instanton equation and a detailed construction of Floer cohomology of thimbles without using Lagrangian boundary conditions, which is denoted by
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
. In Section
2
, we introduce the notion of tame Landau-Ginzburg models following
[
FJY18
]
, explain the setup and derive the energy estimate. In Section
3
, we establish the compact theorem of
\alpha
-instantons, both the local version and the global version. Section
4
is devoted to linear analysis and the canonical
\mathbb{Z}
-grading. In Section
5
, a variant of the
\alpha
-instanton equation is introduced on a general Riemann surface. This allows us to construct the continuation map and verify the invariance of this Floer cohomology. The equivalence between
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
and the ordinary version
\HFF^{*}
is established at the very end of this paper (Section
13.9
) using a vertical gluing theorem.

2.
Floer Cohomology for Thimbles

2.1.
Tame Landau-Ginzburg models

A Landau-Ginzburg Model
(M,W)
is a non-compact complete Kähler manifold
(M,J_{M},g_{M},\omega_{M})
equipped with a holomorphic function
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
called superpotential. Let
J=J_{M}
denote the complex structure of
M
,
g_{M}
the underlying Riemannian metric and
\omega_{M}
the symplectic 2-form. Write
L=\re W
and
H=\im W
. The Cauchy-Riemann equation
(dW)^{0,1}=0
then says that

(2.1)

\nabla L+J\nabla H=0.

In most cases, the real direction is not special; one may “rotate”
W
by multiplying some
e^{-i\theta}\in S^{1}
and consider the gradient vector

(2.2)

\nabla\re(e^{-i\theta}W)=\cos\theta\nabla L+\sin\theta\nabla H=e^{J\theta}\nabla L.

A Landau-Ginzburg model
(M,W)
is called
Morse
if all critical points of
L
are non-generate. By (
2.2
), the critical set
\Crit(W)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Crit(\re(e^{-i\theta}W))
is independent of the choice of
e^{-i\theta}\in S^{1}
. To develop a Floer theory in this context, it is important to control the geometry of
(M,W)
at infinity. A reasonable set of conditions has been proposed in
[
FJY18
]
. Following their work, we introduce the notion of tame Landau-Ginzburg models.

Definition 2.1
.

A Landau-Ginzburg model
(M,W)
is called tame, if the following conditions hold:

(A1)

(M,\omega_{M})
is an exact symplectic manifold, i.e.,
\omega_{M}=d\lambda_{M}
for a smooth 1-form
\lambda_{M}\in\Omega^{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
called primitive.

(A2)

2c_{1}(TM,J_{M})=0
;

(A3)

(M,W)
is Morse;

(A4)

W|_{\Crit(W)}
is injective, i.e., each singular fiber of
W
contains a unique critical point;

(A5)

(M,J_{M},g_{M},\omega_{M})
has bounded geometry, and there exists a proper function
\psi_{M}:M\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\psi_{M}\geq 1
,
|\nabla\psi_{M}|\leq 1
and for some
C_{1},a_{1}>0
, we have

(2.3)

C^{-1}_{1}\psi_{M}<|\nabla H|+1<C_{1}e^{a_{1}\psi_{M}}.

In particular, the function
|\nabla H|^{2}:M\to[0,\infty)
is proper, and the critical set
\Crit(W)=|\nabla H|^{-1}(0)
is finite, though the superpotential
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
is not assumed to be proper.∎

Example 2.2
.

Let
M=\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
W=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}z_{n}^{2})
with each
\lambda_{i}>0
. Then the unique critical point is the origin.
∎

Example 2.3
.

Let
M=\mathbb{C}
and
W
a polynomial of degree
d\geq 2
.
(M,W)
is Morse if
\frac{\partial W}{\partial z}
has only simple zeros.
∎

Example 2.4
.

Definition
2.1
is inspired by the notion of
regular tame exact Landau-Ginzburg systems
defined in
[
FJY18
, Section 2]
. The second condition
(A2)
is to ensure that our Floer cohomology groups are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded; see Section
4
below. The condition
(A5)
is to ensure that the Local Compactness Lemma
3.1
holds for the
\alpha
-instanton equation and is usually the most difficult one to verify in practice. Readers should feel free to replace
(A5)
by any convenient criterion to fulfill this local property.

More examples of tame Landau-Ginzburg models are supplemented by
[
FJY18
, Section 2]
. Suppose that
M=\mathbb{C}^{n}
is equipped with the standard Kähler metric,
W\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}]
is a non-degenerate quasi-homogeneous polynomial plus a lower order one, and
\psi_{M}:M\to\mathbb{R}
is the distance function to the origin, then the condition (
2.3
) can be verified using
[
FJY18
, Proposition 2.5]
.

Alternatively, one may take
M=(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
and equip each factor
\mathbb{C}^{*}\cong\mathbb{C}/2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
with the standard metric on the cylinder. Take
\psi_{M}
to be the pull-back of a distance function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
via the projection map
M\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
W
is a convenient and non-degenerate Laurent polynomial in the sense of
[
FJY18
, Section 2]
, then the condition (
2.3
) is verified by
[
FJY18
, Proposition 2.8]
. We refer interested readers to
[
FJY18
]
for the precise definitions and necessary background from mirror symmetry.
∎

Example 2.5
.

We also give a Landau-Ginzburg model which is not tame. Choose a polynomial
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
with
d
distinct roots, and let
M
be the
A_{d}
-type Milnor fiber

M=\{(y,z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}):f(y)+z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{n}^{2}=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}_{y}\times\mathbb{C}_{z}^{n}

and
W\to\mathbb{C}_{y}
the projection map onto the first factor. Then
(M,W)
is not tame with the induced Kähler metric from
\mathbb{C}_{y}\times\mathbb{C}_{z}^{d}
, since
|\nabla H|^{-1}([0,\epsilon))
is not compact for any
\epsilon>0
. It is not clear to the author what might be a good metric for this example; the scope of this paper is indeed constrained by the condition
(A5)
.
∎

In this paper, we shall
always
assume that
(M,W)
is tame. For any critical point
q\in\Crit(W)
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the stable submanifold of
\re(e^{-i\theta}W)
at
q
:

\Lambda_{q,\theta}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x\in M:\exists p:[0,\infty)_{s}\to M,\partial_{s}p+\nabla\re(e^{-i\theta}W)=0,p(0)=x,\lim_{s\to\infty}p=q\}.

If one thinks of
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
as a projection map that defines a Lefschetz fibration with possibly non-compact fibers, then the image of
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
is a ray
l_{q,\theta}
that emanates from
W(q)
at the angle
e^{i\theta}
, and
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
is the Lefschetz thimble associated to the vanishing path
l_{q,\theta}
; see Figure
5
below. As it is difficult to make sense of the notion of monodromy and vanishing cycles in the infinite dimensional setting, we shall avoid this point of view in this paper.

By
[
Sei03
, Lemma 1.13]
or
[
Eli97
, Lemma 2.5]
,
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
is a Lagrangian submanifold of
(M,\omega_{M})
. Given any
q_{0},q_{1}\in\Crit(W)
and
\theta_{0},\theta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
, the Lagrangian Floer cohomology

\HFF^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{1}},\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}})

can be defined by counting
J
-holomorphic strips with Lagrangian boundary conditions:

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P&=0,\\
P(t,1)&\in\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}},\\
P(t,0)&\in\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}},\\
\end{array}\right.

or a suitable perturbed version of this so as to make the moduli spaces regular. However, generalizing this framework to the infinite dimensional setting is a challenging task
[
Ngu12
,
Ngu18
]
. For the rest of this section, we describe an alternative route to this Floer cohomology so that this generalization becomes completely formal, while the analysis remains pretty much tractable.

2.2.
The
\alpha
-instanton equation on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
and the boundary condition at infinity

For convenience, we write
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{q_{j},\theta_{j}}
for
j=0,1
and denote the Floer cohomology defined using this new approach by
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
to distinguish it from
\HFF^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
. We shall always work with a base field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic
2
to avoid the orientation issue. As usual,
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
is interpreted as an infinite dimensional Morse cohomology. Choose a constant
R\geq\pi
and a smooth function
\alpha:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for some
0<\delta\ll 1
,

(2.5)

\alpha(s)\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta_{1}&\text{if }s\geq R-\delta,\\
\theta_{0}-\pi&\text{if }s\leq\delta.\end{array}\right.

We require further that for some
0<\epsilon_{01}<1
and
\beta\in(\theta_{1}-\frac{\pi}{2},\theta_{0}-\frac{\pi}{2})
, there holds

(2.6)

\re(e^{i(\beta-\alpha(s))})>\epsilon_{01},\forall s\in\mathbb{R}_{s}.

This forces the image of
\alpha
to lie in an interval centered at
\beta
. Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the subspace of
C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
with finite
L^{\infty}_{1}
-norm. To make moduli spaces regular, we shall use a smooth 1-form
\delta H=\delta H_{s}ds\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s};\mathcal{H})
supported on
[0,R]_{s}
as a perturbation term and require that

(2.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}||\delta H_{s}||_{L^{\infty}(M)}ds<1\text{ and }\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}||\delta H_{s}||_{L^{\infty}_{1}(M)}^{2}ds<1.

For technical reason to be explained shortly,
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
can be defined only if the following condition holds:

(2.8)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{the rays $l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}$ and $l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}$ do not contain}\\
\text{any critical values of $W$ except their end points.}\end{array}

Definition 2.6
.

For any
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
satisfying (
2.8
), a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon_{01},\delta H)
is a quintuple satisfying all conditions above.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

The condition (
2.8
) is to ensure a compactness property and is used only in the proof of Lemma
3.8
below. If the function
\alpha(s)
is monotone, then (
2.8
) can be relaxed as follows: if the rays
l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}
and
l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}
intersect at some
y\in\mathbb{C}
, then the segments
\overline{yW(q_{0})}
and
\overline{yW(q_{1})}
do not contain any critical values of
W
except
W(q_{0})
and
W(q_{1})
. See Figure
5
below.
∎

\begin{overpic}[Pic2.png]
\put(90.0,85.0){$\theta_{0}$}
\put(100.0,20.0){$\theta_{1}$}
\put(-35.0,20.0){$\theta_{0}-\pi$}
\put(55.0,20.0){$\beta$}
\put(-40.0,50.0){$\beta-\frac{\pi}{2}$}
\put(80.0,0.0){$\alpha(s)$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4
.

For each critical point
q\in\Crit(W)
, choose a normal neighborhood
\mathcal{O}(q)\subset M
such that
\mathcal{O}(q)
’s are mutually disjoint and the exponential map
\exp_{q}:T_{q}M\to M
maps a small ball centered at the origin bijectively onto
\mathcal{O}(q)
. Let
p_{\model}:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
be a reference path such that
p_{\model}(s)\equiv q_{0}
when
s\leq 0
and
\equiv q_{1}
when
s\geq R
. A smooth
p:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
is said to have finite
L^{2}_{k}
-distance with
p_{\model},k\geq 1
, if there exist some
R_{1}>0
such that

•

p(-s)\in\mathcal{O}(q_{0})
and
p(s)\in\mathcal{O}(q_{1})
for all
s\geq R_{1}
;

•

\exp_{q_{1}}^{-1}(p(s))\in L^{2}_{k}([R_{1},+\infty),T_{q_{1}}M)
and
\exp_{q_{0}}^{-1}(p(s))\in L^{2}_{k}((-\infty,-R_{1}],T_{q_{0}}M)
,

Since
L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{s})\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{s})
for
k\geq 1
, we must have
\lim_{s\to-\infty}p(s)=q_{0}
and
\lim_{s\to\infty}p(s)=q_{1}
. Now consider the path space

(2.9)

\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{p\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{s};M):p\text{ has finite $L^{2}_{k}$-distance with }p_{\model}\},k\geq 1.

The action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(p)
is defined on this space by the formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}

\displaystyle-p^{*}\lambda_{M}+\delta H_{s}(p(s))ds

(2.10)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W(p(s)))+\chi_{(-\infty,0]}\im(e^{-i\theta_{0}}W(q_{0}))-\chi_{[R,\infty]}\im(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W(q_{1}))\big)ds,

where
\chi_{A}
is the indicator function of a subset
A\subset\mathbb{R}
. The function
\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W(p(s)))
is in general not
L^{1}
-integrable on
\mathbb{R}_{s}
– it is necessary to subtract the limits at infinity to make sense of (
2.2
). Indeed, for all
s\gg R
,

|W(p(s))-W(q_{1})|<C|\exp_{q_{1}}^{-1}(p(s))|^{2},\ |W(p(-s))-W(q_{0})|<C|\exp_{q_{0}}^{-1}(p(-s))|^{2}.\

Since
p
has finite
L^{2}
-distance with
p_{\model}
, the integral (
2.2
) is finite. The gradient vector field of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
is given by the formula

\grad\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(p)=J\partial_{s}p(s)+\nabla\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)+\delta H_{s}\big)\big(p(s)\big)\in C^{\infty}\cap L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathbb{R}_{s},p^{*}TM).

Let
\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
be the set of critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
. Any
p\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is called
an
\alpha
-soliton
and solves a pseudo-gradient flow equation:

(2.11)

\partial_{s}p(s)+\nabla\re(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)-J\nabla(\delta H_{s})=0,

which we refer to as
the
\alpha
-soliton equation
in this paper, though (
2.11
) depends also on the perturbation 1-form
\delta H
. Since
W
is Morse, any
\alpha
-soliton
p(s)
converges exponentially in
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-topology to
q_{0}
and
q_{1}
as
s\to\pm\infty
respectively. If
\delta H\equiv 0
, the projection of
p
under
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
is a smooth curve connecting
W(q_{0})
with
W(q_{1})
and is subject to the equation:

\partial_{s}(W(p(s)))=-e^{i\alpha(s)}|\nabla H\circ p(s)|^{2}.

The condition (
2.6
) has the following geometric interpretation, which is important for the energy estimate later on: the path
s\mapsto W(p(s))
is monotone decreasing in the direction of
e^{i\beta}
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic1.png]
\put(-20.0,45.0){\small$W(q_{1})$}
\put(-10.0,0.0){\small$W(q_{0})$}
\put(5.0,20.0){\small$W(p(s))$}
\put(50.0,45.0){$l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}$}
\put(50.0,5.0){$l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}$}
\put(80.0,53.0){$e^{i\theta_{0}}$}
\put(75.0,0.0){$e^{i\theta_{1}}$}
\put(105.0,20.0){$e^{i\beta}$}
\put(48.0,22.0){$y=l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}\cap l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 5
.

To construct the Floer cohomology of
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
, consider a smooth map
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
solving
the
\alpha
-instanton equation
:

(2.12)

\partial_{t}P+J\big(\partial_{s}P+\nabla\re(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)\big)+\nabla(\delta H_{s})=0.

To specify the boundary condition of
P
, fix
p_{\pm}(s)\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
and consider a model map
P_{\model}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
such that

(2.13)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlll}P_{\model}(t,s)&\equiv p_{-}(s)&\text{ if }t<-1,&\\
P_{\model}(t,s)&\equiv p_{+}(s)&\text{ if }t>1,&\\
P_{\model}(\cdot,s)&\to q_{1}&\text{ as }s\to\infty&\text{ uniformly exponentially,}\\
P_{\model}(\cdot,s)&\to q_{0}&\text{ as }s\to-\infty&\text{ uniformly exponentially}.\end{array}\right.

The convergence in (
2.13
) is understood as follows. For some
\zeta>0
and any
k,l\geq 0,s\gg R
, we have

\|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{s}^{l}\exp_{q_{1}}^{-1}(P_{\model}(\ \cdot\ ,s))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{t})},\ \|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{s}^{l}\exp_{q_{0}}^{-1}(P_{\model}(\ \cdot\ ,-s))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{t})}<e^{-\zeta|s|}.

We require the solution
P
to have finite
L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s},M)
-distance with this model map
P_{\model}
for some
k\geq 2
. In particular, the path
\{P(t,\cdot)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}}
is formally a downward gradient flowline for the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
. Denote the space of such solutions by
\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+})
; translating the
t
-variable defines an
\mathbb{R}_{t}
-action on
\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+})
. The quotient space
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})=\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+})/\mathbb{R}
is the moduli space of
\alpha
-instantons. If the perturbation 1-form
\delta H
is chosen generically, the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
becomes Morse at each
p\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
. A compactness theorem then ensures that
\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is a finite discrete set of points. Moreover, for any
p_{\pm}
, the moduli space
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})
is a smooth manifold and can be compactified by broken trajectories. The Morse-Smale-Witten complex of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
is freely generated by
\alpha
-solitons:

\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{p\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})}\mathbb{K}\cdot p.

with the differential map defined by counting
\alpha
-instantons:

(2.14)

\partial p_{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{p_{-}:\ \dim\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})=0}\#\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})\cdot p_{-}.

Then
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is defined as the homology of
(\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}),\partial)
. Under some mild conditions, we will verify that
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is isomorphic to the group
\HFF^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
defined using (
2.4
) in Section
13.9
. In the next few sections, we provide the analytic foundation of this novel Floer cohomology including the basic compactness property of the
\alpha
-instanton equation, canonical gradings and the invariance of
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
as we vary the Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}
.

2.3.
The energy estimate

The first step towards the compactification of
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})
is the energy estimate for the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
). For any solution
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
, consider
the energy density function

u=|\partial_{t}P|^{2}+|\partial_{s}P|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ P|^{2}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}.

and for any open subset
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
, define the energy of
P
over
\Omega
as

\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}u(t,s)dt\wedge ds.

Lemma 2.8

(The Energy Estimate I)
.

Assuming (
2.6
), one can find a constant
\epsilon_{01}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
\alpha
-instanton
P
subject to the boundary condition (
2.13
) and
t_{0}<t_{1}
, we have

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(t_{0},\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(t_{1},\cdot))+2\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}\|\delta H_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}ds

\displaystyle+(t_{1}-t_{0})

\displaystyle\bigg(\epsilon_{01}\re\big(e^{-i\beta}\big(W(q_{0})-W(q_{1})\big)\big)+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}\|\delta H_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}(M)}^{2}ds\bigg)\geq\epsilon_{01}^{\prime}\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;[t_{0},t_{1}]_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}).

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon,0)
denote the Floer datum which is equal to
\mathfrak{a}
except that the perturbation 1-form is trivial. Then the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) is cast into the form

\partial_{t}P+\grad\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}=-\nabla(\delta H_{s}).

Take the square of both sides, then integrating over
[t_{0},t_{1}]_{t}
gives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(P(t_{0},\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(P(t_{1},\cdot))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}|\partial_{t}P|^{2}+|\partial_{s}P+\nabla\re(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)|^{2}-|\nabla(\delta H_{s})|^{2}.

The last term on the right can be controlled in terms of
\|\delta H_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}(M)}
. Now we rewrite the second term as follows

(2.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}|\partial_{s}P|^{2}+|\nabla H|^{2}+2\big\langle\partial_{s}P,\nabla\re\big((e^{-i\alpha(s)}-\epsilon_{01}e^{-i\beta})W\big)\big\rangle

\displaystyle+\int_{[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}\langle\partial_{s}P,\epsilon_{01}\nabla\re(e^{-i\beta}W)\rangle.

The second line of (
2.16
) can be integrated to obtain
\epsilon_{01}(t_{1}-t_{0})\cdot\re(e^{-i\beta}(W(q_{1})-W(q_{0})))
. Since for all
s\in\mathbb{R}_{s}
,
\re(e^{i(\beta-\alpha(s))})>\epsilon_{01}
, we must have

|e^{-i\alpha(s)}-\epsilon_{01}e^{-i\beta}|<\sqrt{1-\epsilon^{2}_{01}}<1-\epsilon^{2}_{01}/2.

Hence one may complete squares and lower bound the first line of (
2.16
) by

\frac{1}{2}\int_{[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}\frac{\epsilon^{2}_{01}}{2}|\partial_{s}P|^{2}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}_{01}}{2}|\nabla H\circ P|^{2}.

Finally, for any path
p\in\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
, the values
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(p)
and
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(p)
can differ at most by
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}\|\delta H_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}ds
. This completes the proof of Lemma
2.8
. In fact, one may take
\epsilon_{01}^{\prime}=\epsilon^{2}_{01}/2
. In the estimate (
2.15
), the two terms that involve the perturbation 1-forms
\delta H
are controlled by (
2.7
).
∎

3.
Compactness

3.1.
The local compactness lemma

Having established the energy estimate in Lemma
2.8
, the next step towards the compactification of
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})
is to obtain the basic
C^{0}
-estimate for
\alpha
-instantons.

Lemma 3.1

(Local Compactness I: the Interior Case)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
be any bounded open subset and
C>0
. Suppose that
\xi:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
is any smooth function such that
|\xi(z)|<C
, and
H^{t}_{z},H^{s}_{z}:M\to\mathbb{R},\ z\in\Omega
are families of Hamiltonian functions on
M
such that

\|\nabla H^{t}_{z}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)},\|\nabla H^{s}_{z}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}<C

for all
z\in\Omega
. Then any sequence of solutions
P_{n}:\Omega\to M
to the Floer equation

(3.1)

\big(\partial_{t}P_{n}-J\nabla H_{z}^{t}\big)+J\big(\partial_{s}P_{n}-\nabla\re(\overline{\xi(z)}W)-J\nabla H_{z}^{s}\big)=0

with the uniform energy bound

(3.2)

\mathcal{E}_{an}(P_{n};\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}|dP_{n}|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ P_{n}|^{2}<C,

has a subsequence that converges in
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-topology on
\Omega
.

Remark 3.2
.

The condition
(A5)
is used in this paper only via Lemma
3.1
. Readers should feel free to replace
(A5)
by any convenient criterion to verify this local result. The analogue of Lemma
3.1
in the context of the Seiberg-Witten equations is
[
KM07
, Theorem 5.1.1]
.
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.1
relies on a diameter estimate for “almost”
J
-holomorphic curves in almost Hermitian manifolds with bounded geometry.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose that
(M,J,g_{M},\omega_{M})
is an almost Hermitian manifold with bounded geometry
(
\omega_{M}
is not necessarily closed
)
. Let
\bar{\partial}_{J}
denote the operator
\partial_{t}+J\partial_{s}
. Then for any
r>2
, there exists a function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
C>0
and for any map
P:B(0,1)\to M
, if

\int_{B(0,1)}|dP|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}\leq C,

then
\diam(P(B(0,\frac{1}{2})))\leq\varphi(C)
.

If
P:B(0,1)\to M
is genuinely
J
-holomorphic, then Proposition
3.3
is a consequence of
[
IS00
, Lemma 1.1 & Corollay 1.5]
. The estimates therein are robust enough to accommodate this general case. For the sake of completeness, we provide the proof of Proposition
3.3
in Appendix
A
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

It suffices to verify an a priori
C^{0}
-estimate for solutions on
\Omega
: for any relatively compact subset
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
, there exists
K=K(\Omega^{\prime})\subset M
compact such that if
P:\Omega\to M
satisfies (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), then
P(\Omega^{\prime})\subset K
. The
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-convergence then follows from elliptic bootstrapping. Without loss of generality, assume that
\Omega=B(0,1)
is the unit disk and
\Omega^{\prime}=B(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Throughout this proof, we use
C_{k},k\geq 1
to denote constants independent of
P
.

Consider the function
f=\psi_{M}\circ P:B(0,1)\to M
, where
\psi_{M}:M\to\mathbb{R}
is given by the condition
(A5)
. Then
|df|=|\langle\nabla\psi_{M},dP\rangle|\leq|dP|
and
|f|<C_{1}(|\nabla H\circ P|+1)
. By the energy bound (
3.2
),
\|f\|_{L^{2}_{1}(B(0,1))}<C_{2}
for some constant
C_{2}>0
.

Fix some
r>2
. By Trudinger’s inequality
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.2 & (4.14)]
, we have
\|e^{a_{1}f}\|_{L^{r}(B(0,1))}<C_{3}
for some
C_{3}>0
. Combined with (
2.3
), this implies that

\|\nabla H\circ P\|_{L^{r}(B(0,1))}<C_{3}C_{1}.

Since the gradients of
H^{t}_{z},H^{s}_{z},z\in\Omega
are bounded uniformly in
L^{\infty}(M)
, we conclude using the equation (
3.1
) that

\int_{B(0,1)}|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}<C_{4}

for some
C_{4}>0
. Note that
\|dP\|_{L^{2}(B(0,1))}^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega)<C
. Now we use Proposition
3.3
to conclude that the diameter of
P(B(0,\frac{1}{2}))
is
<\varphi(C_{4}+C)
. Finally, since the function
\psi_{M}:M\to\mathbb{R}
is proper, the estimate
\|\psi_{M}\circ P\|_{L^{2}(B(0,\frac{1}{2}))}<C_{2}
shows that
P(B(0,\frac{1}{2}))
is confined in a compact subset of
M
.
∎

3.2.
Small Energy Estimates

The
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) recovers the
\theta
-instanton equation

(3.3)

\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P+\nabla\im(e^{-i\theta}W)=0

with
\theta=\theta_{1}
when
s\geq R
and
=\theta_{0}-\pi
when
s\leq 0
. The Local Compactness Lemma
3.1
can be refined in this special case to control the
L^{2}_{k}
-norm of
P
in terms of the energy of
P
provided that this energy sufficiently small.

Lemma 3.4

(Small Energy Estimates)
.

For any bounded open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
, any relatively compact subset
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
and
k\geq 1
, there exist
C_{k},\epsilon_{1}>0
satisfying the following property. If
P:\Omega\to M
solves the standard equation (
3.3
) for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega)<\epsilon_{1}
, then the image
P(\Omega^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{O}(q)
for some
q\in\Crit(W)
, and for all
k\geq 1
,

(3.4)

\|\exp_{q}^{-1}\circ P\|_{L^{2}_{k}(\Omega^{\prime},T_{q}M)}^{2}<C_{k}\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega).

Proof.

With loss of generality, assume
\theta=0,\ \Omega=B(0,1)
and
\Omega^{\prime}=B(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Since
H:M\to\mathbb{C}
is Morse, for any
q\in\Crit(W)
, there is a smaller neighborhood
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(q)\subset\mathcal{O}(q)
and
C_{q}>0
such that for any
x\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(q)
,

(3.5)

|\exp_{q}^{-1}(x)|<C_{q}|\nabla H(x)|.

If
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega)=0
, then
P\equiv q
for some
q\in\Crit(W)
. Hence by Lemma
3.1
there exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\Omega)<\epsilon_{1}
implies that
P(B(0,\frac{3}{4}))\subset\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(q)
for some
q\in\Crit(W)
. When
k=1
, the estimate (
3.4
) follows from (
3.5
). The rest of the proof is by elliptic bootstrapping. When
k\geq 2
, choose a sequence of radii
r_{k}=\frac{1}{2}<r_{k-1}<\cdots<r_{1}=\frac{3}{4}
. We may assume that this critical point
q
has been fixed, and we will not distinguish
\mathcal{O}(q)
with its preimage in
T_{q}M
. Then Garding’s inequality implies that for any
1\leq l\leq k-1
,

\displaystyle\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l+1}(B(0,r_{l+1}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}+C\|(\partial_{t}+J\partial_{s})P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}

\displaystyle=C\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l})}+C\|\nabla H\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\leq C\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}+C\sum_{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{n}=l-1}\|\nabla^{n}(\nabla H)(\nabla^{l_{1}}P,\cdots,\nabla^{l_{l}}P)\|_{L^{2}(B(0,r_{l}))}.

In the last summation, there are two distinct cases: if
n=1
, then there is a unique entry involving
\nabla^{l-1}P
; if
n\geq 2
, then
l_{j}<l-1
, and one may use the Sobolev inequality
\|\nabla^{l_{j}}P\|_{L^{2n}}\leq C^{\prime}\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}}
. Since
\nabla H
is smooth,
\nabla H
along with its higher derivatives are all bounded on
\mathcal{O}(q)
. The norm
\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l})}
can be made small using the induction hypothesis, so any higher order terms involving this norm can be absorbed into the linear one. We deduce from (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l+1}(B(0,r_{l+1}))}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}+\text{ high order terms of }\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\|P\|_{L^{2}_{l}(B(0,r_{l}))}.

The constant
C^{\prime\prime}
may depend on
1\leq l\leq k-1
. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

3.3.
The global version

We now state and prove the global version of Lemma
3.1
, from which one can easily compactify the moduli space
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})
by broken trajectories following the standard argument as in
[
KM07
, Section 16]
. The full proof of this compactification is omitted in this paper.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
be any finite time interval and
P_{n}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
any sequence of solutions to the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) subject to the boundary condition (
2.13
). Then one can find a subsequence of
\{P_{n}|_{I\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}\}
that converges in
L^{2}_{k}(I\times\mathbb{R}_{s},M)
-topology for any
k\geq 1
.

Although the
L^{2}_{k}(I\times\mathbb{R}_{s};M)
-norm of a map
P_{n}|_{I\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}
is not defined, it makes sense to measure the
L^{2}_{k}
-norm for their difference under the boundary condition (
2.13
). More concretely, the
L^{2}_{k}
-convergence in Proposition
3.5
means that for some
R_{1}>R
and any
P_{n}
in this subsequence,
P_{n}
converges smoothly on
I\times[-R_{1},R_{1}]_{s}
. When
s\geq R_{1}
,
P_{n}(t,s)\in\mathcal{O}(q_{1})
and
\exp^{-1}_{q_{1}}\circ P_{n}
converges in
L^{2}_{k}(I\times[R_{1},+\infty)_{s};T_{q_{1}}M)
for all
k\geq 1
. A similar convergence holds when
s\leq-R_{1}
and with
q_{1}
replaced by
q_{0}
.

In general, the total energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u
is not finite. For a counterexample, consider
P(t,s)=p_{-}(s)
but
p_{-}
is not constant. To deduce Proposition
3.5
, it is important to understand the distribution of the energy density function
u
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
. The most ideal result is the following:

Proposition 3.6

(Exponential Decay in the Spatial Direction)
.

There exists constant
C,\zeta>0
such that for any solution
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
to the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) subject to the boundary condition (
2.13
), we have the estimate

u(t,s)\leq Ce^{-\zeta|s|}.

We first prove Proposition
3.5
assuming Proposition
3.6
.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
(Sketch).

Take a finite time interval
I^{\prime}
such that
I\subset\inte(I^{\prime})
. By the small energy estimate from Lemma
3.4
and Proposition
3.6
, there exists some
R_{1}>0
such that
P_{n}(t,s)\in\mathcal{O}(q_{0})
for all
s<-R_{1}
and
\in\mathcal{O}(q_{1})
for all
s>R_{1}
. Fix some
k\geq 2
and
\epsilon>0
. Then there exists another
R_{2}=R_{2}(k,\epsilon)>R_{1}
such that
\|\exp_{q_{0}}^{-1}\circ P_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}
and
\|\exp_{q_{1}}^{-1}\circ P_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}
are bounded uniformly by
\epsilon
on the tail
I^{\prime}\times\{|s|>R_{2}\}
. Over the rectangle
I^{\prime}\times[-R_{2},R_{2}]_{s}
, the
L^{2}_{k}
-convergence of a subsequence is provided by the Local Compactness Lemma
3.1
. In this way, we find a converging subsequence in
L^{2}_{k}
-topology for a fixed
k
. Now we use the diagonal argument to find a converging subsequence in
L^{2}_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
3.6
requires some preliminary results from
[
Wan20a
]
. As the complete proof has been carried in detail in the context of the Seiberg-Witten equations in
[
Wan20b
, Section 6]
, we shall only present the main ideas here. The first step towards Proposition
3.6
is the uniform convergence:

Lemma 3.7

(Uniform Decay in the Spatial Direction)
.

There exists a function
\eta:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\lim_{s\to\infty}\eta(s)\to 0
and such that for any solution
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
to the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) subject to the boundary condition (
2.13
), we have
u(t,s)\leq\eta(|s|)
.

To prove Lemma
3.7
, we have to analyze the time intervals on which the drop of the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
is small. The next lemma relies essentially on the condition (
2.8
).

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
\delta>0
, there exists constants
\epsilon(\delta),R(\delta)>0
with the following property. For any interval
I=[t_{0},t_{1}]_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
with
|t_{1}-t_{0}|=2
and any solution
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
to the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) subject to the boundary condition (
2.13
), if
t\in I,\ |s|>R(\delta)
and
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(t_{0},\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(t_{1},\cdot))<\epsilon(\delta)
, then
u(t,s)<\delta
.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
(Sketch).

If not, we can find a sequence of solutions
P_{n}
and intervals
I_{n}=[t_{0,n},t_{1,n}]_{t}
such that

\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P_{n}(t_{0,n},\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P_{n}(t_{1,n},\cdot))\to 0.

and for some
t_{n}\in I_{n}
and
|s_{n}|\to\infty
,
u(t_{n},s_{n})\geq\delta
for all
n
. By translating the time variable
t
, we may assume that
I_{n}=[-1,1]_{t}
is independent of this sequence. The Local Compactness Lemma
3.1
allows us to find a subsequence that converges to a “broken solution” of (
2.11
):

q_{0}=q_{0}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p_{0}}q_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p_{1}}q_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{p_{l}}q_{l+1}^{\prime}=q_{1}.

with
q_{k}^{\prime}\in\Crit(W)
. This means that for some special
0\leq j\leq l
,
p_{j}:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
is a solution of (
2.11
) with
\lim_{s\to\infty}p_{j}(s)=q_{j+1}^{\prime}
and
\lim_{s\to-\infty}p_{j}(s)=q_{j}^{\prime}
. For any
k\neq j
, we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{s}p_{k}(s)+\nabla\re(e^{-i\theta^{\prime}}W)&=0,\\
\lim_{s\to\infty}p_{k}(s)&=q_{k+1}^{\prime},\\
\lim_{s\to-\infty}p_{k}(s)&=q_{k}^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right.

with
\theta^{\prime}\equiv\theta_{0}-\pi
if
k<j
and
\equiv\theta_{1}
if
k>j
. This convergence is in the following sense: each
p_{k}
defines a map
[-1,1]_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
which is constant in time. For
k=j
,
P_{n}\to p_{j}
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-topology as
n\to\infty
. In general, there exists a sequence of numbers

s^{(0)}_{n}<\cdots<s^{(j-1)}_{n}<s^{(j)}_{n}=0<s^{(j+1)}_{n}<s^{(j+2)}_{n}<\cdots<s^{(l)}_{n}

such that
P_{n}(t,s-s^{(k)}_{n})\to p_{k}(t,s)
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-topology and
\lim_{n\to\infty}|s^{(k)}_{n}-s^{(k-1)}_{n}|=\infty
for all
k
.

The projection of such a broken solution under
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
is a smooth curve connecting
W(q_{0})
with
W(q_{1})
that passes through each
W(q_{k}^{\prime})
; see Figure
6
below. The condition (
2.8
) then implies that each
p_{k}
must be constant unless
k=j
. This contradicts the assumption that
u_{n}(t_{n},s_{n})>\delta
with
|s_{n}|\to\infty
. Notice that if the function
\alpha(s)
is monotone, then one may work with the weaker condition in Remark
2.7
for the last step.
∎

\begin{overpic}[Pic3.png]
\put(-15.0,60.0){\small$W(q_{1})$}
\put(-5.0,0.0){\small$W(q_{0})$}
\put(35.0,10.0){\small$W(q_{1}^{\prime})$}
\put(30.0,45.0){\small$W(q_{2}^{\prime})$}
\put(15.0,52.0){\small$W(q_{3}^{\prime})$}
\put(20.0,25.0){\small$W(p_{1})$}
\put(70.0,55.0){$l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}$}
\put(70.0,7.0){$l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}$}
\put(105.0,68.0){$e^{i\theta_{0}}$}
\put(100.0,0.0){$e^{i\theta_{1}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 6
.
The limit is a broken solution with
j=1
and
l=3
.

To deduce Lemma
3.7
from Lemma
3.8
, we have to rule out a bubbling phenomenon: for a sequence of solutions
P_{n}
, some amount of energy may slide off in the spatial direction as
s\to\infty
and converge to a
\theta
-instanton
P_{\star}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
with
\theta=\theta_{0}-\pi
or
\theta_{1}
and whose total energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P_{\star};\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s})
is finite. Such a solution is called point-like in the literature
[
GMW15
, Section 14.1]
and can be ruled out by
[
Wan20a
, Lemma 2.7]
. In practice, a stronger statement is needed for the proof:

Lemma 3.9

(
[
Wan20a
, Lemma 2.5 & 2.7]
)
.

There exists some constant
\epsilon>0
with the following property. Let
P_{\star}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
be any
\theta
-instanton, i.e., a solution to the equation (
3.3
) with a constant angle
\theta
. If the energy density function
u_{\star}
of
P_{\star}
satisfies the pointwise estimate
u_{\star}(z)<\epsilon
for any
|z|\gg 1
, then
P_{\star}
is constant, i.e.,
P_{\star}\equiv q
for some
q\in\Crit(W)
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
(Sketch).

In this case, one can deduce the exponential decay of
u_{\star}(z)
as
|z|\to\infty
from
[
Wan20a
, Lemma 2.5]
, so in fact the total energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(P_{\star};\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s})
is finite. Then one applies
[
Wan20a
, Lemma 2.7]
to show that
P_{\star}
is constant. The analogue of this lemma in the context of gauged Landau-Ginzburg models is proved in
[
Wan20a
, Theorem 5.1 & 6.1]
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.7
(Sketch).

For any
\delta>0
, we have to find some
R_{1}>0
such that
u(t,s)<\delta
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
and
|s|>R_{1}
. To this end, we cover
\mathbb{R}_{t}
by
\bigcup I_{n}
with
I_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[n-1,n+1]_{t}
. We say an interval
I_{n}
is good if
