\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(n-1,\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}(P(n+1,\cdot))<\epsilon(\delta)

where
\epsilon(\delta)
is the constant in Lemma
3.8
, and bad otherwise. Then the desired estimate holds for all
t
in some good interval and
|s|>R(\delta)
. The number of bad intervals is uniformly bounded (independent of
P
). If the desired
R_{1}
does not exist for some of them, then the bubbling phenomenon would occur, but this is ruled out using Lemma
3.9
. Hence, the desired constant
R_{1}
exists also for bad intervals. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.7
. The analogue of this lemma in the context of the Seiberg-Witten equations is
[
Wan20b
, Theorem 6.3]
.
∎

The passage from the uniform decay in Lemma
3.7
to the exponential decay in Proposition
3.6
is the content of
[
Wan20a
, Theorem 6.1]
. We highlight a few key arguments below and refer the readers to
[
Wan20a
]
for the complete proof.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
(Sketch).

The plan is to apply the maximum principle on the upper half plane
[
Wan20a
, Corollary A.2]
and use a Bochner-type formula
[
Wan20a
, Lemma 6.4]
for the energy density function
u
. To verify the exponential decay of
u
, it suffices to work over the region
|s|\geq R
on which the function
\alpha(s)
is constant. In this case, we have

\displaystyle 0=\frac{1}{2}\Delta|dP|^{2}+|\Hess P|^{2}

\displaystyle+|\Hess H(\partial_{t}P)|^{2}+|\Hess H(\partial_{s}P)|^{2}

(3.7)

\displaystyle+\text{ cubic and quartic terms in $\partial_{t}P$ and $\partial_{s}P$}.

When
0\leq u(z)\ll 1
, the map
P(z)
is close to some critical point
q\in\Crit(W)
; then the Morse condition of
W
implies that

|\Hess H(V)|>\zeta|V|,\ V=\partial_{t}P\text{ or }\partial_{s}P

for some
\zeta>0
, and we may absorb the higher order terms of
dP
in (
3.3
) by the quadratic term when
|dP|\ll 1
. Now we use Lemma
3.7
to deduce that for some
R_{1}>R
,

0\geq(\Delta+\zeta^{2})|dP|^{2},

on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{|s|\geq R_{1}\}
. Finally, we apply the maximum principle to conclude. For this argument to work, it is essential to have
\alpha(s)
constant when
|s|\gg 1
. The analogue of this result in the context of the Seiberg-Witten equations is
[
Wan20b
, Theorem 6.4]
.
∎

4.
Linear Analysis and Gradings

The required Fredholm theory to set up the moduli space
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{-},p_{+})
has been developed in the more sophisticated context of the Seiberg-Witten equations in
[
Wan20b
, Part 4]
. In this section, we recollect a few basic facts from
[
Wan20b
]
with emphasis on the finite dimensional problem addressed in this paper. While the Lagrangian submanifold
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
depends only on the angle
e^{i\theta}\in S^{1}
, a real-valued lift
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
of this angle gives an extra grading on
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
, making our Floer cohomology group
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
canonically
\mathbb{Z}
-graded. As the construction is parallel to the case of Lagrangian boundary conditions, we shall only point out the necessary adaptation and refer readers to
[
Sei08
, Section 11]
for the complete treatment.

4.1.
Graded Lagrangian submanifolds

Given a Hermitian vector space
(V,J_{V})
of
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V=\mathfrak{n}
, let
\Gr(V)=U(\mathfrak{n})/O(\mathfrak{n})
denote the Lagrangian Grassmannian of unoriented linear Lagrangian subspaces. A quadratic volume form on
(V,J_{V})
is an isomorphism of complex lines

\eta_{V}^{2}:(\bigwedge^{top}_{\mathbb{C}}V)^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{C},

which defines a squared phase map on
\Gr(V)
by the formula:

(4.1)

\xi_{V}:\Gr(V)\to S^{1},\ \xi_{V}(\Pi)=\frac{\eta_{V}(v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\mathfrak{n}})^{2}}{|\eta_{V}(v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{\mathfrak{n}})|^{2}}.

where
v_{1},\cdots,v_{\mathfrak{n}}
is any basis of
\Pi\in\Gr(V)
. Denote by
\Gr^{\#}(V)
the pull-back bundle of
\mathbb{R}\to S^{1}
via
\xi_{V}
. Since
(\xi_{V})_{*}:\pi_{1}(\Gr(V))\to\pi_{1}(S^{1})
is an isomorphism,
\Gr^{\#}(V)
is the universal cover of
\Gr(V)
. A
graded
Lagrangian subspace of
V
is an element
\Pi^{\#}=(\Pi,\xi^{\#})
in
\Gr^{\#}(V)
, where
\Pi\in\Gr(V)
is a linear Lagrangian subspace and
\xi^{\#}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\exp(2\pi i\xi^{\#})=\xi_{V}(\Pi)
. Since
\Gr^{\#}(V)
is simply connected, any pair of graded Lagrangian submanifolds
(\Pi^{\#}_{0},\Pi^{\#}_{1})
is connected by a unique path (up to homotopy) in
\Gr^{\#}(V)
. If the underlying Lagrangian subspaces intersect transversely
\Pi_{0}\pitchfork\Pi_{1}
, then one can associate an integer, called the Maslov index of
(\Pi_{0}^{\#},\Pi_{1}^{\#})

(4.2)

i(\Pi^{\#}_{0},\Pi^{\#}_{1})\in\mathbb{Z},

which is the intersection number of this path with a suitable hypersurface in
\Gr^{\#}(V)
; see
[
Sei08
, Section (11g)]
.

By Definition
2.1

(A2)
,
(M,J_{M})
is a Kähler manifold with
2c_{1}(TM,J_{M})=0
; so one may pick a quadratic complex volume form
\eta_{M}^{2}
, i.e., a smooth non-vanishing section of
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{2}=(\bigwedge^{top}_{\mathbb{C}}TM)^{\otimes-2}
. Denote by
\Gr(TM)\to M
the bundle of Lagrangian Grassmannnians and by
\xi_{M}:\Gr(TM)\to S^{1}
the squared phase map associated to
\eta_{M}
, which is defined pointwise by (
4.1
). A graded Lagrangian submanifold
(X,\xi_{X}^{\#})
is a Lagrangian submanifold
X
with a function
\xi_{X}^{\#}:X\to\mathbb{R}
(the grading of
X
) such that
\exp(2\pi i\xi_{X}^{\#}(x))=\xi_{M}(T_{x}L)
for all
x\in L
. The obstruction of such a grading
\xi_{X}^{\#}
is the Maslov class
\mu_{X}\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
classified by the map
X\to S^{1},\ x\mapsto\xi_{M}(T_{x}L)
.

Since
W:M\to\mathbb{C}
is Morse, the second derivative of
d_{2}W\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\nabla dW:T_{q}M\otimes T_{q}M\to\mathbb{C}
is a non-degenerate symmetric
J
-bilinear form at every
q\in\Crit(W)
and hence defines a quadratic complex volume form
\eta_{q}^{2}
on
T_{q}M
by the formula:

(v_{1},\cdots,v_{\mathfrak{n}})\mapsto\det(d^{2}W(v_{j},v_{k}))_{1\leq j,k\leq\mathfrak{n}},v_{j}\in T_{q}M,\ \mathfrak{n}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}TM.

To find the local form of
\eta_{q}^{2}
, take
(M,W)
as in Example
2.2
; then
\eta_{q}^{2}=\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{\mathfrak{n}}(dz_{1}\wedge\cdots dz_{\mathfrak{n}})^{\otimes 2}
. We require that each
\eta_{M}^{2}|_{T_{q}M}=\eta_{q}^{2}
for all
q\in\Crit(W)
. With this convention being said, a grading of
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
is determined by the integral lift
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. A direct computation in this local model shows that
\xi_{M}(T_{q}\Lambda_{q,\theta})=\exp(i\mathfrak{n}\theta).
Then we declare that

(4.3)

\xi^{\#}_{\Lambda_{q,\theta}}(T_{q}\Lambda_{q,\theta})=\frac{\mathfrak{n}\theta}{2\pi}.

Since
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
is contractible, this specifies a grading on
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
.

4.2.
Hessians

For any smooth
p\in\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1}),k\geq 1
, the Hessian of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
at
p
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}:L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s};p^{*}TM)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s};p^{*}TM)

(4.4)

\displaystyle v(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto J\frac{D}{ds}v(s)+\Hess_{p(s)}\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)+\delta H_{s}\big)\big(v(s)\big),

which is clearly
L^{2}
-self-adjoint. Since
p(s)
decays exponentially as
s\to\pm\infty
to
q_{0}
and
q_{1}
respectively, the image of
p(s)
in
M
is a finite path connecting
q_{0}
with
q_{1}
. One may trivialize the bundle
p^{*}TM
for
|s|\gg R
using the Levi-Civita connection and think of
v(s)
as a section in
T_{q_{0}}M
if
s<0
and in
T_{q_{1}}M
if
s>R
. Hence
\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
takes the form

J\partial_{s}-\Hess_{q_{0}}\im(e^{-i\theta_{0}}W)+\text{ a compact operator }

on the interval
(-\infty,0]_{s}
and respectively

J\partial_{s}+\Hess_{q_{1}}\im(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W)+\text{ a compact operator }

on
[R,+\infty)_{s}
. The model problem to understand the Fredholm property of
\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
is the following. Given a Hermitian vector space
(V,J_{V})
, consider an invertible self-adjoint operator
D_{V}:V\to V
that anti-commutes with
J_{V}
. Since
J_{V}D_{V}+D_{V}J_{V}=0
, the spectrum of
D_{V}
is symmetric about the origin. A direct computation shows that the operator

(4.5)

L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s};V)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s};V),\ v(s)\mapsto\partial_{s}v(s)+D_{V}\big(v(s)\big),

is invertible. The spectrum of (
4.5
) is purely essential and is given by
(-\infty,-\lambda_{V}]\cup[\lambda_{V},+\infty)
where
\lambda_{V}
is the first positive eigenvalue of
D_{V}
. Returning to the discussion of Floer cohomology, we shall take
(V,J_{V})=(T_{q_{j}}M,J)
and
D_{V}=(-1)^{j-1}\Hess_{q_{j}}\im(e^{-\theta_{j}}W)
for
j=0,1
. Then a parametrix-patching argument shows that

\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
is Fredholm, so its essential spectrum is disjoint from the origin; in fact, this essential spectrum is given by

(-\infty,-\lambda_{1}]\cup[\lambda_{1},+\infty)

where
\lambda_{1}
is the first positive eigenvalue of
\Hess_{q_{0}}H
or
\Hess_{q_{1}}H
depending on which is smaller (note that
\lambda_{1}
is independent of
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
). One can still make sense of the spectral flow for a path of operators like
\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
. For details, see
[
Wan20b
, Section 11]
.

4.3.
A short review

Before we turn to the canonical grading on
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
, we review briefly how this is done in the classical case. Let
(X,Y)
be any pair of graded Lagrangian submanifolds of
M
and
I_{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[-R,R]_{s}
. Consider the path space

\mathcal{P}(X,Y)_{R}=\{p:I_{R}\to M:\ p(-R)\in X,\ p(R)\in Y\}.

For any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{R}(X,Y)
and any family of Hamiltonian functions
\delta H=\delta H_{s}ds\in\Omega^{1}(I_{R};\mathcal{H})
, consider the
L^{2}
-self adjoint operator:

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{\delta H}:L^{2}_{1}(I_{R},\partial I_{R};p^{*}TM)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(I_{R};p^{*}TM),

\displaystyle v(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto J\frac{D}{ds}v(s)+\Hess_{p(s)}(\delta H_{s})\big(v(s)\big).

where
v(s)
is an
L^{2}_{1}
-section of
p^{*}TM
with
v(-R)\in T_{p(-R)}X
and
v(R)\in T_{p(R)}Y
. This operator is the Hessian of a suitable action functional
\mathcal{A}_{\delta H}
on the path space
\mathcal{P}(X,Y)_{R}
. A pair
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\delta H)
is called
non-degenerate
if the operator (
4.6
) is invertible. If
2c_{1}(TM,J)=0
, then there is a grading function
[
Sei08
, Section (12b)]
:

\overline{\gr}:\mathcal{P}(X,Y)_{R}\times\Omega^{1}(I_{R};\mathcal{H})\dashrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

defined on the subspace of all non-degenerate pairs and subject to the following axioms:

(A-I)

(Index Axiom) Let
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm},\delta H_{\pm})
be non-degenerate and
\mathfrak{p}(t):[-1,1]_{t}\to\mathcal{P}(X,Y)_{R}\times\Omega^{1}(I_{R};\mathcal{H})
any smooth path (if exist) connecting
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}
and constant when
0\leq|t-1|\ll 1
. Thus
\mathfrak{p}(t)
defines a smooth map
P:Z_{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{t}\times I_{R}\to M
along with a 1-form
\delta H^{\prime}=\delta H^{\prime}_{s}ds\in\Omega^{1}(Z_{R};\mathcal{H})
such that
(P,\delta H^{\prime})(t,\cdot)\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{-}
if
t\leq-1
and
\equiv\mathfrak{p}_{+}
if
t\geq 1
. Then the Fredholm operator

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{P}:L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R},\partial Z_{R};P^{*}TM)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(Z_{R};P^{*}TM)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{D}{dt}v+J\frac{D}{ds}v+\Hess_{P}(\delta H_{s}^{\prime})(v).

has index
=\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{-})-\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{+})
(which is also the spectral flow of (
4.5
) along the path
\mathfrak{p}(t)
).

(A-II)

(Normalization Axiom) Suppose that the path
p\in\mathcal{P}(X,Y)_{R}
follows the Hamiltonian flow of some
\delta H\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s};\mathcal{H})
, then the linearization of this flow transports
T_{p(-R)}X\subset T_{p(-R)}M
into a graded Lagrangian subspace of
T_{p(R)}M
, denoted by
\Pi_{X}^{\#}
. Let
\Pi_{Y}^{\#}=T_{p(1)}Y
with the induced grading from
Y
. If
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\delta H)
is non-degenerate, then
\Pi_{X}^{\#}
and
\Pi_{Y}^{\#}
intersect transversely, and

\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p})=i(\Pi_{X}^{\#},\Pi_{Y}^{\#})

is the Maslov index (
4.2
).

The grading function
\overline{\gr}
is determined uniquely by these axioms: the Index Axiom
(A-I)
determines
\overline{\gr}
up to a global
\mathbb{Z}
-action; this ambiguity is then fixed by
(A-II)
.

4.4.
Canonical gradings

We follow the same scheme to define the canonical grading on
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
and look for a connection with the grading function
\overline{\gr}
in the classical case; the normalization axiom will be adapted accordingly. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{R}
denote the space of Floer data whose first entry is equal to some fixed
R\geq\pi
. A pair
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\mathfrak{a})\in\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})\times\mathcal{Q}_{R}
is called
non-degenerate
if the operator
\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
defined by (
4.5
) is invertible. Then the grading function

\gr:\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})\times\mathcal{Q}_{R}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{Z}

defined on the subspace of non-degenerate pairs is subject to the following axioms:

(A’-I)

(Index Axiom) Let
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm},\mathfrak{a}_{\pm})
be non-degenerate and
\mathfrak{p}(t):[-1,1]_{t}\to\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})\times\mathcal{Q}_{R}
be any smooth path (if exist) connecting
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}
and constant when
0\leq|t-1|\ll 1
. Thus
\mathfrak{p}(t)
defines a smooth map
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
, a smooth function
\alpha^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
and a smooth 1-form
\delta H^{\prime}=\delta H^{\prime}_{s}ds\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})
which is supported on
Z_{R}
such that for
\pm t\geq 1
,
(P,\alpha^{\prime},\delta H^{\prime})
is constant in time and determined by
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm},\mathfrak{a}_{\pm})
. Then the Fredholm operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{P}:L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s};P^{*}TM)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s};P^{*}TM)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{D}{dt}v+J\frac{D}{ds}v+\Hess_{P}\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha^{\prime}}W)+\delta H^{\prime}_{s}\big)(v).

has index
=\gr(\mathfrak{p}_{-})-\gr(\mathfrak{p}_{+})
(which is interpreted as a spectral flow).

(A’-II)

(Normalization Axiom) Suppose that for some
R_{1}\geq R
,
p(s)\equiv q_{0}
when
s\leq-R_{1}
and
\equiv q_{1}
when
s\geq R_{1}
, then the restriction of
p
on
[-R_{1},R_{1}]_{s}
determines a path
p_{R_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})_{R_{1}}
. Moreover, any Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon_{01},\delta H)
defines a smooth 1-form

\delta H_{R_{1}}^{\mathfrak{a}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W)ds+\delta H\in\Omega^{1}(Z_{R};\mathcal{H}).

If
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\mathfrak{a})
is non-degenerate, so is
\mathfrak{p}_{R_{1}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(p_{R_{1}},\delta H_{R_{1}}^{\mathfrak{a}})
. Then
\gr(\mathfrak{p})=\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{R_{1}})
.

Remark 4.1
.

There are two remarks in order. First, for any path
p:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
as in
(A’-II)
, the operator
\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
takes the stand form

J\partial_{s}+\Hess_{q_{1}}\im(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W)=J(\partial_{s}+\Hess_{q_{1}}\re(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W)).

when
s\geq R_{1}
. If
v(s)\in\ker\Hess_{p}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
, then
v(R_{1})=T_{q_{1}}\Lambda_{1}\in T_{q_{1}}M
, which is the negative spectrum subspace of
\Hess_{q_{1}}\re(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W))
. The same argument shows that
v(-R_{1})\in T_{q_{0}}\Lambda_{0}
. Thus
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\mathfrak{a})
is non-degenerate if and only if
\mathfrak{p}_{R_{1}}
is non-degenerate.

Secondly, the grading
\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{R_{1}})
is independent of the choice of
R_{1}
. Indeed, one may always pull back the pair
\mathfrak{p}_{R_{1}}=(p_{R_{1}},\delta H^{\mathfrak{a}}_{R_{1}})
to the unit interval
I_{1}=[-1,1]_{s}
. By changing
R_{1}
one obtains a continuous family of invertible
L^{2}
-self-adjoint operators defined on
I_{1}
with the same domain; therefore they have the same grading by the Index Axiom
(A-I)
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

The grading function
\gr
that satisfies Axioms
(A’-I)
and
(A’-II)
exists and is unique.

Proof.

The uniqueness of
\gr
is straightforward. To prove the existence, suppose that
\mathfrak{p}_{\pm}=(p_{\pm},\mathfrak{a}_{\pm})
are non-degenerate and for some
R_{1}\geq R
,
p_{\pm}(s)\equiv q_{0}
when
s\leq-R_{1}
and
\equiv q_{1}
when
s\geq R_{1}
, then their gradings are determined by Axiom
(A’-II)
. We have to show that for this class of non-degenerate pairs Index Axiom
(A’-I)
holds. To this end, consider a smooth map
P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
such that

P(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}q_{0}&\text{ if }s\leq-R_{1},\\
q_{1}&\text{ if }s\geq R_{1},\\
p_{-}(s)&\text{ if }s\leq t,\\
p_{+}(s)&\text{ if }s\geq t,\end{array}\right.

and choose smooth interpolations for
\alpha_{\pm}
and
\delta H_{\pm}
respectively to define the operator
\mathcal{D}_{P}
. Moreover, we can truncate these data on the strip
Z_{R_{1}}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[-R_{1},R_{1}]_{s}
and define another operator
\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}
by (
4.7
). By the Index Axiom
(A-I)
, we have

\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}=\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{+,R_{1}})-\overline{\gr}(\mathfrak{p}_{-,R_{1}})=\gr(\mathfrak{p}_{+})-\gr(\mathfrak{p}_{-}).

It remains to verify that
\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}=\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P}
. The easiest way to see this is by the excision principle, for which we have to introduce four auxiliary operators. To ease our notations, denote
\Pi^{\pm}_{j}
by the positive (resp. negative) spectrum subspace of
\Hess_{q_{1}}\re(e^{-i\theta_{j}}W)
; then
T_{q_{j}}\Lambda_{j}=\Pi^{-}_{j}\subset T_{q_{j}}M
,
j=0,1
. Let

Z^{-}_{R_{1}}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(-\infty,R_{1}]_{s}\text{ and }Z^{+}_{R_{1}}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[-R_{1},+\infty)_{s}.

Given a symplectic vector bundle
V\to Z_{R_{1}}
and Lagrangian sub-bundles
F_{\pm}\subset V|_{\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{\pm R_{1}\}}
, we use

L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R_{1}},\partial Z_{R_{1}};V,F_{-},F_{+})

to denote the space of
L^{2}_{1}
-sections
v
of
V
such that
v(t,\pm R_{1})\in F_{\pm}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
. We adopt a similar convention for sections over
Z^{\pm}_{R_{1}}
in which case only one boundary condition is needed. With that being said, consider the operators:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}:L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R_{1}},\partial Z_{R_{1}};T_{q_{1}}M,\Pi_{1}^{+},\Pi_{1}^{-})\to L^{2}(Z_{R_{1}};T_{q_{1}}M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{+}:L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R_{1}}^{+},\partial Z_{R_{1}}^{+};T_{q_{1}}M,\Pi_{1}^{+})\to L^{2}(Z_{R_{1}^{+}};T_{q_{1}}M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{+}=\mathcal{D}_{1}=\partial_{t}+J\big(\partial_{s}+\Hess_{q_{1}}\re(e^{-i\theta_{1}}W)\big),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}:L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R_{1}},\partial Z_{R_{1}};T_{q_{0}}M,\Pi_{0}^{-},\Pi_{0}^{+})\to L^{2}(Z_{R_{1}};T_{q_{0}}M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}^{-}:L^{2}_{1}(Z_{R_{1}}^{-},\partial Z_{R_{1}}^{-};T_{q_{0}}M,\Pi_{0}^{+})\to L^{2}(Z_{R_{1}}^{-};T_{q_{0}}M),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}^{-}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\partial_{t}+J\big(\partial_{s}-\Hess_{q_{0}}\re(e^{-i\theta_{0}}W)\big).

These operators are invertible – they are all cast into the form
\partial_{t}+\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
for some invertible
L^{2}
-self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. The pattern for the boundary conditions is summarized as in Figure
7
below. Note that
\mathcal{D}_{P}=\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}=\mathcal{D}_{1}
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[R,R_{1}]_{s}
and
=\mathcal{D}_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[-R_{1},-R]_{s}
. Then the excision principle shows that for all
R_{1}\gg R
,

\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}+\Ind\mathcal{D}_{1}^{+}+\Ind\mathcal{D}_{0}^{-}=\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P}+\Ind\mathcal{D}_{1}+\Ind\mathcal{D}_{0},

and therefore
\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P_{R_{1}}}=\Ind\mathcal{D}_{P}
. A concrete proof of this excision formula can be found in
[
Wan20b
, Section A.3]
or in the proof of the vertical gluing theorem in Section
11
; see Lemma
11.5
. We leave the details to interested readers.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

This excision principle is extremely useful as it reduces any index computation on such non-compact Riemann surfaces to the classical case of Lagrangian boundary conditions. This idea will be used repeatedly in the rest of the paper.
∎

.
The Excision Principle.

Remark 4.4
.

For any
\alpha
-soliton
p\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
, we actually have
p(s)\in\Lambda_{0}
when
s\leq 0
and
\in\Lambda_{1}
when
s\geq R
. If
\mathfrak{p}=(p,\mathfrak{a})
is non-degenerate, then the linearized Hamiltonian flow along the path
p(s)
transports
T_{p(-R)}\Lambda_{0}\subset T_{p(-R)}M
into a graded Lagrangian subspace of
T_{p(R)}M
transverse to
T_{p(R)}\Lambda_{1}
. A normalization property like Axiom
(A-II)
still holds in this case. Although it is very enlightening to have this in mind, this property is not very useful for the purpose of this work and will not be proved here.
∎

4.5.
Perturbation and Transversality

A Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon,\delta H)
is called
admissible
if any
\alpha
-soliton
p\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is non-degenerate, i.e., the Hessian of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
at
p
is invertible, and for any
\alpha
-solitons
p_{\pm}
and
P\in\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+})
, the linearization of (
2.12
) is a surjective Fredholm operator from
L^{2}_{1}\to L^{2}
. The support of
\delta H\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s};\mathcal{H})
is always confined in
[0,R]_{s}
, but one can always make
\mathfrak{a}
admissible by choosing
\delta H
generically. This is due to a unique continuation property: if two solutions
P_{0},P_{1}
of (
2.12
) are equal on
\{t_{0}\}\times[0,R]_{s}
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
, then they are equal on the whole space. By taking derivatives of (
2.12
) repeatedly, one can show that under this assumption
P_{0},P_{1}
along with all their higher derivatives would agree at the point
(t_{0},0)
, then this statement follows from Aronszajn’s theorem
[
MS12
, Theorem 2.3.4]
.

5.
The Floer Equation on General Riemann Surfaces

5.1.
Phase functions, the energy estimate and the Floer equation

Having constructed the Floer cohomology
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
, our next goal is to understand its dependence on the Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}
. This requires generalizing the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) to a general Riemann surface and derive the energy estimate. Recall that any smooth function
K\in\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
induces a Hamiltonian vector field
X_{K}\in C^{\infty}(M;TM)
with
dK=\omega_{M}(\cdot,X_{K})
. The Poisson bracket

\{K_{1},K_{2}\}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\omega_{M}(X_{K_{1}},X_{K_{2}})

makes
C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
into a Lie algebra, and the map

(5.1)

(C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R}),\{\cdot,\cdot\})\to(C^{\infty}(M;TM),[\cdot,\cdot]),\ K\mapsto X_{K}

is a Lie algebra homomorphism. We identify
\mathbb{C}
as a subspace of
C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
by sending

(5.2)

x\mapsto\re(\bar{x}W);

so
1\mapsto L
and
i\mapsto H
. Given any compact Riemann surface
S
with boundary, a complex 1-form
\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(S;\mathbb{C})
becomes a
C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
-valued 1-form under the map (
5.2
). By (
5.1
),
\kappa
induces a Hamiltonian vector field
X_{\kappa}\in\Omega^{1}(S;C^{\infty}(M;TM))
. We summarize the relevant maps as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R}),\{\cdot,\cdot\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C^{\infty}(M;TM),[\cdot,\cdot]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\kappa}}
(
5.2
)
(
5.1
)

Definition 5.1
.

A phase function
is a smooth map
\Xi=(a,b):S\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}
with
\det D\Xi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}/dvol_{S}\leq 0
. Its induced 1-form
\kappa_{\Xi}
is defined by the formula

(5.3)

\kappa_{\Xi}=(d_{S}b)\cdot L+(-d_{S}a)\cdot H=-\im(\overline{d_{S}\Xi}\cdot W)\in\Omega^{1}(S;C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})).

The curvature of
d+\kappa_{\Xi}
is computed as

(5.4)

\displaystyle F_{d+\kappa_{\Xi}}

\displaystyle=d_{S}\kappa_{\Xi}+\frac{1}{2}\{\kappa_{\Xi}\wedge\kappa_{\Xi}\}=d_{S}a\wedge d_{S}b\cdot\{L,H\}=|\nabla H|^{2}\cdot\Xi^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle=|\nabla H|^{2}(\det D\Xi)\cdot dvol_{S}\in\Omega^{2}(S;C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})),

which is non-positive everywhere on
M
. The Hamiltonian vector field of
\kappa_{\Xi}
is given by the formula

X_{\kappa_{\Xi}}=(d_{S}a)\cdot\nabla L+(d_{S}b)\cdot\nabla H=\nabla\re(\overline{d_{S}\Xi}\cdot W)\in\Omega^{1}(S;C^{\infty}(M;TM)).\qed

The Floer equation defined for a smooth map
P:S\to M
reads as follows:

(5.5)

(dP-X_{\kappa_{\Xi}+\delta\kappa}-X_{\delta H})^{0,1}=0.

where
\delta\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(S;\mathbb{C})
is an correction term and
\delta H\in\Omega^{1}(S;\mathcal{H})
is a perturbation 1-form. Recall that
\mathcal{H}
is the subspace of
C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
with finite
L^{\infty}_{1}
-norm. Define
the energy density function
of
P
over
S
as

u=|\nabla P|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ P|^{2}:S\to\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Lemma 5.2

(The Energy Estimate II)
.

Suppose the phase function
\Xi:S\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the correction 1-form
\delta\kappa\in\Omega^{1}(S;\mathbb{C})
satisfy the pointwise bound

(5.6)

-\det D\Xi-|\delta\kappa^{0,1}|^{2}\geq\epsilon_{S}

for some constant
\epsilon_{S}>0
. Then there exists some
\epsilon_{S}^{\prime}>0
such that for any solution
P:S\to M
to the Floer equation (
5.5
), we have

(5.7)

\int_{S}P^{*}\omega_{M}-\int_{\partial S}(\Id,P)^{*}\kappa_{\Xi}\geq\epsilon_{S}^{\prime}\int_{S}u\cdot dvol_{S}-4\int_{S}\|\delta H\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}}^{2},

where
\|\delta H\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}}
denotes the norm of
\delta H
as a map
TS\to L^{\infty}_{1}(M;\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Take any
z\in S
and let
\{\partial_{t},\partial_{s}\}
be an oriented orthonormal basis of
T_{z}S
. Then the equation
(dP-X_{\kappa_{\Xi}+\delta\kappa})^{0,1}(\partial_{t})
is cast into the form

A+JB-C=D
with

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}P-X_{\kappa_{\Xi}}(\partial_{t}),

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}P-X_{\kappa_{\Xi}}(\partial_{s}),

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=X_{\delta\kappa}(\partial_{t})+JX_{\delta\kappa}(\partial_{s}),

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=X_{\delta H}(\partial_{t})+JX_{\delta H}(\partial_{s}).

In particular,

(5.8)

|D|^{2}=|A+JB-C|^{2}=|A-C|^{2}+|JB-C|^{2}+2\langle A,JB\rangle-|C|^{2}.

Let
(\Id,P):S\to S\times M
be the graph of
P
. If one thinks of
\kappa_{\Xi}\in C^{\infty}(S\times M;T^{*}S)
and
F_{d+\kappa_{\Xi}}\in C^{\infty}(S\times M;\Lambda^{2}T^{*}S)
as differential forms on
S\times M
, then the computation in
[
MS12
, Lemma 8.1.6]
says that

\langle A,JB\rangle\cdot dvol_{S}=-P^{*}\omega_{M}+d_{S}\big((\Id,P)^{*}\kappa_{\Xi}\big)-(\Id,P)^{*}F_{d+\kappa_{\Xi}}.

Combined with (
5.4
) and (
5.8
), we deduce that

\displaystyle P^{*}\omega_{M}-d_{S}((\Id,P)^{*}\kappa_{\Xi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(|A-C|^{2}+|JB-C|^{2}-|C|^{2}-|D|^{2})\cdot dvol_{S}-(\Id,P)^{*}F_{d+\kappa_{\Xi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}|A-C|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|JB-C|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ P|^{2}(-\det D\Phi-|\delta\kappa^{0,1}|^{2})-|D|^{2}\big)\cdot dvol_{S}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}|A-C|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|JB-C|^{2}+\epsilon_{S}|\nabla H\circ P|^{2}-|D|^{2}\big)\cdot dvol_{S}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\big(\epsilon_{S}^{\prime}u-4\|\delta H\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{1}}\big)\cdot dvol_{S}

for some
\epsilon_{S}^{\prime}>0
. Now we integrate both sides over the surface
S
.
∎

\put(25.0,30.0){\small$\gamma(s)$}
\put(-17.0,67.0){\small$-e^{i\theta_{1}}$}
\put(-10.0,-5.0){\small$-e^{i\theta_{0}}$}
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 8
.
The characteristic curve
\gamma(s)
for
\alpha(s)
.

Definition 5.3
.

A phase pair

(\Xi,\delta\kappa)
on a Riemann surface
S
is a phase function
\Xi:S\to\mathbb{C}
along with a correction term
\delta\kappa
satisfying the estimate (
5.6
).
∎

Example 5.4
.

For any Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon_{01},\delta H)
one can associate
a canonical phase pair
as follows. Take
S=[t_{0},t_{1}]\times[-R_{1},R_{1}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
to start. We say that
\gamma:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
is
a characteristic curve
for the function
\alpha(s)
if
\partial_{s}\gamma(s)=-e^{i\alpha(s)}
. This curve is not unique; one possible normalization is to require that

•

\gamma(s)\in\{-re^{i\theta_{1}}:r\geq 0\}
when
s\gg 1
and
\in\{-re^{i\theta_{0}}:r\geq 0\}
when
s\ll-1
.

Now consider the phase function
\Xi(t,s)=-i\epsilon_{01}e^{i\beta}\cdot t+\gamma(s),
then

\displaystyle-\det D\Xi

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{01}\cos(\beta-\alpha(s)),

\displaystyle\kappa_{\Xi}

\displaystyle=\im((-i\epsilon_{01}e^{-i\beta}dt+e^{-i\alpha(s)}ds)\cdot W).

Let
\delta\kappa=\im(i\epsilon_{01}e^{-i\beta}dt\cdot W)
, then we have

\displaystyle X_{\kappa_{\Xi}+\delta\kappa}(\partial_{t})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle X_{\kappa_{\Xi}+\delta\kappa}(\partial_{s})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\re(e^{-i\alpha(s)}W),

and the Floer equation (
5.5
) recovers the
\alpha
-instanton equation (
2.12
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
. In this case, since
|\delta\kappa^{0,1}|^{2}=\epsilon_{01}^{2}/2
, and our condition (
2.6
) ensures that
-\det D\Xi\geq\epsilon_{01}^{2}
, we may set
\epsilon_{S}=\epsilon_{01}^{2}/2
. The left hand side of (
5.7
) reduces to

\int_{\partial S}P^{*}\lambda_{M}+\im\big((i\epsilon_{01}e^{-i\beta}dt-e^{-i\alpha(s)}ds)\cdot(W\circ P)\big).

This recovers the left hand side of (
2.15
) as
R_{1}\to\infty
, if one ignores the terms coming from
\delta H
. The proofs of Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
5.2
are indeed identical in this special case.
∎

\begin{overpic}[Pic4.png]
\put(25.0,15.0){\large$\xrightarrow{\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi\hskip 14.22636pt}$}
\put(85.0,30.0){\small$\gamma(s)$}
\put(40.0,12.0){\small$-i\epsilon_{01}e^{i\beta}\cdot t$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 9
.
The phase function
\Xi
associated to a Floer datum.

Remark 5.5
.

In the classical development of Floer theory for Lefschetz fibrations, the connection
d+\kappa_{\Xi}
being negatively curved proves important for any compactness results
[
Sei19
, P.69]
. In our case, the image
W\circ P(S)
does not admit any a priori control, and the coefficients of
\kappa_{\Xi}
are forced to be closed (and so exact if the surface
S
is contractible); the negativity of
F_{d+\kappa_{\Xi}}
arises from the relation
\{L,H\}=|\nabla H|^{2}
and the fact that the phase function
\Xi:S\to\mathbb{C}
is orientation reversing.
∎

5.2.
Continuation maps

Given critical points
q_{0},q_{1}\in\Crit(W)
and angles
\theta_{0},\theta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
, one can find different interpolations

\alpha^{-}(s),\alpha^{+}(s):\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying the boundary condition (
2.5
) and such that (
2.6
) holds for possibly different
(\beta^{\pm},\epsilon^{\pm}_{01})
:

\re(e^{i(\beta^{\pm}-\alpha^{\pm}(s))})>\epsilon_{01}^{\pm}>0.

Let
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}=(R^{\pm},\alpha^{\pm}(s),\beta^{\pm},\epsilon^{\pm}_{01},\delta H_{s}^{\pm})
be any admissible Floer data associated to
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
where
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{q_{j},\theta_{j}},j=0,1
. Using the Floer equation (
5.5
), we shall construct a continuation map:

(5.9)

\Cont_{\mathfrak{a}_{+},\mathfrak{a}_{-}}:\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{+})\to\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{-})

such that

(5.10)

\Cont_{\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}}\circ\Cont_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}}=\Cont_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}}

for any triple
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime})
, and
\Cont_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}}=\Id
. This proves that the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
is independent of the choice of the Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}
up to canonical isomorphisms.

Replacing the first entry of a Floer datum by a larger number will only change the functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}}
by a constant; so we may assume that
R^{+}=R^{-}
to start. For simplicity we will take
R^{\pm}=\pi
in the sequel.

Fix a choice of characteristic curves
\gamma^{\pm}(s)
for
\alpha^{\pm}(s)
. We have to specify the phase pair (
\Xi,\delta\kappa
) for the Riemann surface
S=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
to define this continuation map, and we wish
(\Xi,\delta\kappa)
agrees with the canonical ones associated to
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}
when
|t|\gg 1
. For any
K>0
, consider the space
\Emb_{K}(\mathfrak{a}^{+},\mathfrak{a}^{-})
of smooth embeddings

\Xi^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{C}

that satisfies the following properties:

•

for some
c_{\Xi^{\dagger}}\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

(5.11)

\Xi^{\dagger}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-i\epsilon_{01}^{-}e^{i\beta^{-}}\cdot t+\gamma^{-}(s)&\text{ on }(-\infty,0]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s},\\
-i\epsilon_{01}^{+}e^{i\beta^{+}}\cdot t+\gamma^{+}(s)+c_{\Xi^{\dagger}}&\text{ on }[K,\infty)_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s};\end{array}\right.

•

for some
0<\delta\ll\pi
,

(5.12)

\Xi^{\dagger}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{1}(t)-e^{i\theta_{1}}\cdot(s-\pi)&\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\pi-\delta,\pi]_{s},\\
g_{0}(t)+e^{i\theta_{0}}\cdot s&\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\delta)_{s},\end{array}\right.

where
g_{1}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{\dagger}(t,\pi)
and
g_{0}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{\dagger}(t,0)
;

•

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
,

(5.13)

-\im(e^{-i\theta_{j}}\partial_{t}g_{j}(t))-\frac{1}{2}|\partial_{t}g_{j}(t))|^{2}>0,j=0,1.

We shall simple write
\Emb_{K}=\Emb_{K}(\mathfrak{a}^{+},\mathfrak{a}^{-})
when the Floer data
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}
are clear from the context. By (
5.11
),
\Xi^{\dagger}
is determined by its restriction on
[-1,K+1]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
.
We equip
\Emb_{K}
with the smooth topology as a subspace of
C^{\infty}([-1,K+1]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s},\mathbb{C})
. For any
K_{1}<K_{2}
, we have a natural inclusion map
\Emb_{K_{1}}\to\Emb_{K_{2}}
. The proof of the next lemma is deferred to the end of this section.

Lemma 5.6
.

If we equip the direct limit
\Emb\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varinjlim\Emb_{K}
with the inductive topology, then
\Emb
is weakly contractible.

By (
5.12
), any
\Xi^{\dagger}\in\Emb_{K}
extends to an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism
\Xi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
by setting (see Figure
10
below)

(5.14)

\Xi(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}g_{1}(t)-e^{i\theta_{1}}\cdot(s-\pi)&\text{ if }s\geq\pi,\\
\Xi^{\dagger}(t,s)&\text{ if }s\in[0,\pi]_{s},\\
g_{0}(t)+e^{i\theta_{0}}\cdot s&\text{ if }s\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

Finally, away from the rectangle
[0,K]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
, define the correction 1-form
\delta\kappa
by the formula:

(5.15)

\delta\kappa(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\im(i\epsilon_{01}^{-}e^{-i\beta^{-}}dt\cdot W)&\text{ on }(-\infty,0]_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s},\\
\im(i\epsilon_{01}^{+}e^{-i\beta^{+}}dt\cdot W)&\text{ on }[K,+\infty)_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s},\\
\im(\overline{\partial_{t}g_{1}(t)}dt\cdot W)&\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[\pi,+\infty)_{s},\\
\im(\overline{\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)}dt\cdot W)&\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(-\infty,0]_{s},\end{array}\right.

Definition 5.7
.

Given any Floer data
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}=(\pi,\alpha^{\pm},\beta^{\pm},\epsilon_{01}^{\pm},\delta H^{\pm})
for the thimbles
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
, a continuation datum is a quadruple
\mathfrak{c}=(K,\Xi,\delta\kappa,\delta H)
, where

•

K>0
and the phase pair
(\Xi,\delta\kappa)
is constructed as above using
\Xi^{\dagger}\in\Emb_{K}
and such that for some
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}>0
,

(5.16)

-\det D\Xi-|\delta\kappa^{0,1}|^{2}>\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}\text{ for all }(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s};

•

the perturbation 1-form
\delta H\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s};\mathcal{H})
is supported on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
, and
\delta H=\delta H^{+}
when
t\leq 0
and
=\delta H^{-}
when
t\geq K
.

Since
(\Xi,\delta\kappa)
agrees with the phase pair associated to
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}
when
t\leq 0
or
\geq K
(up to a translation by the constant
c_{\Xi^{\dagger}}\in\mathbb{C}
in (
5.11
)), the condition (
5.13
) implies that the pointwise bound (
5.16
) holds away from the rectangle
[0,K]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
for some
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}>0
. Hence, (
5.16
) is fulfilled for a possibly smaller
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}
if the extension of
\delta\kappa
on
[0,K]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
is chosen carefully enough.
∎

\put(100.0,11.0){\small$-i\epsilon_{01}^{-}e^{i\beta^{-}}\cdot t$}

\put(17.0,2.0){\small$K$}
\put(-3.0,12.0){\small$0$}
\put(-3.0,19.0){\small$\pi$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 10
.
The phase function
\Xi:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
for a continuation datum.

Away from the rectangle
[0,K]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
, the Floer equation (
5.5
) associated to any continuation datum
\mathfrak{c}
takes the form

\partial_{t}P+J\big(\partial_{s}P+\nabla\re(e^{-i\tilde{\alpha}(t,s)}W)\circ P\big)+\nabla(\delta H_{s})=0

with

(5.17)

\tilde{\alpha}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha^{-}(s)&\text{ if }t\leq 0,\\
\alpha^{+}(s)&\text{ if }t\geq K,\\
\theta_{1}&\text{ if }s\geq\pi,\\
\theta_{0}-\pi&\text{ if }s\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

The boundary condition for a solution of (
5.5
) is dictated by the same model map
P_{\model}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to M
as in (
2.13
) but this time with

p_{\pm}(s)\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}).

To derive the energy estimate, we apply Lemma
5.2
to the rectangle
[0,K]_{t}\times[-R_{1},R_{1}]_{s}
and let
R_{1}\to\infty
to obtain:

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}^{-}}(P(0,\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(P(K,\cdot))+\text{ some terms involving }\delta H

\displaystyle+\im(W(q_{0})\cdot\overline{g_{0}(K)-g_{0}(0)})-\im(W(q_{1})\cdot\overline{g_{1}(K)-g_{1}(0)})>\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}\int_{[0,K]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}u.

The constant
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}>0
depends only on the continuation datum
\mathfrak{c}
. Since
\tilde{\alpha}
is constant when
|s|\geq\pi
, the proof of Proposition
3.6
remains valid in this case. Hence one may form the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+};\mathfrak{c})
(which does not carry an
\mathbb{R}_{t}
-action) and prove the compactness theorem as usual. The transversality is achieved by choosing
\delta H
generically, so there is a well-defined chain map:

\displaystyle\Ch_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{+})

\displaystyle\to\Ch_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{-})

\displaystyle p_{+}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{p_{-}:\ \dim\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+})=0}\#\mathcal{M}(p_{-},p_{+};\mathfrak{c})\cdot p_{-}.

The induced map on cohomology is the continuation map
\Cont_{\mathfrak{a}^{+},\mathfrak{a}^{-}}
in (
5.9
), which is independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{c}
, since the space
\Emb=\varinjlim\Emb_{K}
is path-connected by Lemma
5.6
. For any Floer data
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime})
, we have a composition map:

(5.19)

\Emb_{K_{2}}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime})\times\Emb_{K_{1}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})\to\Emb_{K_{1}+K_{2}}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime})

sending a pair
(\Xi_{2}^{\dagger},\Xi_{1}^{\dagger})
to the embedding

\Xi^{\dagger}(t,s)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Xi_{2}^{\dagger}(t,s)&\text{ if }t\leq K_{2},\\
\Xi_{1}^{\dagger}(t-K_{2},s)+c_{\Xi^{\dagger}_{2}}+(-i\epsilon_{01}^{\prime}e^{i\beta^{\prime}})\cdot K_{2}&\text{ if }t\geq K_{2},\end{array}\right.

with
c_{\Xi^{\dagger}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}c_{\Xi_{1}^{\dagger}}+c_{\Xi_{2}^{\dagger}}+(-i\epsilon_{01}^{\prime}e^{i\beta^{\prime}})\cdot K_{2}
. This allows us to compose the cobordism data and verify the composition law (
5.10
). Thus, we have proved

Proposition 5.8
.

The Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
, which we shall denote by
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
in the sequel, is independent of the Floer data
\mathfrak{a}
up to canonical isomorphisms.

Proposition
5.8
allows us to compute
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
in some trivial cases.

Lemma 5.9
.

Recall that the projection
W(\Lambda_{q_{j},\theta_{j}})
is a ray
l_{q_{j},\theta_{j}}
emanating from
W(q_{j}),j=0,1

(
see Figure
5
)
. If the rays
l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}
and
l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}
do not intersect in
\mathbb{C}
, then
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
is trivial.

Proof.

Consider a sequence of smooth monotone functions
\alpha^{n}(s)
“approximating” the discontinuous function

\alpha^{\dagger}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta_{1}&\text{ if }s>0,\\
\theta_{0}-\pi&\text{ if }s\leq 0,\\
\end{array}\right.

For instance, we require
\partial_{s}\alpha^{n}
to be supported on
[-\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n}]_{s}
. Since
\alpha^{n}(s)
is monotone, we can choose the same pair
(\beta,\epsilon_{01})
in (
2.6
) for all
n
. Let
\mathfrak{a}^{n}=(\pi,\alpha^{n},\beta,\epsilon_{01},\delta H^{n}\equiv 0)
, and pick an
\alpha^{n}
-soliton
p^{n}\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a}^{n})
, if this space is non-empty for all
n
. We have uniform energy estimates for this sequence
(p_{n})
by Lemma
2.8
. By the compactness theorem,
(p^{n})
contains a subsequence converging a solution of (
2.11
) with
\alpha(s)=\alpha^{\dagger}(s)
and
\delta H=0
. But for this discontinuous
\alpha^{\dagger}
, such a soliton (consider the evaluation at
s=0
) are in bijection with points in
\Lambda_{0}\cap\Lambda_{1}
, which is empty if
l_{q_{0},\theta_{0}}
and
l_{q_{1},\theta_{1}}
do not intersect.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

For
q_{0}=q_{1}=q\in\Crit(W)
and any
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
, the group
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})
is an 1-dimensional vector space generated canonically by the constant map
e_{q}
at
q
, which has
\gr(e_{q})=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be any Floer datum with
\delta H=0
. Then for any
p(s)\in\mathfrak{C}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1};\mathfrak{a})
, the energy estimate from Lemma
2.8
shows that
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}|\partial_{s}p|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ p|^{2}=0
; so a soliton
p(s)\equiv q
must be constant. Since
q_{0}=q_{1}=q
,
T_{q}\Lambda_{0}
and
T_{q}\Lambda_{1}
lives in the same symplectic vector space
T_{q}M
. Then one uses the Normalization Axiom
(A’-II)
from Section
4.4
and
[
Sei08
, Example 11.20]
to show that

\gr(e_{q})=\overline{\gr}(e_{q}^{\prime})=i(T_{q}\Lambda_{0},T_{q}\Lambda_{1})=n\bigg(\lfloor\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}}{2\pi}\rfloor+1\bigg)=0.

where
e_{q}^{\prime}
is the restriction of
e_{q}
on any finite interval, and each
\Lambda_{j}
is graded as in (
4.3
).

Now given any Floer data
\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}
with trivial perturbation 1-forms, we also set
\delta H=0
in the cobordism data
\mathfrak{c}
. Then the left hand side of the energy estimate (
5.18
) is equal to

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}^{-}}(p_{-})-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}^{+}}(p_{+})+\im(W(q)\cdot\overline{g_{0}(K)-g_{0}(0)-g_{1}(K)+g_{1}(0)}).

By (
5.11
)(
5.12
) and the fact that
p_{\pm}(s)\equiv q
, this sum is zero. Hence, the Floer equation defining the continuation map has only a constant solution
P(t,s)\equiv q
. To see that the linearized operator
\mathcal{D}
at this constant solution is invertible, it suffices to work with the Landau-Ginzburg model
(T_{q}M,v\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\langle\Hess_{q}W(v),v\rangle_{g_{M}})
(this is Example
2.2
) for which the linearized problem is identical to the non-linear one. The same energy argument shows that the Hessians of
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}^{\pm}}
at
p_{\pm}
are invertible and
\mathcal{D}
is injective. To see that
\mathcal{D}
has Fredholm index
0
, one uses the Index Axiom
(A’-I)
. This proves that
\mathcal{D}
is also surjective.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Poincaré duality)
.

For any
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
and
q_{0},q_{1}\in\Crit(W)
, there is a grading-preserving isomorphism:

\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{1}\theta_{1}},\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}-2\pi})\cong D\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}},\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}})

where
D
denotes the dual of a graded vector space.

Proof.

For any admissible Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}=(R,\alpha(s),\beta,\epsilon,\delta H)
for the pair
(\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}},\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}})
, consider the involution
\tau(s)=R-s,s\in\mathbb{R}_{s}
and the Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}=(R,\alpha^{\prime}(s),\beta^{\prime},\epsilon,\delta H^{\prime})
with
\alpha^{\prime}(s)=\alpha(\tau(s))-\pi
,
\beta^{\prime}=\beta-\pi
and
\delta H^{\prime}=\tau^{*}\delta H
. Then
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
is admissible for the pair
(\Lambda_{q_{1}\theta_{1}},\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}-2\pi})
, and the complex
\Ch_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{1}\theta_{1}},\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}-2\pi};\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})
is the dual of
\Ch_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}},\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}};\mathfrak{a})
. The fact that this map is grading-preserving follows from the normalization Axiom
(A-II)
and
(A’-II)
.
∎

5.3.
The embedding space

Finally, we prove Lemma
5.6
. This proof will be used again when we construct the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
in Section
8
, so we explain this in some detail.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Let
D^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the unit disk and
f:D^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
any immersion. A simple argument using winding numbers shows that

(5.20)

f
is an embedding if and only if
f|_{S^{1}}
is.

We shall use this criterion frequently to decide whether
f
is an embedding. Now consider the space
\Emb_{K}^{*}
of embeddings

\Xi^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{C}

that satisfies (
5.11
) and a weaker version of (
5.13
):

(5.21)

-\im(e^{-i\theta_{j}}\partial_{t}g_{j}(t))>0,j=0,1,t\in\mathbb{R}_{t},

where
g_{0}(t)=\Xi^{\dagger}(t,0)
and
g_{1}(t)=\Xi^{\dagger}(t,\pi)
. This embedding space
\Emb_{K}^{*}
is more convenient than
\Emb_{K}
: the condition (
5.21
) says that the curve
g_{t}(t)
is transverse to the direction
e^{i\theta_{j}},j=0,1
, which is a property invariant under a self-diffeomorphism of
[0,K]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
preserving the boundary. We have a digram of natural inclusions for any
K<K^{\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{K^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{K}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Emb_{K^{\prime}}^{*}.}

For any continuous map
\varphi:S^{n}\to\Emb_{K}
,
n\geq 0
, we show that
\varphi
is null-homotopic as a map
S^{n}\to\Emb_{K_{2}}
for some
K_{2}>K
. We write
\Xi_{r}^{\dagger}=\varphi(r):\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{C},r\in S^{n}
and
c_{r}=c_{\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}}\in\mathbb{C}
for the constant in (
5.11
). The function
r\mapsto c_{r}
is continuous, so
c_{\max}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\max|c_{r}|<\infty
.
This null-homotopy is constructed in three steps:

Step
1. For some
K_{1}\gg K
,
\varphi
is homotopic to a family in
\Emb_{K_{1}}
with
c_{r}\equiv 0,r\in S^{n}
.

It suffices to deform the embedding
\Xi_{r}^{\dagger}
on the rectangle
[K,+\infty)_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
using a linear homotopy. Pick a cutoff function
\chi_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that
\chi_{1}(x)=0
for
x\leq 0
and
=1
for
x\geq 1
. For any
r\in S^{n}
and
y\in[0,1]
, consider the map

\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}(t,s)=-y\cdot c_{r}\cdot\chi_{1}(\frac{t-K}{K_{1}-K})+\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s),\ (t,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}.

Then
\Xi_{r,0}^{\dagger}=\Xi_{r}^{\dagger}
. For any
K\leq t\leq K_{1}
,

\big|\partial_{t}\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}(t,s)-(-i\epsilon^{+}_{01}e^{i\beta^{+}})\big|=\bigg|\frac{yc_{r}}{K_{1}-K}\cdot(\partial_{x}\chi_{1})(\frac{t-K}{K_{1}-K})\bigg|\leq\frac{c_{\max}\|\partial_{x}\chi_{1}\|_{\infty}}{K_{1}-K}.

Using (
5.20
), one verifies that
\Xi^{\dagger}_{r,y}
is an embedding satisfying (
5.11
)(
5.12
)(
5.13
) for any
K_{1}\gg K
; then

\varphi_{y}:S^{n}\to\Emb_{K_{1}},\ r\mapsto\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger},\ y\in[0,1],

is the desired homotopy.

Step
2. From now on, we assume that
c_{r}\equiv 0,r\in S^{n}
. In this case,
\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s)
is constant in
r
when
t\leq 0
or
\geq K
, and we show that
\varphi
is null-homotopic in the larger space
\Emb_{K}^{*}
.

Since
S^{n}
is compact, the property (
5.12
) holds for this family with a uniform constant
0<\delta_{\min}\ll 1
, i.e.,

\partial_{s}\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcr}-e^{i\theta_{1}}&\text{ if }&\pi-\delta_{\min}\leq s\leq\pi,\\
e^{i\theta_{0}}&\text{ if }&0\leq s\leq\delta_{\min},\end{array}\right.

for all
r\in S^{n}
. We first show that
\varphi
is homotopic in
\Emb_{K}^{*}
to a family
\varphi_{2}
which is also constant in
r
when
s\in[0,\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2}]\cup[\pi-\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2},\pi]
. This is done by pushing the embeddings
\Xi_{r}^{\dagger}
“outwards”; see Figure
11
below. To illustrate, pick a base point
*\in S^{n}
and a smooth function
f:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\text{supp}f=[0,K]_{t}
, so
f>0
on
(0,K)_{t}
. Consider the path

(5.22)

g_{0}^{*}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{C},\ t\mapsto\Xi^{\dagger}_{*}(t,0)-f(t)e^{i\theta_{0}},

which is still transverse to the direction
e^{i\theta_{0}}
, as well as the embedding

\displaystyle\Xi_{0,*}^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle(t,s)

\displaystyle\mapsto g_{0}^{*}(t)+e^{i\theta_{0}}\cdot s.

Choose another cutoff function
\chi_{2}:[0,\pi]_{s}\to[0,1]
such that
\chi_{2}^{\prime}\leq 0
,
\chi_{2}(s)\equiv 1
on
[0,\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2}]
and
\equiv 0
on
[\delta_{\min},\pi]
. Then consider the linear homotopy

\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}(t,s)=(1-y\chi_{2}(s))\cdot\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s)+y\chi_{2}(s)\cdot\Xi^{\dagger}_{0,*}(t,s),\ r\in S^{n},\ y\in[0,1].

Note that
\Xi_{r,y}(t,s)=\Xi_{r}(r,s)
for all
s\in[\delta_{\min},\pi]
and
y\in[0,1]
. For
s\in[0,\delta_{\min}]
, we compute

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}(t,s)

\displaystyle=(1-y\chi_{2}(s))\cdot\partial_{t}\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s)+y\chi_{2}(s)\cdot\partial_{t}\Xi^{\dagger}_{0,*}(t,s),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}(t,s)

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{0}}+y\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)\cdot(\Xi_{0,*}^{\dagger}(t,s)-\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,s))

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{0}}+y\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)\cdot(g_{0}^{*}(t)-\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,0))

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{0}}\cdot(1-y\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)f(t))+y\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)\cdot(\Xi^{\dagger}_{*}(t,0)-\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(t,0)).

Since
\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)\leq 0
,
1-y\chi_{2}^{\prime}(s)f(t)\geq 0
. Moreover,
\partial_{t}\Xi_{r,y}(t,s)
is transverse to the direction
e^{i\theta_{0}}
for all
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\delta_{\min}]_{s}
. Since
f(t)\neq 0
on
(0,K)
, if
f
is multiplied by a large constant
\gg 0
, then
\Xi_{r,y}^{\dagger}
becomes an immersion (and so an embedding by (
5.20
)) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\delta_{\min}]_{s}
for any
r\in S^{n}
and
y\in[0,1]
. We apply the same trick and construct another homotopy for the other boundary component
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{\pi\}
so that at the end of the second homotopy we obtain a family
(\Xi^{\dagger}_{r,2})_{r\in S^{n}}
which is constant whenever
s\in[0,\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2}]\cup[\pi-\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2},\pi]
or
t\in(-\infty,0]\cup[K,\infty)
. Finally, we construct the null-homotopy of
\varphi
using the fact that the group
\Diff(D^{2},\partial D^{2})
of self-diffeomorphisms rel boundary is trivial
[
Sma59
]
.

Step
3. By
Step
2, we have a continuous map
\psi:(D^{n+1},S^{n})\to(\Emb_{K}^{*},\Emb_{K})
with
\psi|_{S^{n}}=\varphi
. We show that for some
K_{2}\gg K
,
\psi
can be pushed into
\Emb_{K_{2}}
inside
\Emb_{K_{2}}^{*}
by precomposing with a family of self-diffeomorphisms of
[0,K_{2}]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}
. More precisely, one can find a continuous family of self-diffeomorphisms

(5.23)

\Phi_{r,y}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s},r\in D^{n+1},y\in[0,1]

such that

•

\Phi_{r,y}=\Id
if
t\not\in[0,K_{2}]
or
s\in[\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2},\ \pi-\frac{\delta_{\min}}{2}]
;

•

\Phi_{r,y}=\Id
when
r\in S^{n}
or
y=0
;

•

\Phi_{r,y}
preserves the
s
-coordinate, i.e.,
\Phi_{r,y}(t,s)=(\tilde{\Phi}_{r,y,s}(t),s)
for a family
\tilde{\Phi}_{r,y,s}
of self-diffeomorphism of
[0,K_{2}]_{t}
rel boundary;

•

for
y=1
,
\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}\circ\Phi_{r,1}
satisfies the stronger condition (
5.13
).

Note that the weaker condition (
5.21
) is invariant under this operation. It is always possible to achieve (
5.13
) by reparameterizing the time variable. For instance, take
K_{2}=K\epsilon^{-2}
for some
0<\epsilon\ll 1
, and consider a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\Phi}:[0,K_{2}]_{t}\to[0,K_{2}]_{t}
such that

\partial_{t}\tilde{\Phi}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&t\in[0,\epsilon]\cup[K_{2}-\epsilon,K_{2}]_{t},\\
\epsilon&t\in[2\epsilon,K\epsilon^{-1}],\\
\leq 1+2\epsilon&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then for
\epsilon\ll 1
, the path
\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}(\tilde{\Phi}(t),s)
satisfies (
5.13
) for all
r\in D^{n+1}
and
s\in\{0,\pi\}
. Then the existence of the family
\Phi_{r,y}
in (
5.23
) follows from the contractibility of
\Diff(D^{1},\partial D^{1})
.

Finally, (
5.12
) is achieved by precomposing
\Xi^{\dagger}_{r}\circ\Phi_{r,y}
with another family of self-diffeomorphisms of
[0,K_{2}]_{t}\times[0,s]_{s}
fixing the boundary. This completes the proof of Lemma
5.6
.

(The key observation here is that given (
5.21
), (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) can be arranged by reparameterizing the surface near the boundary).
∎

.
Push the curves
g_{0}(t)
and
g_{1}(t)
outwards.

Part III
Fukaya-Seidel Categories

In this part we take up the task of constructing the Fukaya-Seidel category of a tame Landau-Ginzburg model. As we shall avoid Lagrangian boundary condition whenever possible, higher operations in the Fukaya-Seidel category are defined by considering the Floer equation (
5.5
) on Riemann surfaces with planar ends — to each boundary component of a pointed disk is attached a lower half plane
\mathbb{H}^{-}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{-}_{s}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{s}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\pm s\geq 0\}
denotes the closed half real line. The analysis of the Floer equation (
5.5
) on such a surface is analogous to the case of
\alpha
-instantons on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
.

To make this idea work, we have to specify a family of metrics on pointed disks which is isometric to
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\delta)_{s}
near each boundary component. This is done by choosing suitable meromorphic quadratic differentials, and we summarize relevant theories in Section
7
. Section
8
is devoted to the construction of the Fukaya-Seidel category
\mathcal{A}
, the Koszul dual category
\mathcal{B}
and the diagonal bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
. Their invariance is verified using categorical localization. In Section
9
we explain the origin of the geometric filtration through a neck-stretching limit and upgrade
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
into a filtered bimodule. Section
10
is devoted to the proof of the Koszul duality, i.e., Theorem
1.9

(
1
)

. The vertical gluing theorem, which is the main analytic input of this work, is proved in Section
11
.

Since this paper is also targeted at people who specialize in gauge theory, we begin this part with a short review of
A_{\infty}
-categories in Section
6
. The standard reference of this subject is
[
Sei08
, Chapter 1]
. Since our base field
\mathbb{K}
is of characteristic
2
, we shall ignore the signs in
A_{\infty}
-associativity relations. Experts should feel free to skip this section completely.

6.
Algebraic Preliminaries

6.1.
A_{\infty}
-categories and
A_{\infty}
-functors

An
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}
consists of a set of objects
\Ob\mathcal{A}
, a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector space
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})
for every pair of objects
(X_{0},X_{1})
, and composition maps of every order
d\geq 1
,

\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{d}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{d})[2-d].

By
[k]
, we mean the degree of a graded vector space is shifted down by
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. These maps are required to satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-associativity relations:

(6.1)

\sum_{m,n}\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{d-m+1}(a_{d},\cdots,a_{m+n+1},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(a_{m+n},\cdots,a_{n+1}),a_{n}\cdots,a_{1})=0,\ d\geq 1.

In this paper, all
A_{\infty}
-categories are assumed to be strictly unital, i.e., there exists an element
e_{X}\in\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X,X)
for all objects
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
such that

(6.2)

\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(e_{X})=0,\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(\cdot,e_{X})=\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(e_{X},\cdot)=\Id\text{ and }\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{d}(\cdots,e_{X},\cdots)=0,d\geq 3.

The first order map
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}
makes
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})
into a chain complex for every pair
(X_{0},X_{1})
, and
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}
is a chain map of degree 0. The cohomological category
H(\mathcal{A})
associated to
\mathcal{A}
consists of the same set of objects, with the morphism spaces replaced by
\Hom_{H(\mathcal{A})}(X_{0},X_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H(\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1}),\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{1})
and the composition maps by
[\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}]
. A dg category is considered as an
A_{\infty}
-category with
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv 0
,
d\geq 3
.

An
A_{\infty}
-functor between two
A_{\infty}
-categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
consists of a map
\mathcal{F}:\Ob\mathcal{A}\to\Ob\mathcal{B}
and mutilinear maps of every order
d\geq 1
,

\mathcal{F}^{d}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{0},\mathcal{F}X_{d})[1-d],

which satisfy the polynomial equations:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{r}

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{r}}\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{r}(\mathcal{F}^{s_{r}}(a_{d},\cdots,a_{d-s_{r}+1}),\cdots\mathcal{F}^{s_{1}}(a_{s_{1}},\cdots,a_{1}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}\mathcal{F}^{d-m+1}(a_{d},\cdots,a_{m+n+1},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(a_{m+n},\cdots,a_{n+1}),a_{n}\cdots,a_{1}).

A (strictly unital)
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
preserves strict units, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}^{1}(e_{X})=e_{\mathcal{F}X}
and
\mathcal{F}^{d}(\cdots,e_{X},\cdots)=0
for all objects
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
and
d\geq 2
. By (
6.3
),
\mathcal{F}^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{0},\mathcal{F}X_{1})
is a chain map, which induces an ordinary linear functor between their cohomological categories
H(\mathcal{F}):H(\mathcal{A})\to H(\mathcal{B}).
An
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is called a quasi-isomorphism (resp. a quasi-equivalence) if
H(\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism (resp. equivalence) of categories. The functor category
\fun(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is an
A_{\infty}
-category consisting of
A_{\infty}
-functors between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
as objects, while cycles of
\hom_{\fun(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
are considered as natural transformations between
A_{\infty}
-functors
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
. For the precise definition, see
[
Sei08
, Section (1d)]
. We record an elementary lemma derived from the
A_{\infty}
-relations (
6.3
).

Lemma 6.1
.

For any objects
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
and any
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, the following diagrams are commutative up to chain-homotopy:

(6.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{1},\mathcal{F}X_{2})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{0},\mathcal{F}X_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{1}\otimes\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}(\cdot,\mathcal{F}^{1}(\cdot))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{1}}

(6.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{1},X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{1},\mathcal{F}X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{2})\otimes D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{F}X_{0},\mathcal{F}X_{2})\otimes D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}\mapsto\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}(a,\cdot)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\mapsto\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}(b,\mathcal{F}^{1}(\cdot))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{1}\otimes\Id}

where the second diagram (
6.5
) is obtained from the first (
6.4
) by replacing
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})
by its vector dual
D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})
.

6.2.
A_{\infty}
-modules and
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

Denote by
\Chain
the dg category of chain complexes of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces, and let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be
A_{\infty}
-categories. An
A_{\infty}
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}
over
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a
A_{\infty}
-bifunctor
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}^{\opp}\to\Chain
. More concretely,
\mathcal{N}
assigns to every pair of objects
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
and
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
a graded
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\mathcal{N}(Y,X)
along with maps

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r|1|s}:

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{r-1},X_{r})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\mathcal{N}(Y_{s},X_{0})

\displaystyle\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{s-1},Y_{s})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\to\mathcal{N}(Y_{0},X_{r}),r,s\geq 0,

which satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-associativity relations:

\displaystyle\sum_{r^{\prime}<r,s^{\prime}<s}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r|1|s^{\prime}}(a_{r},\cdots,a_{r-r^{\prime}+1},\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r-r^{\prime}|1|s-s^{\prime}}(a_{r-r^{\prime}},\cdots,a_{1},x,b_{s},\cdots,b_{s^{\prime}+1}),b_{s^{\prime}},\cdots,b_{1})

(6.7)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m\leq r}\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r-m|1|s}(a_{r},\cdots,a_{n+m+1},\mu^{m}_{\mathcal{A}}(a_{n+m},\cdots,a_{n+1}),a_{n},\cdots,a_{1},x,b_{s},\cdots,b_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{n+m\leq s}\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r|1|s-m}(a_{r},\cdots a_{1},x,b_{s},\cdots,b_{n+m+1},\mu^{m}_{\mathcal{B}}(b_{n+m},\cdots,b_{n+1}),b_{n},\cdots b_{1})=0.

We further impose a unitality condition: for any objects
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
and
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mu^{1|1|0}_{\mathcal{N}}(e_{X},\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{0|1|1}(\cdot,e_{Y})=\Id,

\displaystyle\mu^{r|1|s}_{\mathcal{N}}(\cdots,e_{X},\cdots,x,\cdots)

\displaystyle=\mu^{r|1|s}_{\mathcal{N}}(\cdots,x,\cdots,e_{Y},\cdots)=0\text{ for }r+s\geq 2.

There are two additional points of view to think of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules. For any
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}
, construct an
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}
by setting
\Ob\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}=\Ob\mathcal{B}\sqcup\Ob\mathcal{A}
,

(6.9)

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}(X_{0},X_{1})

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1}),

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1}),

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}(Y_{0},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(Y_{0},X_{0}),

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}(X_{0},Y_{0})

\displaystyle=\{0\},

and
\mu_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{d}=\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{d},\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{d}
or
\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{r|1|s},r+1+s=d
, depending on the entries. Then the relations
(
6.2
)
and
(
6.8
)
are equivalent to saying that
(\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}},\mu_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}}^{d},d\geq 1)
is an
A_{\infty}
-category in the sense of (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).

Consider the
A_{\infty}
-category with a single object
*
and
\hom_{\mathbb{K}}(*,*)=\mathbb{K}e_{*}
, which we denote by
\mathbb{K}
. A right
\mathcal{B}
-module (resp. left
\mathcal{A}
-module) is simply a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\mathcal{M}
with
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{K}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{K}
), and we write
\mathcal{M}(Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{M}(Y,*)
(resp.
\mathcal{M}(X)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{M}(*,X)
). More concretely, a right
A_{\infty}
-module over
\mathcal{B}
is an
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{B}^{\opp}\to\Chain
which assigns to every object
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
a graded vector space
\mathcal{M}(Y)
together with composition maps of every order
d\geq 1
,

\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{d}=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{0|1|d-1}:\mathcal{M}(X_{d-1})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{d-2},X_{d-1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{M}(X_{0}),

satisfying a suitable variant of (
6.2
) and (
6.8
); see
[
Sei08
, Section (1j)]
. The space of right
\mathcal{B}
-modules form a dg category, which we denote by
\mathcal{Q}=\rmod(\mathcal{B})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\fun(\mathcal{B},\Chain)
. For any
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1}\in\Ob\mathcal{Q}
, a morphism
t=(t^{d})_{d\geq 1}\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})
of degree
|t|
, also called a pre-module homomorphism, consists of a sequence of maps:

t^{d}:\mathcal{M}_{0}(Y_{d-1})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{d-1},Y_{d-2})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\to\mathcal{M}_{1}(Y_{0})[|t|-d+1].

The strict unitality requires
t^{d}(x,a_{d-1},\cdots,a_{1}),d\geq 2
to vanish if one of
a_{i}
’s is the unit. The differential on
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})
is defined by the formula:

\displaystyle(\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}t)^{d}(x,

\displaystyle b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{1})=\sum_{n}\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}^{n+1}\big(t^{d-n}(x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{n+1}),b_{n},\cdots,b_{1}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n}t^{n+1}\big(\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}^{d-n}(b,a_{d-1},\cdots,b_{n+1}),b_{n},\cdots,b_{1}\big)

(6.10)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m+n<d}t^{d-m+1}\big(x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{n+m+1},\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{m}(b_{n+m},\cdots,b_{n+1}),b_{n}\cdots,b_{1}\big),

while the higher compositions are defined by:

\big(\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(t_{2},t_{1})\big)^{d}(x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{1})=\sum_{n}t_{2}^{n+1}\big(t_{1}^{d-n}(x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{n+1}),b_{n},\cdots,b_{1}\big),\ \mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{d}=0,d\geq 3.

A right
\mathcal{B}
-module homomorphism
t:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
is a cycle of
\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
, whose first order map
t^{1}
induces an ordinary module homomorphism
H(t):H(\mathcal{M}_{0})\to H(\mathcal{M}_{1})
.

Returning to the discussion of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules, an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}
defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor
r_{\mathcal{N}}
which assigns to each object
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
the right
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathcal{N}(-,X)
. The mutilinear maps on the morphism spaces

r_{\mathcal{N}}^{d}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{N}(-,X_{0}),\mathcal{N}(-,X_{d}))

are precisely given by
\mu_{\mathcal{N}}^{d|1|*}
. Then the relations (
6.2
) are equivalent to the polynomial equations (
6.4
) for
r_{\mathcal{N}}
. Similarly, let
\lmod(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\fun(\mathcal{A},\Chain)
denote the dg-category of left
\mathcal{A}
-modules. Then the
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}
also defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor

l_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathcal{B}\to\lmod(\mathcal{A})^{\opp}.

Consider
\mathcal{A}
as the diagonal
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})
-bimodule with

\mathcal{A}(X_{0},X_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1}),X_{0},X_{1}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}.
Then we obtain the Yoneda embedding functors:

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\to\rmod(\mathcal{A}),\ l_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\to\lmod(\mathcal{A})^{\opp},

which are cohomologically full and faithful. More generally, we have

Lemma 6.2

(
[
Sei03
, Lemma 2.12]
)
.

For
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
, denote by
\mathcal{X}^{r}
(resp.
\mathcal{X}^{l}
) the Yoneda image of
X
under
r_{\mathcal{A}}
(resp.
l_{\mathcal{A}}
). Then for any right
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}
and any left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, the Yoneda maps,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(X)

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\rmod(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{X}^{r},\mathcal{M}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(X)

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\lmod(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{X}^{l},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{d+1}(x,\cdots))_{d\geq 1},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mu_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}^{d+1}(\cdots,x))_{d\geq 1}.

are quasi-isomorphisms.

In this paper, we shall use the extended
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}
to formulate the functoriality (and invariance) of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules. An
A_{\infty}
-bifunctor between an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}
and an
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
-bimodule
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is simply
A_{\infty}
-functor
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathcal{G}(\Ob\mathcal{A})\subset\Ob\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}(\Ob\mathcal{B})\subset\Ob\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. In other words, any
A_{\infty}
-bifunctor
\mathcal{G}
consists of
A_{\infty}
-functors
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{G}|_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{G}|_{\mathcal{B}}
together with maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r|1|s}:

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{r-1},X_{r})\otimes\cdots\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\mathcal{N}(Y_{s},X_{0})

\displaystyle\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{s-1},Y_{s})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\to\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}Y_{0},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}X_{r}),r,s\geq 0.

satisfying certain relations. The next lemma allows us to think of
\mathcal{G}
in terms of the
A_{\infty}
-functors
r_{\mathcal{N}}
and
r_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any
A_{\infty}
-bifunctor
\mathcal{G}
, the bilinear maps
\mathcal{G}^{r|1|s},r,s\geq 0
define an
A_{\infty}
-natural transformation
r_{\mathcal{G}}\in\fun_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Q})}(r_{\mathcal{N}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\circ r_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}})
. Conversely, any bifunctor
\mathcal{G}
consists of
A_{\infty}
-functors
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and such an
A_{\infty}
-natural transformation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rmod(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rmod(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Downarrow{r_{\mathcal{G}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}

In particular,
\mathcal{G}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}
are quasi-isomorphisms and
H(r_{\mathcal{G}})
is a natural isomorphism between the functors
H(r_{\mathcal{N}})
and
H(\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\circ r_{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}):H(\mathcal{A})\to H(\mathcal{Q})
.

6.3.
Mapping cones

By
[
Sei08
, Section (3s)]
, the dg-category
\mathcal{Q}=\rmod(\mathcal{B})
of right
\mathcal{B}
-modules is triangulated. For any
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism
t=(t^{d})_{d\geq 1}:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
of degree zero, the mapping cone
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}one(t)
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(Y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{0}(Y)[1]\oplus\mathcal{M}_{1}(Y),

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{d}\big((x_{0},x_{1}),a_{d-1},\cdots,a_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}^{d}(x_{0},a_{d-1},\cdots,a_{1}),\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}^{d}(x_{1},a_{d-1},\cdots,a_{1})+t^{d}(x_{0},a_{d-1},\cdots,a_{1})\big)

We have obvious inclusion and projection pre-homomorphisms:

(6.11)

\iota_{0}:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{C},\ \pi_{0}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{M}_{0},\ \iota_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{C},\ \pi_{1}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{M}_{1},

with
\deg\iota_{0}=-1,\deg\pi_{0}=1
and
\deg\iota_{1}=\deg\pi_{1}=0
,
which fit into the digram below:

(6.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

Lemma 6.4

(
[
Sei08
, Lemma 3.35]
)
.

A direct computation shows that

\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\iota_{1})=\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\pi_{0})=0,\ \mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\iota_{0})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(\iota_{1},t),\ \mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\pi_{1})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(t,\pi_{0}),\ 0=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\pi_{0},\iota_{1}),

and moreover,
e_{\mathcal{C}}=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\iota_{1},\pi_{1})+\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\iota_{0},\pi_{0})
. By the distinguished triangulation detection lemma
[
Sei08
, Lemma 3.7]
, the diagram (
6.12
) descends to an exact triangle in the cohomological category of
\mathcal{Q}
.

6.4.
Directed
A_{\infty}
-categories

In this paper, we shall primarily work with finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories, meaning that
(\Ob\mathcal{A},\prec)
is a finite set equipped with a total order
\prec
such that

\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&\text{ if }X_{1}\prec X_{0},\\
\mathbb{K}\cdot e_{X_{0}}&\text{ if }X_{0}=X_{1},\\
\text{finite dimensional over }\mathbb{K}&\text{ if }X_{0}\prec X_{1}.\end{array}\right.

For finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories, we only consider
A_{\infty}
-modules (resp.
A_{\infty}
-bimodules) with
finite dimensional
morphism spaces. Let
\Chain_{\fin}
denote the dg category of
finite dimensional
chain complexes of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces. Let
\mathcal{P}_{l}=\lfmod(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\fun(\mathcal{A},\Chain_{\fin})
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\fun(\mathcal{A}^{\opp},\Chain_{\fin}))
denote the dg category of finite left
\mathcal{A}
-modules (resp. right
\mathcal{A}
-modules). Consider the duality functor

\mathcal{D}:\Chain_{\fin}\to\Chain_{\fin}^{\opp},

which assigns to a finite chain complex
V
its dual complex
DV[-\mathfrak{n}]
whose degree is shifted up by an integer
\mathfrak{n}
. This defines a pair of quasi-isomorphisms of dg categories, denoted also by
\mathcal{D}
,

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{r},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{P}_{r}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{D}\mathcal{M},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N},

which allows us to go back and forth between finite right
\mathcal{A}
-modules and finite left
\mathcal{A}
-modules. This degree shifting is conventional, and in applications one takes
\mathfrak{n}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(TM,J_{M})
; cf. Section
12.5
. For any left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{M}
assigns to each object
X\in\mathcal{A}
the dual complex
D\mathcal{M}(X)[-n]
along with the dualized composition maps
\mu_{\mathcal{D}\mathcal{M}}^{d}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{d}
:

(6.14)

D\mathcal{M}(X_{0})[-\mathfrak{n}]\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{d-2},X_{d-1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},X_{1})\to D\mathcal{M}(X_{d-1})[-\mathfrak{n}].

Since
\mathcal{M}
is strictly unital and
\mathcal{A}
is directed, the map (
6.14
) is only non-tautological if
X_{0}\prec X_{1}\prec\cdots\prec X_{d-1}
. A similar condition holds also for any pre-module homomorphism
t\in\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{0})
; hence, the dg category
\mathcal{P}_{r}
(and so
\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}
) has finite dimensional morphism spaces. Moreover, by
[
Sei08
, Corollary 5.26]
,
\mathcal{P}_{r}
(and so
\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}
) is a triangulated envelope of
\mathcal{A}
.

In the same vein, the duality functor provides a rotational operation on the space of finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories. Suppose that
\Ob\mathcal{A}
is ordered such that
