X_{i}\prec X_{j}
if
i<j
; then define a new
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{A}
with

(6.15)

\displaystyle\Ob\mathcal{C}\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle:X_{2}\prec\cdots\prec X_{n}\prec X_{1},

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{A}}(X_{i},X_{1})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{1},X_{n})[-\mathfrak{n}],

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{A}}(X_{i},X_{j})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{i},X_{j}),2\leq i,j\leq n.

The composition maps
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{d}
that involve the object
X_{1}
are dualized as in (
6.14
), while others remain fixed. It is clear that
\mathcal{C}^{n}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}
if
\mathcal{A}
has
n
objects. The next three lemmas, which hold in general for any c-unital
A_{\infty}
-categories, allow us to speak of invariance of finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories.

Lemma 6.5

(
[
Sei08
, Corollary 1.14]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be any quasi-isomorphism between finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories, then
\mathcal{F}
has an inverse up to homotopy, i.e., there exists another quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}
is homotopic to
\Id_{\mathcal{B}}
, and
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}
is homotopic to
\Id_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be quasi-isomorphisms between finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories such that the induced functor
H(\mathcal{F}_{0})=H(\mathcal{F}_{1}):H(\mathcal{A})\to H(\mathcal{B})
are identical on the cohomological categories. Then
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is homotopic to
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

If
\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}
induce the same map on
\Ob\mathcal{A}
, then the difference
\mathcal{F}_{0}-\mathcal{F}_{1}\in\hom_{\fun(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
defines a cycle.
\mathcal{F}_{0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
are called homotopic if
\mathcal{F}_{0}-\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a boundary, which implies that
H(\mathcal{F}_{0})=H(\mathcal{F}_{1})
as ordinary functors. Homotopy is an equivalence relation between
A_{\infty}
-functors
[
Sei08
, Section (1h)]
. With that being said, Lemma
6.6
follows from the homological perturbation theory
[
Sei08
, Section (1i)]
and by repeating the proof of
[
Sei08
, Corollary 1.14]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be any quasi-equivalence between finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories such that
\mathcal{F}
is injective on
\Ob\mathcal{A}
. Then the cohomological functor
H(\mathcal{F})
identifies
H(\mathcal{A})
with a full subcategory of
H(\mathcal{B})
. Let
G:H(\mathcal{B})\to H(\mathcal{A})
be any equivalence of categories such that
G\circ H(\mathcal{F})=\Id_{H(\mathcal{A})}
, and
H(\mathcal{F})\circ G
is natural isomorphic to
\Id_{H(\mathcal{B})}
. Then there is a quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
with
H(\mathcal{G})=G
.

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{B}
formed by the image of
\mathcal{F}
. By Lemma
6.5
,
\mathcal{F}
has an inverse
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}:\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{A}
up to homotopy. In particular,
H(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})=G|_{H(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})}
. In order to extend
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
to the whole
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{B}
while lifting the given cohomological functor
G
, one exploits the extension lemma
[
Sei08
, Lemma 1.10]
and repeats the argument in
[
Sei08
, Lemma 2.8 & Theorem 2.9]
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1}
be finite right
\mathcal{B}
-modules. An
A_{\infty}
-module homomorphism
t:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
is called a quasi-isomorphism if
H(t):H(\mathcal{M}_{0}(Y))\to H(\mathcal{M}_{1}(Y))
is an isomorphism for all objects
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 6.8

(
[
Sei08
, Lemma 1.16]
)
.

If
t:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism, then the left and right composition with
t
induce quasi-isomorphisms

\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\text{ and resp. }\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{0})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{1})

for any
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Any quasi-isomorphism
t:\mathcal{M}_{0}\to\mathcal{M}_{1}
admit a quasi-inverse
s:\mathcal{M}_{1}\to\mathcal{M}_{0}
such that
[\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(t,s)]=[e_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}]
in
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{1}))
and
[\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(s,t)]=[e_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}]
in
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{0}))
. In particular,
H(s)=H(t)^{-1}:H(\mathcal{M}_{1}(Y))\to H(\mathcal{M}_{0}(Y))
for all objects
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
6.8
, we find cycles
s_{0},s_{1}\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{0})
such that
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(s_{0},t)\cong e_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}
and
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(t,s_{1})\cong e_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
is a dg category with
\mu^{3}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}\equiv 0,d\geq 3
, we have

s_{1}=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(e_{\mathcal{M}_{0}},s_{1})\cong\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(s_{0},t),s_{1})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(s_{0},\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(t,s_{1}))\cong\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}(s_{0},e_{\mathcal{M}_{1}})=s_{0}.\qed

6.5.
Koszul Duality

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be finite directed
A_{\infty}
-categories, and let
\mathcal{N}
be an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodules such that all morphism spaces
\mathcal{N}(Y,X)
are finite dimensional. Then the
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{N}}
defined by (
6.9
) is a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category if we insist that
Y\prec X
for all
X\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
and
Y\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
. Suppose that
\Ob\mathcal{B}=\{Y_{m},\cdots,Y_{1}\}
is directed such that
Y_{j}\prec Y_{k}
if
j>k
; then consider the right
\mathcal{B}
-modules

\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!}(Y_{j})=\mathbb{K}\text{ and }\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!}(Y_{k})=0\text{ if }k\neq j,\ j=1,\cdots,m.

A simple computation shows that
\{\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!}\}_{j=1}^{m}
form a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-subcategory of
\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
, which we denote by
\mathcal{B}^{!}
, with

\mathcal{Y}^{!}_{1}\prec\mathcal{Y}^{!}_{2}\prec\cdots\prec\mathcal{Y}^{!}_{m}.

Indeed, we have

\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!},\mathcal{Y}_{k}^{!})=0\text{ if }j>k
and
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!},\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!})=\mathbb{K}\cdot e_{\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!}}.

Definition 6.10
.

A finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{A}
is called
the Koszul dual category
of
\mathcal{B}
, if there exists a finite
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\Delta={}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
such that the induced functor

r_{\Delta}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B})

identifies
\mathcal{A}
with the subcategory
\mathcal{B}^{!}
by a quasi-isomorphism. In particular, if
\Ob\mathcal{A}
is directed such that
X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec\cdots\prec X_{m}
, then

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\Delta(Y_{k},X_{j})=\delta_{jk}.

This bimodule is to specify the quasi-isomorphism
r_{\Delta}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}^{!}
and is viewed as part of the data that defines the Koszul duality.
∎

Let
\bar{\Delta}={}_{\mathcal{B}^{!}}\bar{\Delta}_{\mathcal{B}}
be the
(\mathcal{B}^{!},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule inducing the canonical embedding
\mathcal{B}^{!}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. Then any Koszul dual category
(\mathcal{A},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})
of
\mathcal{B}
fits into a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{r}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\bar{\Delta}}}

In particular, the extended
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{\Delta}}
by Lemma
6.3
.

Lemma 6.11
.

If
(\mathcal{A},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})
is any Koszul dual category of
\mathcal{B}
, then the dual
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
-bimodule
\mathcal{D}\Delta={}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{D}\Delta)_{\mathcal{A}}
identifies
\mathcal{B}
as the Koszul dual category of
\mathcal{A}
. In particular, the induced functor

\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{D}\Delta}:\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle Y_{k}

\displaystyle\mapsto(X_{j}\mapsto(\mathcal{D}\Delta)(S_{j},Y_{k})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D(\Delta(Y_{k},X_{j}))).

is cohomologically full and faithful.

Proof.

It suffices to work out the special case that
(\mathcal{A},_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})=(\mathcal{B}^{!},_{\mathcal{B}^{!}}\bar{\Delta}_{\mathcal{B}})
. The Yoneda embedding functor
r_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}
identifies
\mathcal{B}
with a full subcategory
\mathcal{B}^{\wedge}
of
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. Let
\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{k}
be the Yoneda image of
Y_{k}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
, and consider the
(\mathcal{B}^{\wedge},\mathcal{B}^{!})
-bimodule
\Delta^{\prime}

\Delta^{\prime}(\mathcal{Y}_{k}^{r},\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{Y}_{k}^{r},\mathcal{Y}_{j}^{!}).

By Lemma
6.2
, the Yoneda functor
r_{\mathcal{B}}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\wedge}
extends to a quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{\Delta}}\to\mathcal{E}_{\Delta^{\prime}}
. Using Lemma
6.3
, we may check instead that the functor
r_{\mathcal{D}\Delta^{\prime}}:\mathcal{B}^{\wedge}\to\rfmod(\mathcal{B}^{!})
is cohomologically full and faithful.
This functor can be viewed as a composition:

(6.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lmod(\mathcal{Q}_{r})^{\opp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lmod(\mathcal{B}^{!})^{\opp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lfmod(\mathcal{B}^{!})^{\opp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rfmod(\mathcal{B}^{!}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{B}^{!}}}
inclusion
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{*}}

where
\iota:\mathcal{B}^{!}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}
denotes the inclusion functor. Since the morphism spaces of
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
are all finite dimensional, the image of
\iota^{*}\circ l_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
lies in the smaller category
\lfmod(\mathcal{B}^{!})^{\opp}
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
is a triangulated envelop of
\mathcal{B}^{!}
and the left Yoneda embedding functor
l_{\mathcal{B}^{!}}
is cohomologically full and faithful, the same holds for the dashed arrow in (
6.16
). This finishes the proof of Lemma
6.11
.
∎

The dashed arrow in (
6.16
) can be understood more concretely as follows. Let
(\mathcal{A},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})
be any Koszul dual category of
\mathcal{B}
, then we have a pair of adjoint dg-equivalences:

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{A}}^{l}:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{B}}^{r}:\mathcal{Q}_{r}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}={}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\mapsto\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}(\mathcal{N},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}),

with
\mathcal{P}_{l}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lfmod(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{Q})
. The dual (
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}
)-bimodule
\mathcal{D}\Delta
then induces adjoint dg-equivalences between
\mathcal{P}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{l}^{\opp}
with
\mathcal{Q}_{l}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lfmod(\mathcal{B})
:

(6.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{B}}^{l}

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}(-,{}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{D}\Delta)_{\mathcal{A}}):\mathcal{Q}_{l}^{\opp}\to\mathcal{P}_{r}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{A}}^{r}

\displaystyle=\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{r}}(-,{}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{D}\Delta)_{\mathcal{A}}):\mathcal{P}_{r}\to\mathcal{Q}_{l}^{\opp}.

These functors fit into a large diagram as follows:

(6.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}_{l}^{\opp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
,
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{D}\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{A}}^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{B}}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{B}}^{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{A}}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{D}\Delta}}

with canonical (natural) quasi-isomorphisms
r_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{A}}^{l}\circ l_{\mathcal{A}}
and
l_{\mathcal{D}\Delta}\to\mathcal{K}^{r}_{\mathcal{B}}\circ r_{\mathcal{A}}
defined by the Yoneda embedding. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{*}\circ r_{\Delta}=l_{\mathcal{D}\Delta}
by construction.

6.6.
A Postnikov decomposition

This section is based on
[
Sei08
, Section (5i)]
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be any finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category with

\Ob\mathcal{B}:Y_{m}\prec\cdots\prec Y_{2}\prec Y_{1}.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{k,r},1\leq k\leq m
denote the full dg-subcategory of
\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
consisting of finite right
\mathcal{B}
-modules
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathcal{N}(Y_{j})
acyclic for all
1\leq j\leq k
. Then we have canonical projection and inclusion functors:

(6.20)

\mathcal{Q}_{r}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathcal{Q}_{k,r}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathcal{Q}_{r},

where
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is defined by

\mathcal{F}_{k}\mathcal{M}(Y_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{M}(Y_{j})&\text{ if }k+1\leq j\leq m,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Because
\mathcal{B}
is directed, the (counit) natural transformation
\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{F}_{k}\to\Id_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
, which assigns to each
\mathcal{M}
the inclusion homomorphism
\xi^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{F}_{k}\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
, gives a quasi-isomorphism

(6.21)

\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{k,r}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{F}_{k}\mathcal{M})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})

so the functor
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is right adjoint to
\mathcal{I}_{k}
. When it is clear from the context, we shall not distinguish
\mathcal{N}
from its image
\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{N}
. The rest of this section is devoted to the construction of a dg-functor
\mathcal{L}_{k}
:

(6.22)

\mathcal{L}_{k}:\mathcal{Q}_{r}\to\mathcal{Q}_{k,r}

which is left-adjoint to
\mathcal{I}_{k}
. For any
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}
, we construct a closed morphism
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{M}
(the unit of this adjunction) inducing a natural quasi-isomorphism for all
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k,r}
:

(6.23)

\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{k,r}}(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{I}_{k}\mathcal{N}).

For all
k
, there is an evaluation
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism of degree
0
:

t^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}(Y_{k})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{k}^{r}\to\mathcal{M}

such that for every
d\geq 1
and
m\geq j_{d}>j_{d-1}>\cdots>j_{1}>k
,

(t^{k}_{\mathcal{M}})^{d}=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}^{d+1}:\mathcal{M}(Y_{k})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{j_{1}},Y_{k})\otimes\cdots\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{j_{d}},Y_{j_{d-1}})\to\mathcal{M}(Y_{j_{d}})[1-d].

The construction of
\nu_{\mathcal{M}}^{(k)}
is done recursively. Define
\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M}
to be the mapping cone of
t_{\mathcal{M}}^{1}
, i.e., the abstract twist of
\mathcal{M}
along
Y_{1}
in the sense of
[
Sei08
, Section 5]
. The horizontal map
\nu^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}
in the exact triangle (
6.24
) below then gives a quasi-isomorphism

\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{N})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{N})

for any
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{Q}_{1,r}
, since
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathcal{N})
is acyclic by the Yoneda embedding theorem.

(6.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}one(t_{\mathcal{M}}^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}(Y_{1})\otimes\mathcal{Y}^{r}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathcal{M}}}

This construction is clearly functorial in
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. For each
2\leq k\leq M
, define
\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{M}
recursively by the formula

\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{C}one(t^{k}_{\mathcal{L}_{k-1}\mathcal{M}}),

and they fit into a ladder consisting of exact triangles on the left and commutative triangles on the right (see
[
Sei03
, P.76]
):

(6.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{m}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{m-1}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{m-1}\mathcal{M}(Y_{m})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{M}(Y_{3})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{3}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M}(Y_{2})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{2}^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{M}(Y_{1})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{m}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{m-1}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(Y_{m})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(Y_{3})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{3}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(Y_{2})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{2}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}(Y_{1})\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{1}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R)}

The morphism
\nu^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}
is then obtained as a suitable composition of left vertical arrows in (
6.25
)
(L)
. This completes the construction of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
. For any
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}
, there is a similar ladder (
6.25
)
(R)
associated to the decreasing filtration

0=\mathcal{F}_{m}\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{F}_{m-1}\mathcal{N}\to\cdots\to\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N},

where the direction of arrows has been reversed comparing to (
6.25
)(L). In particular, each complex
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is filtered by

(6.26)

0=\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{m}\mathcal{N})\subset\cdots\subset\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{N})\subset\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{N})=\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}).

Theorem 6.12

(
[
Sei03
, Proposition 5.17 & Remark 5.25]
)
.

This filtration induces a spectral sequence converging to
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))
, whose starting term can be identified as

E_{1}^{kj}=\big(H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Y}_{k}^{!}))\otimes H(\mathcal{N}(Y_{k}))\big)^{k+j}.

This spectral sequence can be understood also in terms of the ladder (
6.25
)
(L)
using the adjunction (
6.21
) and (
6.23
). Instead of applying the covariant functor
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},-))
to (
6.25
)
(R)
, one can apply the contravariant functor
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(-,\mathcal{N}))
to (
6.25
)
(L)
to obtain an exact couple using
[
GM03
, P.263]
. These two points of views, as explained by Seidel in
[
Sei03
, Proposition 5.17]
and
[
Sei03
, Remark 5.25]
respectively, are unified by the diagram below:

(6.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{2,r}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{1,r}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{0,r}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{F}_{0}\mathcal{N})}

The horizontal arrows in (
6.27
) are quasi-isomorphisms. Unlike the right column, the left column of (
6.27
) is not given by inclusions of chain complexes. Nevertheless, any chain map can be replaced by its mapping cylinder which is an inclusion, so the left (resp. the middle) column also defines a spectral sequence converging to
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}))
. The diagram (
6.27
) then shows that these spectral sequences are all isomorphic to each other (no matter which column we pick).

By passing to the cohomological category of
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
, the morphism
[\nu^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}]:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{M}
is characterized by the property (
6.23
), and as such is uniquely defined up to isomorphisms in
H(\mathcal{Q}_{r})
. In Section
9
and Section
13
, we shall construct for any tame Landau-Ginzburg model a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{B}
and for any stable thimble or a compact exact graded Lagrangian submanifold a filtered
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathcal{M}
:

0=\mathcal{M}^{(0)}\subset\mathcal{M}^{(1)}\subset\mathcal{M}^{(2)}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{M}^{(m)}=\mathcal{M}

such that the projection morphism
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{(k)}
is isomorphic to
[\nu^{k}_{\mathcal{M}}]
in
H(\mathcal{Q}_{r})
. Then the complex
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is filtered by
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{M}^{(k)},\mathcal{N}),\ 0\leq k\leq m
. In some sense, the geometry is smarter and produces a better right
\mathcal{B}
-module for us.

6.7.
Quotients and localizations

We review the basic theory of the quotients and localizations of
A_{\infty}
-categories due to Lyubashenko-Ovsienko
[
LO06
]
, Lyubashenko-Manzyuk
[
LM08
]
and Drinfeld
[
Dri04
]
, generalizing much earlier work of Verdier
[
Ver96
]
on quotients and localizations of triangulated categories. These techniques are mainly to invert certain morphisms in the
A_{\infty}
-categories and to verify the invariance of the Fukaya-Seidel categories. Since the quotient construction will be applied to only dg categories in this paper, the work of Drinfeld
[
Dri04
, Section 3]
would suffice for our purpose, but we shall mostly follow
[
LO06
, Section 2.6]
in this exposition.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be any dg category, considered as an
A_{\infty}
-category with
\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{d}=0,d\geq 3
, and let
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}
be a full subcategory. The quotient dg category
\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}
has the same set of objects as
\mathcal{B}
with morphism spaces replaced by

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{X_{1},\cdots,X_{d}\in\Ob\mathcal{A},d\geq 0}

\displaystyle\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{d},Y_{1})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{d-1},X_{d})

(6.28)

\displaystyle\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(X_{1},X_{2})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},X_{1})[d],

for any
Y_{0},Y_{1}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
. The
d=0
component is, by convention,
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})
. The first order map
\mu_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}^{1}
is the usual bar differential:

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}^{1}(b_{d+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{d+1}b_{d+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{1}(b_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{n=1}^{d}b_{d+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}(b_{n+1},b_{n})\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1},

and
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}
is defined by contracting the end points (note that
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}=0
for
d\geq 3
):

\mu_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}^{2}(a_{r+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{1},b_{r+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1})=a_{r+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{2}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}(a_{1},b_{r+1})\otimes b_{r}\otimes\cdots\otimes b_{1}.

The quotient category
\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}
comes with a canonical (quotient) dg functor
\pi:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}
which sends
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y_{1})
to the
d=0
component of (
6.7
). The next lemma is proved by considering the length filtration of
\mu_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}^{1}
on (
6.7
).

Lemma 6.13

(
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.13]
)
.

Suppose that
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
is acyclic for all
X\in\mathcal{A}

(
i.e.,
Y
is right orthogonal to
\mathcal{A}
)
, then
\pi^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y_{0},Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}(Y_{0},Y)
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
Y_{0}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
. Similarly, if
\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,X)
is acyclic for any
X\in\mathcal{A}

(
i.e.,
Y
is left orthogonal to
\mathcal{A}
)
, then
\pi^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,Y_{1})\to\hom_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}(Y,Y_{1})
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
Y_{1}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be any finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category, and let
\mathcal{T}
be a collection of closed morphisms in
\mathcal{E}
. Then any
t:Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
in
\mathcal{T}
can be also viewed as an
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism
t\in\hom_{\rfmod(\mathcal{E})}(\mathcal{Y}_{0},\mathcal{Y}_{1})
using the Yoneda embedding functor. Define the localized
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]
to be the image of the composition

(6.29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rfmod(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rfmod(\mathcal{E})/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})
denotes the full subcategory of
\rfmod(\mathcal{E})
whose objects consist of all cones
\mathcal{C}one(t)
of
t\in\mathcal{T}
. In particular, the localized
A_{\infty}
-category comes with a canonical quotient functor
\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]
, denoted also by
\pi
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Suppose that
Y_{0}\prec Y_{1}
in
\Ob\mathcal{A}
and
t:Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
is a closed morphism
\mathcal{T}
. Then there exists another closed morphism
a\in\hom_{\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]}(Y_{1},Y_{0})
such that when passing the cohomological category,
[\mu_{\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]}^{2}(\pi^{1}(t),a)]=[e_{Y_{1}}]
, and
[\mu_{\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]}^{2}(a,\pi^{1}(t))]=[e_{Y_{0}}]
.

If one can verify that
\pi^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{E}}(Y_{j},Y_{j})\to\hom_{\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}]}(Y_{j},Y_{j}),j=0,1
are quasi-isomorphisms using Lemma
6.13
(and so
[e_{Y_{j}}]\neq 0,j=0,1
), then
[a]
is the inverse of
[t]
in
H(\mathcal{E}[\mathcal{T}^{-1}])
.

Proof.

We identify
\mathcal{E}
with its Yoneda image in
\mathcal{Q}=\rfmod(\mathcal{E})
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}one(t)
be the mapping cone of
t:\mathcal{Y}_{0}\to\mathcal{Y}_{1}
. The morphism
a
is defined as

a=\pi_{0}\otimes\iota_{1}\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y}_{0})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{C})[1]\subset\hom_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{0}),

where
\pi_{0}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{Y}_{0}
and
\iota_{1}:\mathcal{Y}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}
are the projection and inclusion homomorphisms defined in (
6.11
). Then
\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(a)=0
by Lemma
6.4
. Moreover,

\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(t,a)=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,\pi_{0})\otimes\iota_{1}\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{Y}_{1})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{C})[1]\subset\hom_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(\mathcal{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1}),

is cohomologous to
e_{Y_{1}}
. Indeed, by Lemma
6.4
again,

\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(\pi_{1}\otimes\iota_{1})=\mu_{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}(\pi_{1})\otimes\iota_{1}+\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\pi_{1},\iota_{1})=\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,\pi_{0})\otimes\iota_{1}+e_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}.

Similarly, one verifies that
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{C}one(\mathcal{T})}(a,t)=\pi_{0}\otimes\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\iota_{1},t)
is cohomologous to
e_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}
.
∎

7.
Meromorphic Quadratic Differentials with Double Poles

To construct the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
without using Lagrangian boundary condition, we shall consider the Floer equation (
5.5
) on Riemann surfaces with planar ends (Section
7.3
)— starting with a suitable metric on a pointed disk, we attach to each boundary component a lower half plane
\mathbb{H}^{-}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{-}_{s}
. The existence of such a metric on pointed disks becomes essential to our construction and is tied closely to the theory of measured foliations and meromorphic quadratic differentials on Riemann surfaces. This section is of expository nature: we review some important results on complex analysis of Riemann surfaces, especially the work of Gupta-Wolf
[
GW17
]
, addressing this existence problem. The family version is then discussed in Section
7.8
.

7.1.
Measured foliations with centers

Let
\overline{S}
be a closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 0
.

Definition 7.1
.

A
measured foliation with centers

(\mathcal{F},\mu)

on
\overline{S}
consists of a set of centers
\Sigma=\{x_{1},\cdots x_{n}\}\subset\overline{S}
, a set of singular points
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{y_{1},\cdots,y_{m}\}
disjoint from
\Sigma
, an open cover
\{U_{j}\}
of
\overline{S}\setminus(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})
, and a collection of non-vanishing closed real-valued 1-form
\{\varphi_{j}\}
, one for each
U_{j}
, satisfying the following properties:

(F1)

\varphi_{j}
=
\pm\varphi_{k}
on
U_{j}\cap U_{k}
;

(F2)

for each
x_{j}\in\Sigma
, there exists a local chart
w_{j}:W_{j}\to\mathbb{C}
and
a_{j}>0
such that
\varphi_{k}=\im(a_{j}w_{j}^{-1}dw_{j})
on
W_{j}\cap U_{k}
;

(F3)

for each
y\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, there exists a local chart
z:V\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and an integer
l\geq 1
such that
\varphi_{k}=\im(z^{l/2}dz)
on
V\cap U_{k}
for some branch of
z^{l/2}
on
V\cap U_{k}
. The number
l+2
is called the order of the singularity
y
. ∎

The kernels
\ker\varphi_{j}
define a smooth line field of
\overline{S}
, which integrates to give a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on the complement
\overline{S}\setminus(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})
. The local model of
\mathcal{F}
near a center or a singular point are determined by
(F2)
and
(F3)
. Within the local chart
W_{j}
, the foliation
\mathcal{F}
consists of radial lines emanating from the center
x_{j}\in\Sigma
. Near a singular point of order
l+2
,
\mathcal{F}
is obtained by gluing
(l+2)
rectangles
[-1,1]_{t}\times[0,b]_{s}
, foliated by the 1-form
ds
, according to the pattern in Figure
12
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic31.png]
\end{overpic}

Figure 12
.
The local structure of
\mathcal{F}
near a center (left) and a singular point of order 5 (right).

The collection of 1-forms in
(F1)
is patched to obtain a continuous map
|\varphi|:T(\overline{S}\setminus\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, which is equal to
|\varphi_{j}|
on
U_{j}
, and defines a transverse invariant measure
\mu
on
\mathcal{F}
: for any arc
A\subset\overline{S}\setminus(\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime})
, let
\mu(A)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{A}|\varphi|
. If
A_{0},A_{1}
are arcs transverse to the foliation
\mathcal{F}
, and if they are isotopic via a family of such arcs with the end points lying on the same leaves, then
\mu(A_{0})=\mu(A_{1})
. For any free homotopy class
[\gamma]
of closed curves in
\overline{S}\setminus\Sigma
, define
\mu([\gamma])=\inf_{\gamma\in[\gamma]}\mu(\gamma)
, where the infimum is taken over all curves
\gamma
in the class
[\gamma]
. If
\gamma_{j}
is any small loop linking the center
x_{j}\in\Sigma
once, then the constant
a_{j}>0
in
(F2)
is equal to the length
\mu([\gamma_{j}])=\mu(\gamma_{j})
divided by
2\pi
and is independent of the choice of the local charts.

A pair of measured foliations with centers are called
equivalent
, if one is isotopic to the other after possibly some Whitehead moves (see Figure
13
). Denote by
\mathcal{MF}_{2}
the space of equivalent classes of measured foliations with centers. If
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is equivalent to
(\mathcal{G},\nu)
, then
\mu([\gamma])=\nu([\gamma])
for any closed
\gamma
. Conversely, the equivalent class of a measured foliation with centers is determined by its valuation on finitely many homotopy classes of curves. In fact, one can prove:

Theorem 7.2

(
[
FLP12
]
[
ALPS16
, Proposition 3.9]
)
.

Let
\overline{S}
be any closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 0
. Then the space
\mathcal{MF}_{2}
of equivalent classes of measured foliations on
\overline{S}
with
n
centers is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3(2g-2+n)}
provided that
2g-2+n>0
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic32.png]
\end{overpic}

Figure 13
.
Whitehead moves.

When
\Sigma
is empty,
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is a measured foliation on
\overline{S}
in the sense of Thurston, and Theorem
7.2
is proved using a pair-of-pants decomposition of
\overline{S}
. By
[
FLP12
, Proposition 6.7]
, a measured foliation on a pair-of-pants is determined (up to isotopy and Whitehead moves) by the transverse measures of three boundary components. To obtain a measured foliation on
\overline{S}
using the cut-and-pasting technique, one has to record one additional twisting number for each gluing curve, giving rise to
(3g-3)\cdot(1+1)
parameters in total. This is known as the Thurston-Dehn parametrization; see
[
FLP12
, Exposé 6]
. The general case for measured foliations with centers is not very different: we remove a central disk in the sink neighborhood
W_{j}
for each center
x_{j}\in\Sigma
and obtain a measured foliation on a bordered surface with
n
boundary components; then we repeat the previous argument. We refer to
[
ALPS16
, Section 3]
for a complete proof of Theorem
7.2
.

7.2.
Meromorphic quadratic differentials with double poles

From now on, let
\overline{S}
be a compact Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 0
, and let
\Sigma=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
be
n
distinct marked points on
\overline{S}
.

Definition 7.3
.

A meromorphic quadratic differential

\phi
on
\overline{S}
is a
(2,0)
-tensor that is locally of the form
h(w)dw\otimes dw
for some meromorphic function
h(w)
. In this paper, we are concerned with meromorphic quadratic differentials with double poles at
\Sigma
.
∎

The local expansion of
\phi
near each
x_{j}\in\Sigma
takes the form

(\frac{a_{j}^{2}}{w^{2}_{j}}+\frac{c_{1}}{w_{j}}+c_{0}+\cdots)dw_{j}\otimes dw_{j},\ a_{j},c_{1},c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}.

The leading coefficient
a_{j}^{2}
, called the complex residue of
\phi
at
x_{j}
, is independent of the choice of the holomorphic coordinate
w_{j}
. In fact, one can normalize further and write

(7.1)

\phi=\frac{a_{j}^{2}}{w_{j}^{2}}dw_{j}\otimes dw_{j}\cdot

for a special choice of
w_{j}
. We always assume that
a_{j}^{2}>0
for all
1\leq j\leq m
and declare
a_{j}
to be the positive square root. By the Riemann-Roch theorem, the space of meromorphic quadratic differentials with fixed complex residues has dimension
3g-3+n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{0}(\overline{S},\mathcal{K}^{\otimes 2}(\Sigma))
, where
\mathcal{K}=\Lambda^{1,0}\overline{S}
is the canonical bundle of
\overline{S}
.

Definition 7.4
.

The
singular-flat metric

g_{\phi}
induced by a meromorphic quadratic differential
\phi
is a singular metric defined by the formula
g_{\phi}(v,v)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}|\phi(v,v)|,v\in T_{y}\overline{S},y\in\overline{S}
. Away from the zero locus
\phi^{-1}(0)
and
\Sigma
, one can locally write
\phi=dw\otimes dw
for some
w
, then
g_{\phi}
is the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{C}_{w}
. The singularities of
g_{\phi}
are at the zeros of
\phi
: a cone-angle of
(l+2)\pi
is associated to any zeros of order
l\geq 1
. The metric
g_{\phi}
is complete near any double pole
x_{j}\in\Sigma
: let
z_{j}=\ln(w_{j})
in the normal form (
7.1
), then
g_{\phi}
is the product metric on
z_{j}\in(-\infty,b]\times S^{1}
multiplied by
a_{j}^{2}
(the circle factor has length
2\pi a_{j}
).
∎

Definition 7.5
.

Any meromorphic quadratic differential
\phi
with double poles at
\Sigma
induces a pair of measured foliations on
\overline{S}\setminus\Sigma
. The horizontal foliation
(\mathcal{F}_{h},\mu_{h})
is defined locally by the 1-form
\im(\pm\sqrt{\phi})
away from
\phi^{-1}(0)
, which extends to a measured foliation with centers in the sense of Definition
7.1
. The vertical foliation
(\mathcal{F}_{v},\mu_{v})
is defined locally by
\re(\pm\sqrt{\phi})
; near each
x_{j}\in\Sigma
,
\mathcal{F}_{v}
consists of concentric circles linking
x_{j}
.
∎

The next theorem explains the relation between measured foliations and quadratic differentials. We are most interested in the case when
g=0
,
n\geq 3
, and when all centers are located on the equator of the Riemann sphere.

Theorem 7.6

(
[
HM79
]
[
GW17
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
\overline{S}
be any compact Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 0
, and let
\Sigma=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
be
n
distinct marked points on
\overline{S}
such that
\chi(\overline{S}\setminus\Sigma)=2-2g-n<0
. Then for any
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
measured foliation on
\overline{S}
with
n
centers, there exists a unique meromorphic quadratic differential
\phi
with double poles at
\Sigma
and whose horizontal foliation is equivalent to
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
.

Idea of Proof.

When
\Sigma=\emptyset
, Theorem
7.6
was due to Hubbard-Masur
[
HM79
]
. Shorter proofs were found later by Kerchhoff
[
Ker80
]
, Gardiner
[
Gar87
]
and Wolf
[
Wol96
]
. The analytic proof by Wolf is generalized further to measured foliations with centers in
[
GW17
]
.

Wolf’s idea is to lift the measured foliation
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
to the universal cover
\tilde{S}
of
\overline{S}
, denoted by
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mu})
. Then the leave space of
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mu})
, which depends only on the equivalent class of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
, is an
\mathbb{R}
-tree
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
. If
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is induced from a holomorphic quadratic differential, then the projection map

\tilde{S}\to\tilde{\mathcal{T}}

is a
\pi_{1}(\overline{S})
-equivariant harmonic map, and
\phi
is equal to the Hopf differential. When
\Sigma=\emptyset
, the existence of this harmonic map follows from the variation principle using an energy minimizing sequence
[
Wol96
]
. When
\Sigma\neq\emptyset
, the energy of such a harmonic map is infinite, so one has to establish uniform energy bounds for harmonic maps defined on a compact exhaustion of the surface. Readers are referred to
[
GW17
]
for the complete argument.
∎

7.3.
Pointed disks and their completions

For any
d\geq 2
, a
(d+1)
-pointed disk

S
is the closed unit disk
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{C}
with finitely many boundary marked points
\Sigma=\Sigma^{-}\sqcup\Sigma^{+}
removed and such that
|\Sigma^{-}|=1
and
|\Sigma^{+}|=d
. The points in
\Sigma^{-}
and
\Sigma^{+}
are called incoming and respectively outgoing points of
S
at infinity and are labeled anti-clockwise along
\partial\mathbb{D}
with
\Sigma^{-}=\{\zeta_{0}\}
and
\Sigma^{+}=\{\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{d}\}
. The connected components of
\partial S=\partial\mathbb{D}\setminus\Sigma
are denoted by
C_{0},\cdots,C_{d}
with each
C_{k}
connecting
\zeta_{k}
and
\zeta_{k+1}
,
0\leq k\leq d
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic36.png]
\put(-10.0,46.0){\small$\zeta_{0}$}
\put(10.0,0.0){\small$\zeta_{1}$}
\put(85.0,0.0){\small$\zeta_{2}$}
\put(105.0,50.0){\small$\zeta_{3}$}
\put(25.0,95.0){\small$\zeta_{4}$}
\put(-2.0,20.0){\small$C_{0}$}
\put(50.0,-10.0){\small$C_{1}$}
\put(100.0,25.0){\small$C_{2}$}
\put(80.0,90.0){\small$C_{3}$}
\put(0.0,80.0){\small$C_{4}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 14
.
A 5-pointed disk.

To construct the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
, we attach planer ends to
S
, one for each
C_{k}
, to form a complete Riemannian surface without boundary. To this end, we use a suitable quadratic differential
\phi
on
S
to specify a metric that is flat near
\partial S
. The existence of such
\phi
is then deduced using Theorem
7.6
.

Definition 7.7
.

A meromorphic quadratic differential
\phi
on
\mathbb{D}
is called
S
-
compatible
, if
\phi
can be extended to a meromorphic quadratic differential
\Phi
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
with double poles at
\Sigma
. Moreover, we require that the complex residue of
\Phi
at each
\zeta_{k}\in\Sigma
is
a_{k}^{2}>0
for some
a_{k}>0
, and that
\Phi
is totally real along
\partial S
. This means that
\Phi(v,v)>0
for any
v\in T_{w}(\partial S),w\in\partial S
. In particular, each
C_{k}\subset\partial S
is a closed leave of the horizontal foliation of
\Phi
and is disjoint from the zero locus
\Phi^{-1}(0)
. By identifying
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{H}^{+}
, one may recover
\Phi
on
\mathbb{H}^{-}
from
\phi
using the reflection principle, i.e., the formula
\Phi(\bar{z})=\bar{\phi}(z)dz\otimes dz,z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}.

∎

For any
S
-compatible quadratic differential
\phi
, the singular-flat metric induced by
\phi
can be smoothed out in a small neighborhood of
\phi^{-1}(0)
using a “singular” conformal transformation to obtain a Riemannian metric on
S
, denoted by
g_{\phi}
. This metric is flat away from this neighborhood of
\phi^{-1}(0)
. Each component
C_{k}
of
\partial S
is totally geodesic, and a collar neighborhood of
C_{k}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\epsilon)_{s},0<\epsilon\ll 1
. We define the completion of
S
by attaching lower half planes
\mathbb{H}^{-}_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}^{-}
, one for each
C_{k}
:

\hat{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S\ \bigcup\ \mathbb{H}^{-}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbb{H}^{-}_{d},

which inherits a complete Riemannian metric from
S
.
\mathbb{H}^{-}_{k}
is called
the planar end
associated to
C_{k}
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic8.png]
\put(-15.0,47.0){$\im\hat{\iota}_{0}$}
\put(95.0,78.0){$\im\hat{\iota}_{3}$}
\put(95.0,47.0){$\im\hat{\iota}_{2}$}
\put(95.0,16.0){$\im\hat{\iota}_{1}$} \put(30.0,0.0){$\mathbb{H}^{-}_{0}$} \put(30.0,90.0){$\mathbb{H}^{-}_{3}$} \put(65.0,63.0){$\mathbb{H}^{-}_{2}$} \put(65.0,30.0){$\mathbb{H}^{-}_{1}$} \put(35.0,20.0){\small$t_{0}^{+}$}
\put(50.0,57.0){\small$t_{1}^{+}$}
\put(51.0,36.0){\small$t_{2}^{+}$}
\put(35.0,70.0){\small$t_{3}^{+}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 15
.
The completion of a 4-pointed disk.

A set of
strip-like ends

\{\iota_{k}\}_{k=0}^{d}
of width
(a_{0},\cdots,a_{d})
consists of proper holomorphic embeddings
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}\to S
, one for each
\zeta_{k}\in\Sigma^{\pm}
, such that

•

\iota_{k}^{-1}(\partial S)=\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times\{0,\pi a_{k}\}
,

•

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\iota_{k}(t,\cdot)=\zeta_{k}
, and

•

the images
V_{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\im\iota_{k}
are pairwise disjoint.

If in addition
\iota_{k}^{*}g_{S}
is the product metric on
\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}
, then we say that
\{\iota_{k}\}_{k=0}^{d}
is
adapted
to the quadratic differential
\phi
, in which case, the embedding
\iota_{k}
is determined uniquely by
\phi
up to a time translation, and
\iota_{k}^{*}\phi=dz\otimes dz
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}^{\pm}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}
. Each
\iota_{k}
extends to an isometric embedding
\hat{\iota}_{k}=\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\hat{S}
called
the plane-like end
at
\zeta_{k}\in\Sigma^{\pm}
. Let
\sigma_{k}:\mathbb{H}^{-}_{k}\to\hat{S}
denote the inclusion map, which are normalized such that

\sigma_{0}^{-1}\circ\iota_{0}(0,0)=(0,0)\text{ and }\sigma_{k}^{-1}\circ\iota_{k}(0,\pi a_{k})=(0,0),\ 1\leq k\leq d.

The length of
g_{\phi}
along
C_{k}
is defined as the constant
t^{+}_{k}=t^{+}_{k}(\phi)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that (see Figure
15
)

(7.2)

\sigma_{d}^{-1}\circ\iota_{0}(0,\pi a_{0})=(t_{d}^{+},0)\text{ and }\sigma_{k-1}^{-1}\circ\iota_{k}(0,0)=(t_{k-1}^{+},0),\ 1\leq k\leq d.

7.4.
Measured foliations on pointed disks

The existence of
S
-compatible quadratic differentials is best understood via measured foliations on pointed disks, which is tied closely to ribbon metric trees.

A ribbon tree

\mathcal{T}
is a tree with finitely many vertices and edges and with a cyclic ordering on the set of edges incident to each vertex. The boundary
\partial\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{T}
, which consists of all exterior vertices of
\mathcal{T}
(i.e. vertices of valence
1
), then comes with a cyclic ordering. In this paper, we assume that a total order
\prec
on
\partial\mathcal{T}
which lifts this cyclic ordering is fixed, and that the valence of each vertex of
\mathcal{T}
is either
1
or
\geq 3
. Any ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}
admits an embedding
\lambda:(\mathcal{T},\partial\mathcal{T})\to(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D}\setminus\{-1\})
so that the cyclic ordering at each vertex is given by the positive orientation of
\mathbb{D}
, and such that
\partial\mathcal{T}
is ordered positively along
\mathbb{D}\setminus\{-1\}\cong[-\pi,\pi]
. In the sequel, we shall always use such an embedding
\lambda
to specify the ribbon structure on
\mathcal{T}
.
A metric ribbon tree
is a ribbon tree with a metric.

Example 7.8
.

There are two types of metric ribbons trees
\mathcal{T}^{d+1}
and
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}
that we shall use frequently in this paper. In
\mathcal{T}^{d+1}
, all exterior vertices are distance
\frac{\pi}{2}
apart from the unique interior vertex, and
|\partial\mathcal{T}^{d+1}|=d+1
. The second metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}
has two interior vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
connected by an edge of length
R
.
|\partial\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}|=d_{1}+d_{2}
. The first
d_{1}
vertices (resp. the last
d_{2}
vertices) in
\partial\mathcal{T}
are attached to
v_{1}
(resp.
v_{2}
) with distance
\frac{\pi}{2}
. See Figure
16
.
∎

\begin{overpic}[Pic33.png]
\put(3.0,10.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{0}$}
\put(63.0,10.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{0}$}
\put(77.0,20.0){$v_{1}$}
\put(77.0,28.0){$v_{2}$}
\put(85.0,23.0){$R$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 16
.
Metric ribbon trees
\mathcal{T}^{5}
and
\mathcal{T}^{5,6}_{R}
.

Example 7.9
.

A more general class of examples will be used in Proposition
8.26
: the metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}}_{R_{1},\cdots,R_{n-1}}
has
n
interior vertices
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
with each
v_{k}
lying on the unique geodesic connecting
v_{1}
and
v_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
and such that
d_{\mathcal{T}}(v_{1},v_{k})=R_{k-1},2\leq k\leq n
with

(7.3)

0\leq R_{1}\leq R_{2}\leq\cdots\leq R_{n-1}.

Moreover, for every
1\leq k\leq n
, there are
d_{k}
exterior vertices attached to
v_{k}
with distance
\frac{\pi}{2}
. The total order on
\partial\mathcal{T}
is suggested as in Figure
17
. We allow some
v_{k}
’s to collide with each other, in which case some equalities are achieved in (
7.3
). For instance, if
R_{k}=0
for all
k
, then
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}}_{R_{1},\cdots,R_{n-1}}=\mathcal{T}^{d_{1}+\cdots+d_{n}}
.
∎

\begin{overpic}[Pic37.png]
\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{0}$}
\put(13.0,20.0){$v_{1}$}
\put(36.0,20.0){$v_{2}$}
\put(51.0,20.0){$v_{3}$}
\put(90.0,20.0){$v_{4}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 17
.
The metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}^{3,1,2,4}_{R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}}
with
n=3
.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\Gamma^{d+1}
denote the space of metric ribbon trees
\mathcal{T}
with
(d+1)
exterior vertices, and
\partial\mathcal{T}
is labeled as
(\mathfrak{o}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{o}_{d})
with respect to the total ordering. To put a metric on
\Gamma^{d+1}
, we declare the embedding

\displaystyle\Gamma^{d+1}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{d(d+1)/2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\mapsto(d_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j},\mathfrak{o}_{k}))_{0\leq j<k\leq d},

to be isometric. In particular,
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
are called
\epsilon
-close, if their images in
\mathbb{R}^{d(d+1)/2}
have distance
<\epsilon
.
∎

Let
S=\mathbb{D}\setminus\Sigma
be any pointed
(d+1)
-disk and
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
a measured foliation on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with centers at
\Sigma
. We always assume that

•

(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is invariant under the anti-holomorphic involution fixing the equator
\partial\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}
, and that

•

\partial S=\partial\mathbb{D}\setminus\Sigma
consists of only regular leaves.

In this case, the equivalent class of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is uniquely determined by a metric Ribbon tree as follows. Let
\mathcal{T}
be the leaf-space of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
when restricted to
S
. By Poincaré Recurrence Theorem
[
FLP12
, Theorem 5.2]
,
\mathcal{F}
consists of only closed leaves. Since
S
is contractible, this implies that
\mathcal{T}
is a ribbon tree. Each boundary component
C_{k}\subset\partial S
is collapsed into an exterior vertex. The total order on
\partial\mathcal{T}
is defined such that
f_{S}(C_{j})\prec f_{S}(C_{k})
if
j<k
. The push-forward of the measure
\mu
then makes
\mathcal{T}
into a metric space (and so a metric Ribbon tree). The leaf-space of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is the metric ribbon graph
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
obtained by gluing two copies of
\mathcal{T}
along
\partial\mathcal{T}
. Then the projection map

(7.4)

f_{S}:(S,\partial S)\to(\mathcal{T},\partial\mathcal{T})

extends to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant map on the double

(7.5)

\overline{f}_{S}:\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\overline{\mathcal{T}}.

\begin{overpic}[Pic34.png]
\put(75.0,20.0){$f_{S}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 18
.
A measured foliation whose leaf-space is isometric to
\mathcal{T}^{3,4}_{R}
.

The transverse measure of an arc connecting
C_{j},C_{k}\subset\partial S
is equal to the distance
d_{\mathcal{T}}(f_{S}(C_{j}),f_{S}(C_{k}))
, so
\mathcal{T}
determines the equivalent class of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
. Conversely, any metric Ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}
with
|\partial\mathcal{T}|=d+1
can be realized as the leaf-space of some
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
on
S
. This follows from a simple induction argument on
|\partial\mathcal{T}|=d+1
, starting with
d=2
. The next lemma follows from Theorem
7.6
by taking
\overline{S}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For any metric Ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}
with
(d+1)
exterior vertices and any
(d+1)
-pointed disk
S
, there exists a unique
S
-compatible quadratic differential
\phi
such that the leaf-space of the horizontal foliation of
\phi
, which we denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\phi}
, is isometric to
\mathcal{T}
.

Remark 7.12
.

Following Wolf’s idea this
S
-compatible quadratic differential is obtained from a harmonic projection map (
7.4
), or equivalently, a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-equivariant harmonic map (
7.5
), as the Hopf differential
\phi=-4(f_{S})_{w}^{2}dw\otimes dw
. Since the topology of
S
is rather simple, there is no need to pass to the universal cover, so Wolf’s argument should be simplified. See
[
Wol96
]
[
GW17
]
for more detail.
∎

Example 7.13

(
[
MP98
, Example 5.1]
)
.

Identify
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{H}^{+}
, and set
d=2
,
\zeta_{0}=0,\zeta_{1}=1
and
\zeta_{2}=\infty
. Then

\phi(z)=b_{0}\big(\frac{dz}{z}\big)^{2}+b_{1}\big(\frac{dz}{1-z}\big)^{2}+b_{2}\big(\frac{dz}{z(1-z)}\big)^{2}

with

b_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(a_{0}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}-a_{1}^{2}),\ b_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}-a_{0}^{2}),\ b_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(a_{0}^{2}+a_{1}^{2}-a_{2}^{2}),

is the unique
S
-compatible quadratic differential with complex residues
a_{k}^{2}
at
\zeta_{k},0\leq k\leq 2
. The zero locus
\phi^{-1}(0)
is disjoint from
\partial\mathbb{H}^{+}\cong\mathbb{R}
if and only if
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})
is subject to the triangle inequalities (and so comes from a metric ribbon tree with three exterior vertices).
∎

7.5.
Families of pointed disks

Our next step is to state the version of Lemma
7.11
when
S
is allowed to vary in a family. Following
[
Sei08
, Section 9]
, let

(7.6)

\mathcal{S}^{d+1}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}

denote the universal family of
(d+1)
-pointed disk, and let
\{\iota_{k}^{d+1}\}_{k=0}^{d}
be a universal choice of strip-like ends of width
(a_{0},\cdots,a_{d})
. Then the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
of
(d+1)
-pointed disk comes with a smooth vector bundle

(7.7)

\mathcal{V}^{d+1}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}

whose fiber
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}_{r}
at
r\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
is the space of
S_{r}
-compatible quadratic differentials on
\mathbb{D}
(where we allow the complex residues to be any real numbers). There are several ways to trivialize the bundle
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}
locally, and we shall use spectral projections. For
r_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
, fix a metric
g_{r_{0}}
on
S_{r_{0}}
such that
(\iota^{d+1}_{k})^{*}g_{r_{0}}
is the product metric on
\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s},0\leq k\leq d
, then pick a local trivialization of (
7.6
) (as a family of smooth surfaces) near
r_{0}
that makes the ends
\{\iota_{k}^{d+1}\}_{k=0}^{d}
constant in the sense of
[
Sei08
, Section (9a)]
. In this way,
g_{r_{0}}
is viewed as a metric on nearby fibers
S_{r}
(though not necessarily compatible with the complex structure). Now consider the truncated surface

S_{r}^{\trun}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S_{r}\setminus\coprod_{k=0}^{d}\iota_{k}^{d+1}(\inte(\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t})\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}),

and define the vertical and horizontal boundary of
S_{r}^{\trun}
as

\displaystyle\partial^{v}S_{r}^{\trun}

\displaystyle=\coprod_{k=0}^{d}\iota_{k}^{d+1}(\{0\}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}),

\displaystyle\partial^{h}S_{r}^{\trun}

\displaystyle=S_{r}^{\trun}\cap\partial S_{r}.

Note that the standard
\bar{\partial}
-operator on
\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,\pi a_{k}]_{s}
takes the form
\partial_{t}+i\partial_{s}
. The operator
i\partial_{s}
acting on the space

L^{2}_{j}([0,\pi a_{k}]_{s},\{0,\pi a_{k}\};\mathbb{C})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{h\in L^{2}_{k}([0,\pi a_{k}]_{s};\mathbb{C}):h(0),h(\pi a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}\},\ j\geq 1,

is
L^{2}
-self-adjoint, with 1-dimensional kernel. Let

\displaystyle\Pi_{k}^{\pm}:L^{2}_{j}([0,\pi a_{k}]_{s},\{0,\pi a_{k}\};\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\to H^{\pm}_{k,j},

\displaystyle\Pi^{0}_{k}:L^{2}_{j}([0,\pi a_{k}]_{s},\{0,\pi a_{k}\};\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R},\ 0\leq k\leq d

denote the spectral projections onto the positive (resp. negative) subspace and the kernel. Then the fiber
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}_{r}
is identified with the kernel of

(7.8)

\mathbb{D}_{r}:\bar{\partial}\oplus(\Pi^{-}\circ\res):L^{2}_{j}(S_{r}^{\trun},\partial^{h}S_{r}^{\trun};E_{r})\to L^{2}_{j-1}(S_{r}^{\trun};\Lambda^{0,1}S_{r}^{\trun}\otimes E_{r})\oplus H^{d+1,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}},\ j\geq 1

with
E_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Lambda^{1,0}S_{r}^{\trun})^{\otimes 2}
. We explain the meaning of (
7.8
) in more detail. The operator

\res:L^{2}_{j}\to L^{2}_{j-1/2}(\partial^{v}S_{r}^{\trun},\partial^{v}S_{r}^{\trun}\cap\partial^{h}S_{r}^{\trun};\mathbb{C}(dz)^{\otimes 2})

is the boundary restriction map, and
\Pi^{-}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Pi^{+}_{0},\Pi^{-}_{1},\cdots,\Pi^{-}_{d})
is the spectral projection onto the subspace

(7.9)

H^{d+1,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H_{0,j-\frac{1}{2}}^{+}\oplus H_{1,j-\frac{1}{2}}^{-}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{d,j-\frac{1}{2}}^{-}.

Moreover, the domain
L^{2}_{j}(S_{r}^{\trun},\partial^{h}S_{r}^{\trun};E_{r})
of (
7.8
) consists of
L^{2}_{j}
-sections which are totally real along the horizontal boundary
\partial^{h}S_{r}^{\trun}
.

As
r
varies in a neighborhood of
r_{0}
, (
7.8
) is a smooth family of surjective Fredholm operators over a fixed domain. Thus
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}_{r}=\ker\mathbb{D}_{r}
can be trivialized locally, and we compute
\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{V}^{d+1}=\Ind_{\mathbb{R}}\bar{\partial}=2d-1
. A smooth section
\phi
of
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}
is tested locally by the following property: for any compactly supported smooth section
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S^{\trun}_{r_{0}};(TS^{\trun}_{r_{0}})^{\otimes 2})
, the pairing

(7.10)

r\mapsto\langle\phi_{r},\psi\rangle

is a smooth function near
r_{0}
(
S^{\trun}_{r}
and
S^{\trun}_{r_{0}}
are identified as smooth surfaces using a local trivialization of (
7.6
)). By the unique continuation property, one may further require that the support of
\psi
to lie in any fixed small ball of
S^{\trun}_{r_{0}}
.

\mathcal{V}^{d+1}
comes with a surjective bundle map

\Res^{d+1}=(\Res_{k}^{d+1})_{k=0}^{d}:\mathcal{V}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{d+1}

which associates a compatible quadratic differential with its complex residue at
\zeta_{k}\in\Sigma
. Equivalently,
\Res_{k}^{d+1}=\Pi_{k}^{0}\circ\res
. Then the family version of Lemma
7.11
states the following:

Lemma 7.14
.

For any
d\geq 2
and any metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}
with
|\partial\mathcal{T}|=d+1
, there exists a continuous section
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
of
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
such that
\phi_{\mathcal{T},r}
is the unique
S_{r}
-compatible quadratic differential such that the leaf space of the horizontal foliation is isometric to
\mathcal{T}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}_{\phi_{\mathcal{T},r}}=\mathcal{T}
. By construction,
\Res^{d+1}(\phi_{\mathcal{T},r})=(a_{0}^{2},\cdots,a_{d}^{2})
for all
r\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
, where
a_{k}=d_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathfrak{o}_{k-1},\mathfrak{o}_{k})/\pi,0\leq k\leq d

(
by convention
\mathfrak{o}_{d+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{o}_{0})
.

Remark 7.15
.

The continuity of
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
follows from a basic property of harmonic maps defined on varying domains and is not stated explicitly in
[
GW17
]
. For holomorphic quadratic differentials on closed Riemann surfaces, this was proved in
[
HM79
, Section 1.2]
. The section
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
might not be smooth when
\phi_{\mathcal{T},r}
has a zero of order
\geq 2
. This can be avoided by requiring each interior vertex of
\mathcal{T}
to have valence
=3
(which is the generic case), so the horizontal foliation of
\phi_{\mathcal{T},r}
must have
d-1
distinct singular leaves. In practice, we shall work with a smooth approximation of
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}
, so the metric ribbon trees in Example
7.8
would suffice for our work.
∎

7.6.
Leafed trees

A
d
-leafed tree is a properly embedded planar tree
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
d+1
semi-infinite edges: one root and
d
leaves. Two
d
-leafed trees are considered same if they are isotopic in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A flag
f=(v,e)
in a graph is a pair consisting of a vertex
v
and an adjacent edge
e
. A finite edge is also called interior (otherwise exterior). Let
\Ve(T)
denote the set of vertices,
\Ed^{\inte}(T)
the set of interior edges, and
\Fl(T)
the set of flags.

Define a partial order on the space of
d
-leafed trees as follows:
T^{\prime}<T
if
T
is obtained from
T^{\prime}
by collapsing some interior edges into vertices, in which case we identify
\Ed^{\inte}(T)
with a subset of
\Ed^{\inte}(T^{\prime})
. The final object of this partial order is the
d
-leafed tree
T_{*}
with no interior edges and with a single vertex.

A
d
-leafed tree
T
is oriented by flowing upwards from the root to the leaves; see Figure
19
. A flag
f=(v,e)
is called positive if
e
is oriented away from
v
, and negative otherwise. For any interior edge
e
, let
f^{\pm}(e)=(v^{\pm}(e),e)
be the positive (resp. the negative) flag attached to
e
, where
v^{+}(e)
(resp.
v^{-}(e)
) denotes the initial point (resp. the end point) of
e
. For any vertex
v
, we label the flags attached to
v
anticlockwise as
f_{0}(v),\cdots,f_{|v|-1}(v)
starting with the unique negative flag
f_{0}(v)
, and
|v|
is the valence of
v
. A
d
-leafed tree
T
is called stable if
|v|\geq 3
for all
v\in\Ve(T)
.

7.7.
Deligne-Mumford-Stasheff compactifications

As a set, the Deligne-Mumford-Stasheff compactification of
\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
is

\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}=\coprod_{T}\mathcal{R}^{T}

where the union is over all stable
d
-leafed trees. Define
\mathcal{R}_{v}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{R}^{|v|}
and
\mathcal{S}_{v}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{S}^{|v|}
,
v\in\Ve(T)
. Then the
T
-stratum
\mathcal{R}^{T}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\prod_{v\in\Ve(T)}\mathcal{R}_{v}
comes with a fiber bundle

\mathcal{S}^{T}\to\mathcal{R}^{T}

which is the product of
\mathcal{S}_{v}\to\mathcal{R}_{v},v\in\Ve(T)
. The gluing construction from
[
Sei08
, Section (9f)]
then makes
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
into a smooth manifold with corners, with
\mathcal{R}^{T_{*}}=\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
as the top dimensional stratum.

Our next goal is to extend
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}
into a smooth vector bundle over
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
. For any
d
-leafed tree
T
and
v\in\Ve(T)
, let
\mathcal{V}_{v}=\mathcal{V}^{|v|}\to\mathcal{R}_{v}
be the bundle of compatible quadratic differentials over
\mathcal{R}_{v}
. For any flag
f=(v,e)
, let
\Res_{f}:\mathcal{V}_{v}\to\mathbb{R}
denote the map taking the complex residue at the marked point at
f
. Define
\mathcal{V}^{T}
to be the kernel of the surjective bundle map over
\mathcal{R}^{T}
:

(7.11)

\displaystyle\Res^{T}:\bigoplus_{v\in\Ve(T)}\mathcal{V}_{v}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{\Ed^{\inte}(T)},

\displaystyle(\phi_{v})_{v\in\Ve(T)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\Res_{f^{+}(e)}(\phi_{v^{+}(e)})-\Res_{f^{-}(e)}(\phi_{v^{-}(e)})\big)_{e\in\Ed^{\inte}(T)}.

Lemma 7.16
.

There exists a smooth vector bundle
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
which restricts to the bundle
\mathcal{V}^{T}\to\mathcal{R}^{T}
over each
T
-stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.16
.

Recall from
[
Sei08
, Section (9f)]
that any universal choice of strip-like ends
\{\iota_{f}\}_{f\in\Fl(T)}
for the family
\mathcal{S}^{T}\to\mathcal{R}^{T}
defines a smooth embedding

(7.12)

\overline{\gamma}^{T}:(-1,0]^{\Ed^{\inte}(T)}\times\mathcal{R}^{T}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}.

which identifies
\{0\}\times\mathcal{R}^{T}
with the
T
-stratum
\mathcal{R}^{T}\subset\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
and defines the smooth structure of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
near
\mathcal{R}^{T}
. For simplicity, we assume the width of
\iota_{f}
is 1 for all
f=(v,e)
, so every
\iota_{f}
is a proper embedding

\iota_{f}:\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,\pi]\times\mathcal{R}_{v}\to\mathcal{S}_{v}.

fibered over
\mathcal{R}_{v}
, which restricts to a strip-like end on each fiber. We must construct local trivializations of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}
near
\mathcal{R}^{T}\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
for all
d
-leafed trees
T
. To ease our notation, we focus on the case for
\codim
-1 strata, i.e., when
|\Ed^{\inte}(T)|=1
and
|\Ve(T)|=2
, and leave the general case to interested readers. Let
e
be the unique interior edge of
T
and
v^{\pm}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}v^{\pm}(e)
.
For each
r_{0}=(r_{v^{+}},r_{v^{-}})\in\mathcal{R}^{T}
, we construct a bundle isomorphism

\mathcal{V}^{T}\to(\overline{\gamma}^{T})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}

defined in a neighborhood of
(0,r_{0})\in(-1,0]\times\mathcal{R}^{T}
, which trivializes
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}
locally. Note first that the truncated surface
S^{\trun}_{r_{*}}
associated to
r_{*}=\overline{\gamma}^{T}(\rho_{e},r_{v^{\pm}})\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1},\rho_{e}\in(-1,0)
is obtained by gluing
S_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}}^{\trun}
with a finite strip along the vertical boundary:

(7.13)

\displaystyle S_{r_{*}}^{\trun}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S_{v^{+},r_{v^{+}}}^{\trun}\coprod\Stp_{e}\coprod S_{v^{-},r_{v^{-}}}^{\trun}/\sim,

\displaystyle\Stp_{e}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[0,-\ln(-\rho_{e})]_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}

where
\iota_{f^{+}(e)}(0,s)
is identified with
(0,s)\in\Stp_{e}
, and
\iota_{f^{-}(e)}(0,s)
with
(-\ln(-\rho_{e}),s)\in\Stp_{e},s\in[0,\pi]
. The pointed disk
S_{r_{*}}
is then obtained by attaching semi-infinite strips to
\partial^{v}S_{r_{*}}^{\trun}
. The fiber
\mathcal{V}_{r_{*}}^{d+1}
can be understood as the kernel of a surjective Fredholm operator (
7.8
) with spectral projections, but we shall exploit the decomposition (
7.13
) this time. Recall that the spectral projections are defined using the
L^{2}
-adjoint operator
i\partial_{s}
:

\Pi^{\pm}\ \text{(resp. $\Pi^{0}$)}:V_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}([0,\pi],\{0,\pi\};\mathbb{C})\to H^{\pm}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\text{(resp. $\mathbb{R}$)}.

Then the fiber
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}_{r_{*}}
is also identified with the kernel of

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{\prime}_{r_{*}}:L^{2}_{j}(S_{v^{+},r_{v^{+}}}^{\trun},\partial^{h}S_{v^{+},r_{v^{+}}}^{\trun};E_{+})

\displaystyle\oplus L^{2}_{j}(S_{v^{-},r_{v^{-}}}^{\trun},\partial^{h}S_{v^{-},r_{v^{-}}}^{\trun};E_{-})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{j-1}(S_{v^{+},r_{v^{+}}}^{\trun};F_{+})\oplus L^{2}_{j-1}(S_{v^{+},r_{v^{+}}}^{\trun};F_{-})\oplus H^{T,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle(\phi_{+},\phi_{-})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\bar{\partial}\phi_{+},\bar{\partial}\phi_{-},\Pi^{-}_{\rho_{e}}(\res(\phi_{+}),\res(\phi_{-}))\big).

where
E_{\pm}=(\Lambda^{1,0}S_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}}^{\trun})^{\otimes 2}
,
F_{\pm}=E_{\pm}\otimes\Lambda^{0,1}S_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}}^{\trun}
,

H^{T,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H^{|v_{+}|,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus H^{|v_{-}|,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\mathbb{R}

and
H^{|v_{\pm}|,-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}
is the negative spectral subspace (
7.9
) associated to
v^{\pm}
.
\Pi^{-}_{\rho_{e}}
is the spectral projection map as in (
7.8
) except at the flags
f_{\pm}(e)
. More precisely, the entry of
\Pi^{-}_{\rho_{e}}
associated to
f_{\pm}(e)
is defined by

\displaystyle V_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus V_{j-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\to V_{j-\frac{1}{2}}=H^{-}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus H^{+}_{j-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\mathbb{R}

(7.14)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sigma^{+}\\
\sigma^{-}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\Pi^{-}&-e^{l_{e}(i\partial_{s})}\Pi^{-}\\
-e^{l_{e}(-i\partial_{s})}\Pi^{+}&\Pi^{+}\\
\Pi^{0}&-\Pi^{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\sigma^{+}\\
\sigma^{-}\end{pmatrix}

where
l_{e}=-\ln(-\rho_{e})
is the length of
\Stp_{e}
.
Since the spectrum of
(i\partial_{s})
on
V_{j-\frac{1}{2}}
is
\mathbb{Z}
, one verifies that the
(1,2)
- and
(2,1)
-entry of (
7.7
) are smooth in the variable
\rho_{e}\in(-1,0]
and vanish when
\rho_{e}=0
. In the latter case,
r_{*}=r_{0}=(r_{v^{+}},r_{v^{-}})
corresponds to the unglued surface, and
\mathbb{D}_{r_{0}}^{\prime}
is the operator
\mathbb{D}_{r_{v^{+}}}\oplus\mathbb{D}_{r_{v^{-}}}
in (
7.8
) combined with the third row of (
7.7
). Recall that the bundle map
\Res^{T}:\mathcal{V}_{v^{+}}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{v^{-}}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\displaystyle(\phi_{v^{+}},\phi_{v^{-}})\mapsto\Pi^{0}\circ\res(\phi_{v^{+}})-\Pi^{0}\circ\res(\phi_{v^{-}})=\Res_{f^{+}(e)}(\phi_{v^{+}})-\Res_{f^{-}(e)}(\phi_{v^{-}}).

Thus
\ker\mathbb{D}_{r_{0}}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{T}_{r_{0}}
are equal by construction. Combined with a trivialization of
\mathcal{V}^{T}
near
r_{0}
, this trivializes
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}
in a neighborhood of
\overline{\gamma}^{T}(0,r_{0})
.

Finally, we have to verify that the smooth structure on
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}
would agree for different trivializations. Similar to (
7.10
), a smooth section
\phi
of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{d+1}
can be tested locally by the following property: for any compactly supported sections
\psi_{\pm}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(S^{\trun}_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}},(TS^{\trun}_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}})^{\otimes 2})
, the pairing

(7.15)

(\rho_{e},r)\in(-1,0]\times\mathcal{R}^{T}\mapsto\langle\phi_{\overline{\gamma}^{T}(\rho_{e},r)},(\psi_{+},\psi_{-})\rangle.

is smooth near
(0,r_{0})
. By the unique continuation property, we may further require the support of
\psi_{\pm}
to lie in any fixed small ball in
S^{\trun}_{v^{\pm},r_{v^{\pm}}}
. This property is independent of the choice of strip-like ends
\{\iota_{f}\}_{f\in\Fl(T)}
(which is used for the construction of
\overline{\gamma}^{T}
). In the same vein, one verifies that the trivializations are consistent also near strata of
\codim\geq 2
. This completes the proof of Lemma
7.16
.
∎

7.8.
Consistent families of quadratic differentials

The complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus T
of a
d
-leafed tree consists of
d+1
connected regions, which we identify as the boundary of a metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}
, labeled anticlockwise by
\mathfrak{o}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{o}_{d}
. By convention, the root of
T
is adjacent to
\mathfrak{o}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{o}_{d}
. If the flag
f
adjacent to
\mathfrak{o}_{j},\mathfrak{o}_{k}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, define the width
\mathfrak{w}(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}d_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j},\mathfrak{o}_{k})/\pi
, where
d_{\mathcal{T}}
is the distance function in
\mathcal{T}
.
For any subset
A\subset\partial\mathcal{T}
and any
v\in\Ve(T)
, denote by
\mathcal{T}_{A}
the unique metric ribbon subtree of
\mathcal{T}
with
\partial\mathcal{T}_{A}=A
, and by
\mathcal{T}_{v}
the subtree of
\mathcal{T}
whose boundary consists of all connected regions adjacent to
v
.

Following Lemma
7.11
, for any
(d+1)
-pointed disk
S
, there is a bijection
\partial S\to\partial\mathcal{T}
as ordered sets, sending the
j
-th component
C_{j}
to
\mathfrak{o}_{j}
. This map is locally constant as
S
varies in a family. Hence, one can think of
\partial\mathcal{T}
as a set of labels for the universal family
\mathcal{S}^{d+1}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
. In the same vein, the family
\mathcal{S}_{v}\to\mathcal{R}_{v}
is labeled by
\partial\mathcal{T}_{v}
.

\begin{overpic}[Pic35.png]
\put(-11.0,59.0){root}

\put(40.0,45.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{1}$}

\put(41.0,70.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{3}$}
\put(5.0,70.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{4}$}

\put(63.0,3.0){$\mathfrak{o}_{0}$}
