.
The image of
\tau
is shaded by green.
d_{1}=3
and
d_{2}=4
.

Definition 9.7
.

Suppose that
S
is labeled by a subset
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
d_{1}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}|
and
d_{2}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{0}|
. Then a smooth embedding
\Xi^{\dagger}:S\to\mathbb{C}
is called
rigid
if

(9.20)

\Xi^{\dagger}\circ\tau(t,s)=-i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta}\cdot t+\gamma^{A}_{R}(s)

for all
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[2\pi,R-2\pi]_{s}
, where
\gamma^{A}_{R}(s)
denotes the characteristic curve associated to the unique incoming end of
S
. A cobordism datum
\mathfrak{b}_{\phi}=(K,\Xi,\delta\kappa,\delta H)
on the completion
\hat{S}
is called
rigid
if

•

\Xi
is induced from some rigid embedding
\Xi^{\dagger}\in\Emb_{K}^{S}
;

•

\tau^{*}(\delta\kappa)=\im(i\epsilon_{*}e^{-i\beta}dt\cdot W)
;

•

\delta H\equiv 0
on the image of
\tau
and
\|\delta H\|_{\infty}<\frac{1}{R}
.

This means that for a rigid cobordism datum the Floer equation (
5.5
) takes the standard form

\partial_{t}P+J\partial_{s}P-\nabla H=0\text{ on }\im\tau.\qed

Remark 9.8
.

Intuitively, the surface
S
is decomposed into three pieces using
\tau
:

(9.21)

S=S^{un}\ \bigcup\ Z_{R}\ \bigcup\ S^{st},

so that
\tau:Z_{R}\to S
is the inclusion map.
S^{un}
(resp.
S^{st}
) is the surface that lies below (resp. above)
Z_{R}
. Each component of (
9.21
) then carries a compatible quadratic differential by restricting
\phi
. Take any
q\in\Crit(W)
. Then we label
S^{un}
by the ordered set
\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}\sqcup\{\Lambda_{q,\pi}\}
and
S^{st}
by
\{\Lambda_{q,2\pi}\}\sqcup\mathbf{Th}_{0}
, where
\Lambda_{q,\pi}
(resp.
\Lambda_{q,2\pi}
) is attached to the boundary component

S^{un}\cap Z_{R}=\tau(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{2\pi\})\ (\text{resp. }S^{st}\cap Z_{R}=\tau(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{R-2\pi\})).

A rigid embedding
\Xi^{\dagger}\in\Emb_{K}^{S}
is then obtained by patching some embedding
\Xi^{\dagger}_{un}\in\Emb_{K}^{S^{un}}
of
S^{un}
,
\Xi^{\dagger}_{st}\in\Emb_{K}^{S^{st}}
of
S^{st}
(up to a translation) and the standard embedding (
9.20
) of
Z_{R}
.
∎

Fix some
R_{0}\geq 7\pi
, and let
\phi
be
S
-compatible and
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R_{0}}
. For any
R\geq R_{0}
, we shall describe a stretching map that transform
(S,\phi)
into a singular flat surface
(S_{R},\phi_{R}^{\circ})
which is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}_{R}^{d_{1},d_{2}}
,

(9.22)

(S,\phi)\mapsto(S_{R},\phi_{R}^{\circ}).

This is done by varying the width of
Z_{R}
in the decomposition (
9.21
) which will potentially change the conformal structure of
S
. Choose a smooth function
\chi:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to[0,1]
such that
\text{supp}\chi\subset[3\pi,R_{0}-3\pi]_{s}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}}\chi(s)ds=1
. For any
R\geq R_{0}
, let
h_{R}:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}_{s}
denote the diffeomorphism such that
\partial_{s}h_{R}(s)=1+\chi(s)\cdot(R-R_{0})
and
h_{R}(s)=s
for
s\leq 3\pi
, then

h_{R}\big([3\pi,R_{0}-3\pi]_{s}\big)=[3\pi,R-3\pi]_{s}.

Consider a diffeomorphism of the form

(9.23)

\displaystyle h_{R}^{*}:Z_{R_{0}}

\displaystyle\to Z_{R}

\displaystyle(t,s)

\displaystyle\mapsto(t,h^{*}_{R,t}(s))

where
h^{*}_{R,t}:[3\pi,R_{0}-3\pi]_{s}\to[3\pi,R-3\pi]_{s},t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
is a family of diffeomorphisms such that

(9.24)

h^{*}_{R,t}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{R}(s)&\text{ if }t\leq 0,\\
h_{R}(s+\xi_{\phi})-\xi_{\phi}&\text{ if }t\geq t_{\phi},\end{array}\right.

and that
\text{supp}(\partial_{s}h^{*}_{R}(s)-1)\subset\inte(Z_{R_{0}})
. The last condition allows us to extend
h_{R}^{*}
to be a diffeomorphism
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R_{0}+\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,R+\pi]_{s}
. The constants
\xi_{\phi}
and
t_{\phi}
are defined as in (
9.19
). As we will explain shortly, the condition (
9.24
) is essential to Lemma
9.9
below, which makes the gluing construction possible when considering the family version of
(
9.22
)
. If
\xi_{\phi}=0
, then one can simply take
h^{*}_{R,t}=h_{R}
for all
t
, so
h^{*}_{R}(t,s)=(t,h_{R}(s))
. In general, a convenient choice of
h^{*}_{R,t}
is to conjugate
h_{R}
by a translation, i.e.,

h^{*}_{R,t}(s)=h_{R}(s+\xi(t))-\xi(t)

for some smooth function
\xi:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Suppose that such a diffeomorphism
h^{*}_{R}:Z_{R_{0}}\to Z_{R}
in (
9.23
) is chosen and that
S
is decomposed as
S^{un}\cup Z_{R_{0}}\cup S^{st}
using
\phi
. Then the
(d+1)
-pointed disk
S_{R}
in (
9.22
) is obtained by replacing
Z_{R_{0}}
by
Z_{R}
:

S_{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S^{un}\ \bigcup\ Z_{R}\ \bigcup\ S^{st}

with the default complex structure on each piece. The quadratic differential
\phi_{R}^{\circ}
is defined by patching
\phi|_{S^{un}}
and
\phi|_{S^{st}}
with the standard
(2,0)
-tensor
dz\otimes dz
on
Z_{R}
. There is a diffeomorphism

(9.25)

h_{R,\phi}^{\circ}:S\to S_{R}

which is identity on
S^{un},S^{st}
and is equal to
h_{R}^{*}
on
Z_{R_{0}}
. Intuitively, the map
h^{\circ}_{R,\phi}
is stretching the width of the infinite strip
Z_{R_{0}}
and is clearly not conformal; see Figure
32
. In particular,
(h_{R,\phi}^{\circ})^{*}(\phi_{R}^{\circ})\neq\phi
. The next two lemmas are immediate from our construction.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
\{\iota_{k}\}_{k=0}^{d}
be the set of strip-like ends adapted to
\phi
on
S
which was used to define the embedding
\tau:Z_{R_{0}}\to S
in (
9.18
). Then there exists a set of strip-like ends
\{\iota_{k}^{R}\}_{k=0}^{d}
on
S_{R}
adapted to
\phi_{R}^{\circ}
such that the following diagrams commute: for
k=0,d_{1}
,

(9.26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,R_{0}+\pi]_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{\pm}_{t}\times[0,R+\pi]_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{R},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\times h_{R}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\circ}_{R,\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}^{R}}

and for all
k\neq 0,d_{1}
,

(9.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{R}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\circ}_{R,\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k}^{R}}

In particular, we have
t_{k}^{+}(\phi_{R}^{\circ})=t_{k}^{+}(\phi)
for all
R\geq R_{0}
and
0\leq k\leq d
, where
t_{k}^{+}
is the length of the singular flat metric defined by (
7.2
). Finally, for all
0\leq j\leq d_{1}-1
and
d_{1}\leq k\leq d
, we have

d_{\mathcal{T}_{\phi}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j},\mathfrak{o}_{k})=d_{\mathcal{T}_{\phi_{R}}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j},\mathfrak{o}_{k})+(R-R_{0}),

while
d_{\mathcal{T}_{\phi}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j^{\prime}},\mathfrak{o}_{k^{\prime}})=d_{\mathcal{T}_{\phi_{R}^{\circ}}}(\mathfrak{o}_{j^{\prime}},\mathfrak{o}_{k^{\prime}})
for any other pairs
(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
. Hence,
\phi_{R}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}
.

\put(76.0,14.3){\small$Z_{R}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 32
.
The stretching map
h^{\circ}_{R,\phi}:S\to S_{R}
.

Lemma 9.10
.

Suppose that
S
is labeled by a subset
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
d_{1}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}|\geq 1
,
d_{2}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{0}|\geq 1
. Then for any rigid cobordism datum
\mathfrak{b}_{\phi}=(K,\Xi,\delta\kappa,\delta H)
in the sense of Definition
9.7
, one can construct a rigid cobordism datum
\mathfrak{b}_{\phi_{R}^{\circ}}=(K,\Xi_{R},\delta\kappa_{R},\delta H_{R})
such that
(h_{R,\phi}^{\circ})^{*}\delta\kappa_{R}=\delta\kappa
on
S^{un}\sqcup S^{st}
and

\Xi_{R}^{\dagger}\circ h^{\circ}_{R,\phi}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Xi^{\dagger}(z)&\text{ if }z\in S^{un},\\
\Xi^{\dagger}(z)+(R-R_{0})&\text{ if }z\in S^{st}.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, on
Z_{R}\subset S_{R}
,
(\Xi^{\dagger}_{R},\delta\kappa_{R})
is specified by Definition
9.7
; cf. the equation (
9.8
) and Remark
9.8
. However, the perturbation 1-form
\delta H_{R}
is not necessarily related to
\delta H
.

9.4.
The family version

Let us now consider the family version of Lemma
9.9
and
9.10
. For each
d\geq 2
, write
\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{\base}
for another copy of
\mathcal{R}^{d+1}
, which comes with smooth bundles
\mathcal{S}^{d+1}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{\base}
and
\mathcal{V}^{d+1}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{\base}
. Let
\phi^{\base}=(\phi_{T}^{\base})
be a
(\epsilon,\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}})
-consistent section of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{2m}_{\base}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{2m}_{\base}
equipped with a set of strip-like ends
(\iota_{T}^{\base})
adapted to
\phi^{\base}
. This means that for any subset
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
|A|\geq 3
, there is

•

a smooth section
\psi_{A}^{\base}
of
\mathcal{V}^{|A|}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
which is
\epsilon
-close to the subtree
\mathcal{T}_{R_{0}}^{d_{1},d_{2}}
, where
d_{1}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}|
and
d_{2}=|A\cap\mathbf{Th}_{0}|
;

•

a set of strip-like ends
(\iota_{A,k}^{\base})_{k=0}^{|A|-1}
of
\mathcal{S}^{|A|}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
adapted to
\psi_{A}^{\base}
;

and they satisfy the conditions in Lemma
7.18
. When
A\subset\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}
or
\mathbf{Th}_{0}
, we require that
\psi_{A}^{\base}
is the restriction of
\phi_{*}
in the definition of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in (
9.1
), and so are the strip-like ends.

Now for any subset
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
|A|\geq 3
and
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 1
, the constants in (
9.19
) define smooth functions:

r\in\mathcal{R}_{\base}^{|A|}\mapsto(\xi_{\psi_{A,r}^{\base}},t_{\psi_{A,r}^{\base}}),

with
|\xi_{\psi_{A,r}^{\base}}|<\epsilon\ll 1
. For any
R\geq R_{0}
, by choosing a smooth family of diffeomorphisms (
9.23
)

(9.28)

Z_{R_{0}}\times\mathcal{R}_{\base}^{|A|}\to Z_{R}

parametrized by
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\base}^{|A|}
, we obtain smooth maps

g^{\circ}_{R,A}:\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{|A|}\text{ and }\psi^{\circ}_{R,A}:\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}\to\mathcal{V}^{|A|},

which are pointwise defined by (
9.22
).
\psi^{\circ}_{R,A}
is a smooth section of
(g^{\circ}_{R,A})^{*}\mathcal{V}^{|A|}
. By Lemma
9.9
,
\psi^{\circ}_{R,A}
is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}
. Moreover, there is a smooth bundle map

h_{R,A}^{\circ}:\mathcal{S}^{|A|}_{\base}\to(g^{\circ}_{R,A})^{*}\mathcal{S}^{|A|}

which is defined fiberwise by (
9.25
), along with a set of
\psi^{\circ}_{R,A}
-adapted strip-like ends
(\iota^{\circ}_{R,A,k})_{k=0}^{|A|-1}
for the family
(g^{\circ}_{R,A})^{*}\mathcal{S}^{d+1}
such that the diagrams (
9.26
) and (
9.27
) commute fiberwise.

Finally, if
A\subset\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}
or
\mathbf{Th}_{0}
, we simply set
g^{\circ}_{R,A}=\Id
and
\psi^{\circ}_{R,A}=\psi^{\base}_{A}
. The next lemma then follows from the same line of arguments as in Lemma
7.18
and Lemma
8.13
, and the proof is omitted. Note that the commutativity of (
8.32
) follows from (
9.26
) and (
9.27
).

Lemma 9.11
.

By choosing the family of diffeomorphisms (
9.28
) inductively for any
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
, one can construct an
(\epsilon,\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R})
-consistent family
\phi^{\circ}_{R}
of quadratic differentials
(
in the sense of Definition
8.28
)
parametrized by a smooth degree one map
g^{\circ}_{R}:\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{2m}_{\base}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{2m}
such that for any subset
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
|A|\geq 3
, the component
(\psi_{R,A}^{\circ},g^{\circ}_{R,A})
of
(\phi_{R}^{\circ},g^{\circ}_{R})
associated to
A
is given by the construction above.
g^{\circ}_{R}
is called the stretching map.

Remark 9.12
.

If
\xi_{\psi_{A,r}^{\base}}\equiv 0
for all
A
and
r\in\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
, then one can simply take (
9.28
) to be a constant family of diffeomorphisms by taking
h_{R}^{*}(t,s)=(t,h_{R}(s))
at each fiber . However, author did not figure out a simple criterion to make this possible.
∎

Remark 9.13
.

Since the reference section
\phi^{\base}
is only
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}}
, the smooth map
g^{\circ}_{R}
might not be injective in general. If
\phi^{\base}
is the section
\phi_{\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}}}
in Lemma
7.14
, then one can verify that
g^{\circ}_{R}
is bijective, and
\phi^{\circ}_{R}
is the pull-back of
\phi_{\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R}}
. This heuristic motivates the stretching construction. As mentioned in Remark
7.15
, the section
\phi_{\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}}}
might not be smooth in general, so we have taken a detour to make this idea work.
∎

Lemma 9.14
.

For any
R_{0}\geq 7\pi
, any
(\epsilon,\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}})
-consistent section
\phi^{\base}=(\phi^{\base}_{T})
of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{2m}_{\base}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{2m}_{\base}
and a set of adapted strip-like ends
(\iota^{\base}_{T})
, one can find a consistent family
(\mathfrak{b}_{A}^{\base})_{A\subset\mathbf{Th}}
of cobordism data such that for all
A
and
r\in\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{A,r}^{\base}
is rigid in the sense of Definition
9.7
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same line of arguments as in Lemma
8.13
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

We shall use the rigid cobordism data obtained in Lemma
9.14
to define the directed
A_{\infty}
-category

\mathcal{E}_{R_{0}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th};\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R_{0}},\phi^{\base},(\mathfrak{b}_{A}^{\base})_{A\subset\mathbf{Th}})

and denote the associated
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule by
\Delta_{R_{0}}
. In principle, one may increase the value of
R_{0}
in Lemma
9.14
to define
\Delta_{R}
for all
R\geq R_{0}
, but the lower bound
C_{\mathbf{Th}}
in Lemma
8.16
may also change accordingly, as the choice of the cobordism data could be pretty random. However, this problem is remedied by Lemma
9.10
.

Lemma 9.15
.

Suppose that
R_{0}\geq 7\pi
has been fixed, and a consistent family
(\mathfrak{b}^{\base}_{A})_{A\subset\mathbf{Th}}
of rigid cobordism data has been chosen as in Lemma
9.14
. Then for any
R\geq R_{0}
, one can construct a consistent family of rigid cobordism data
(\mathfrak{b}_{R,A})_{A\subset\mathbf{Th}}
for the pair
(\phi^{\circ}_{R},g^{\circ}_{R})
using Lemma
9.10
. This means that for any
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
|A|\geq 3,d_{1},d_{2}\geq 1
, and any
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\base}^{|A|}
,

\mathfrak{b}_{R,A,r}=(K_{A},\Xi_{R,A,r},\delta\kappa_{R,A,r},\delta H_{R,A,r})

is obtained from
\mathfrak{b}^{\base}_{A,r}=(K_{A},\Xi^{\base}_{A,r},\delta\kappa^{\base}_{A,r},\delta H_{A,r}^{\base})
using Lemma
9.10
, and
\mathfrak{b}_{R,A,r}=\mathfrak{b}^{\base}_{A,r}
are set equal if
A\subset\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}
or
A\subset\mathbf{Th}_{0}
. In particular, the constant
K_{A}
is independent of
R\geq R_{0}
, and the phase pair
(\Xi_{R,A,r},\delta\kappa_{R,A,r})
is determined canonically by
(\Xi^{\base}_{A,r},\delta\kappa^{\base}_{A,r})
. Then the perturbation 1-forms
\delta H_{R,A,r}
are chosen generically to make moduli spaces regular.

Following (
8.34
), for any
R\geq R_{0}
, we construct the finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category

\mathcal{E}_{R}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th};\mathcal{T}^{m,m}_{R},\phi^{\circ}_{R},(\mathfrak{b}_{R,A})),\ \mathbf{Th}=\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}\sqcup\mathbf{Th}_{0}.

using the cobordism data
(\mathfrak{b}_{R,A})
obtained from Lemma
9.15
. This
A_{\infty}
-category satisfies the following important property.

Lemma 9.16
.

For any
A\subset\mathbf{Th}
with
|A|\geq 3
, there exists a constant
C_{A}>0
with the following property. For any
R\geq R_{0}
, if the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}}(\{p_{k}\}_{k=0}^{|A|-1};\mathfrak{b}_{R,A})

is nonempty, then we have an energy inequality:

(9.29)

\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(p_{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{|A|-1}\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(p_{k})\geq-C_{A}.

Here the parameter space
\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
is labeled by
A
, and
\mathfrak{a}_{k},p_{k},0\leq k\leq|A|-1
denote the Floer datum and respectively the soliton associated to each strip-like end of
\mathcal{S}^{|A|}_{\base}\to\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
.

Proof of Lemma
9.16
.

It suffices to verify that the estimate (
9.29
) holds if the moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{S_{g^{\circ}_{R,A}(s)}}(\{p_{k}\}_{k=0}^{|A|-1};\mathfrak{b}_{R,A,r})

is nonempty for some
r\in\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
. For any
r
in a fixed compact subset of
\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}
, this lower bound is provided by (
8.24
), because the constant
K_{A}
and the length
t^{+}_{k}(\psi_{R,A,r}^{\circ})=t^{+}_{k}(\psi^{\base}_{A,r}),0\leq k\leq|A|-1
are independent of
R\geq R_{0}
by our construction. See Lemma
9.9
and
9.10
. If
r
is close to a lower stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}^{|A|}_{\base}
, one may repeat the proof of Lemma
8.16
using the commutative diagram (
8.32
). We leave details to the readers.
∎

If
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 1
, then the
d_{1}
-th outgoing end
\iota_{A,d_{1}}
is distinguished: for any
k\neq d_{1}
, the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{k}}
and the soliton
p_{k}
are independent of
R
. Hence by (
9.29
)

\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(p_{0})-\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{d_{1}}}(p_{d_{1}})\geq-C_{A}^{\prime}

for a constant
C_{A}^{\prime}>0
independent of
R
, if the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{R}^{|A|}_{\base}}(\{p_{k}\}_{k=0}^{|A|-1};\mathfrak{b}_{R,A})
is non-empty. On the other hand,
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{d_{1}}}(p_{d_{1}})
and
\mathcal{A}_{W,\mathfrak{a}_{0}}(p_{0})
will blow up linearly as
R\to\infty
, and by Lemma
9.5
, the leading coefficients depend the filtration level to which
p_{0},p_{d_{1}}
belong. Thus we have proved

Corollary 9.17
.

Let
\Delta_{R}
denote the
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule induced by
\mathcal{E}_{R}
. Then for all
R\gg R_{0}
, the relation (
9.17
) holds for
\Delta_{R}
, and therefore
\Delta^{(l)}_{R}
is a submodule of
\Delta_{R}
for all
0\leq l\leq m
.

10.
Koszul Duality: Proof of Theorem
8.3

In this section, we prove Theorem
8.3
assuming some properties about the pairs-of-pants cobordism map, namely, Lemma
10.3
and
10.4
. These properties will be proved later in Section
11
using the vertical gluing theorem. Recall from Example
8.2
that a thimble
\Lambda_{q,\theta},\ q\in\Crit(W)
is called
stable
if
\theta\in(0,\theta_{\star})
and respectively
unstable
if
\theta\in(\pi,\pi+\theta_{\star})
. Let
\mathbf{Th}_{st}
(resp.
\mathbf{Th}_{un}
) be any admissible set of stable (resp. unstable) thimbles, and set
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{st})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{un})
. Then the construction from Section
9
can be generalized to define a filtered
(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}})
-bimodule
\widetilde{\Delta}_{R}
for any
R\gg\pi
. The critical points of
W
associated to
\mathbf{Th}_{st}
or
\mathbf{Th}_{un}
might not be mutually distinct. This bimodule
\widetilde{\Delta}_{R}
potentially depends on many auxiliary data, but its existence is already useful for the proof of Theorem
8.3
.

10.1.
Some reductions

Recall that the diagonal
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\Delta
defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor
r_{\Delta}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{k}
denote the image of
S_{k}
under
r_{\Delta}
. By Remark
8.4
, it remains to show that for all
k_{1}<k_{2}
, the chain map

(10.1)

(r_{\Delta})^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{k_{1}},S_{k_{2}})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}},\mathcal{S}_{k_{2}})

is a quasi-isomorphism, and by Lemma
6.3
and Proposition
8.31
, we may verify (
10.1
) for one convenient choice of
(\phi^{\circ},g^{\circ})
and
(\mathfrak{b}_{A})
. We shall use the construction from Section
9
. Fix some
R\gg\pi
such that Corollary
9.17
holds, so the diagonal bimodule
\Delta=\Delta_{R}
carries an energy filtration:

0\subset\Delta^{(0)}\subset\Delta^{(1)}\subset\Delta^{(2)}\subset\cdots\subset\Delta^{(m)}=\Delta,

Hence each
\mathcal{S}_{k},1\leq k\leq m
is filtered by a sequence of finite right
\mathcal{B}
-modules, denoted by

0\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(0)}\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(1)}\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(2)}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(m)}=\mathcal{S}_{k}.

By Lemma
9.3
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}(U_{j})=0
if
j<k
, and for
j\geq k
,

0=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(0)}(U_{j})=\cdots=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(k-1)}(U_{j})\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(k)}(U_{j})\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(j)}(U_{j})=\cdots=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(m)}(U_{j}).

Since the
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{B}
is directed, this means that the thimbles
U_{j},1\leq j\leq k_{1}-1
are not involved in the definition of
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}},\mathcal{S}_{k_{2}})
. Thus one may focus on the last
(m-k_{1}+1)
critical points of
W
and carry out the proof instead for the
A_{\infty}
-subcategories
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
with

\Ob\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\sqcup\Ob\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}:U_{m}\prec\cdots\prec U_{k_{1}}\prec S_{k_{1}}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m},

and
\Delta
restricts to the filtered
(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}})
-bimodule
\widetilde{\Delta}
. With this in mind, we shall assume from now on that
k_{1}=1
and
k_{2}=k>1
. The general case is then addressed using this reduction.

10.2.
Associated Graded Modules

Write
\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(l)}/\mathcal{S}^{(l-1)}_{k},\ 0\leq l\leq m
for the associated graded submodule of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
. Using the filtration on
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, we obtain a ladder consisting of exact triangles on the right:

(10.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{1},S_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(m)},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{m}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(m-1)},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{m-1}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(1)},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{1}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(0)},\mathcal{S}_{k}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r_{\Delta})^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}

To show that the top horizontal arrow
(r_{\Delta})^{1}
is a quasi-isomorphism in (
10.2
), we have to understand how
H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}^{(l)}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))
changes at each step. The next lemma is crucial to this proof.

Lemma 10.1
.

For any
1\leq n\leq l\leq m
, the right
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{n}
is quasi-isomorphic to
D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{U}_{l}
, where
D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})
denotes the dual complex of
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})
and
\mathcal{U}_{l}
is the Yoneda image of
U_{l}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
.

By taking
n=1
, Lemma
10.1
implies that

\displaystyle H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))\cong H(D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{1},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{U}_{l},\mathcal{S}_{k})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{H(\mathcal{A})}(S_{1},S_{k})\otimes H(\mathcal{S}_{k}(U_{l}))

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Hom_{H(\mathcal{A})}(S_{1},S_{k})&\text{ if }l=k,\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{array}\right.

so in the ladder (
10.2
), we have

\displaystyle H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}^{(k-1)}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))\cong\cdots\cong H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}^{(0)}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))

\displaystyle\cong 0,

(10.5)

\displaystyle H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}^{(m)}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))\cong\cdots\cong H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}))

\displaystyle\cong H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k})).

We have to verify the
k
-th row of the ladder (
10.2
) is a quasi-isomorphism:

(10.6)

\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{1},S_{k})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(k)},\mathcal{S}_{k})\xleftarrow{\cong}\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k}),

which fits into a diagram

(10.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{1},S_{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(k)},\mathcal{S}_{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{k})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The right vertical arrow of (
10.7
) is induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(k)}\to\mathcal{S}_{k}
. Replacing
\mathcal{S}_{k}
by
\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}
in (
10.2
) with
l=k
shows that this arrow is an isomorphism. The bottom arrow of (
10.7
) is an isomorphism by (
10.2
).

For any
1\leq l\leq m
, the associated graded bimodule
\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta=\Delta^{(l)}/\Delta^{(l-1)}
defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor
r_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. In particular, for any
1\leq n\leq l
, there is a chain map

(10.8)

(r_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta})^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\to hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}),

which defines the top horizontal arrow in (
10.7
).

Lemma 10.2
.

For any
1\leq n\leq l\leq m
, (
10.8
) is quasi-isomorphism.

The diagram (
10.7
) is commutative: any cycle
a
in
\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{1},S_{k})
gives an
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism
\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}
which is composed to give

\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\text{quotient}}\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{1}\xrightarrow{(r_{\mathcal{G}^{k}\Delta})^{1}(a)}\mathcal{G}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{\text{inclusion}}\mathcal{S}_{k}.

This should be compared with

\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{1}\xrightarrow{\text{inclusion}}\mathcal{S}_{1}\xrightarrow{(r_{\Delta})^{1}(a)}\mathcal{S}_{k}

under the left vertical arrow of (
10.7
). They are equal because
(r_{\Delta})^{1}(a)
preserves the filtration and sends
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(l)}
to
\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(l)}
for all
0\leq l\leq m
; especially we take
l=k
. Lemma
10.2
implies that the top horizontal arrow in (
10.7
) is an isomorphism, and so is the left vertical arrow. Thus to complete the proof of Theorem
8.3
, it remains to verify Lemma
10.1
and
10.2
, which dominates the rest of Section
10
.

10.3.
Proof of Lemma
10.1
: Step 1

Note that for any
1\leq n\leq l\leq m
the chain map (
10.8
) can dualized to obtain an
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism

(10.9)

r_{n}:\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{n}\to D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}.

More concretely,
r_{n}
is defined by the formulae

\displaystyle(r_{n})^{d}:\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{n}(U_{j_{d}-1})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j_{d-2}},U_{j_{d-1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j_{0}},U_{j_{1}})

\displaystyle\to D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}(U_{j_{0}})

\displaystyle(x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{1})

\displaystyle\mapsto\mu_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta}^{1|1|d-1}(\ \cdot\ ,x,b_{d-1},\cdots,b_{1})

for every
d\geq 1
and
m\geq j_{0}>j_{1}>\cdots>j_{d-1}\geq 1
. When
n=l
, the strict unitality of
\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta
implies that
(r_{l})^{d}=0
when
d\geq 2
and
(r_{l})^{1}
is an isomorphism between vector spaces. The next lemma shows that (
10.9
) is also a quasi-isomorphism when
n<l
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For any
R\gg\pi
,
1\leq j\leq m
and
1\leq n<l
, the chain map

\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{n})\xrightarrow{x\mapsto\mu_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta}^{1|1|0}(\cdot,x)}D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{l})

induced by the pair-of-pants cobordism is a quasi-isomorphism, and therefore
r_{n}
is a quasi-isomorphism between right
\mathcal{B}
-modules.

\begin{overpic}[Pic48.png]
\put(103.0,67.0){\small$S_{2}$}
\put(4.0,-4.0){\small$U_{4}$}
\put(-1.0,-1.0){\small$U_{3}$}
\put(-4.0,4.0){\small$U_{2}$}
\put(-5.0,9.0){\small$U_{1}$}
\put(55.0,55.0){\small$H(q_{4})$}
\put(55.0,45.0){\small$H(q_{3})$}
\put(55.0,28.0){\small$H(q_{2})$}
\put(55.0,18.0){\small$H(q_{1})$}
\put(-5.0,27.0){\small$V_{2}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 33
.
V_{2}
is a geometric representative of
\mathcal{U}_{2}
(with
l=2
).

10.4.
Proof of Lemma
10.1
: Step 2

It remains to verify that
\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}_{l}
. To this end, we have to enlarge the
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{B}
slightly. Let
x_{l}
denote the
l
-th critical point of
W
. Now consider the set of unstable thimbles

\mathbf{Th}_{un}=(U_{m},\cdots,U_{2},U_{1},V_{l})

with
V_{l}=\Lambda_{x_{l},\eta_{l}^{\prime}}
and
\eta_{l}^{\prime}\in(0,\eta_{1})
. See Figure
33
. Let
\mathbf{Th}_{st}=(S_{l})
, and define

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{st})\text{ and }\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{un}).

Then the union
\mathbf{Th}_{un}\sqcup\mathbf{Th}_{st}
is ordered as

U_{m}\prec\cdots\prec U_{2}\prec U_{1}\prec V_{l}\prec S_{l}.

Following Section
9
, one can construct a directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
inducing a filtered
(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}})
-bimodule
\widetilde{\Delta}
. This point of view is not so useful as
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
consists of a single object and is somewhat degenerate, and we will think of this differently this time: let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
denote the
A_{\infty}
-subcategory of
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
with
\Ob\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=(V_{l},S_{l})
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{\widetilde{\Delta}}
defines an
(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{B})
-bimodule
\widehat{\Delta}
together with an
A_{\infty}
-functor

r_{\widehat{\Delta}}:\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B}).

Let
\mathcal{V}_{l}
denote the image of
V_{l}
under
r_{\widehat{\Delta}}
. Unlike the case for stable thimbles,
\mathcal{V}_{l}
does not carry a filtration, whereas the unique morphism space
\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(V_{l},S_{l})=\widehat{\Delta}(V_{l},S_{l})
of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is filtered by the action functional:

(10.10)

0=\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(0)}(V_{l},S_{l})\subset\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(1)}(V_{l},S_{l})\subset\cdots\subset\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(m)}(V_{l},S_{l})=\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(V_{l},S_{l}).

This filtration is preserved by the chain map

(r_{\widehat{\Delta}})^{1}:\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(n)}(V_{l},S_{l})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{l},\mathcal{S}_{l}^{(n)}),\ 0\leq n\leq m,

therefore giving rise to chain maps between the associated graded complexes/modules:

(r_{\widehat{\Delta}})^{1}:\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(V_{l},S_{l})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(n)}(V_{l},S_{l})/\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{(n-1)}(V_{l},S_{l})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{l},\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{l}),\ 0\leq n\leq m

denoted also by
(r_{\widehat{\Delta}})^{1}
. Note that the filtration (
10.10
) is rather boring, since by Lemma
9.3
,
\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(V_{l},S_{l})=0
if
n\neq l
and is generated by the quasi-unit
e_{x_{l}}
if
n=l
by Corollary
9.4
. We focus on this special filtration level
n=l
in the next lemma.

Lemma 10.4
.

For any
1\leq j\leq m
, consider the product map

\mu^{1|1|0}_{\widehat{\Delta}}:\mathcal{G}^{l}\hom_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}(V_{l},S_{l})\otimes\widehat{\Delta}(U_{j},V_{l})\to\mathcal{G}^{l}\widehat{\Delta}(U_{j},S_{l}).

Then
\mu^{1|1|0}(e_{x_{l}},\cdot):\widehat{\Delta}(U_{j},V_{l})\to\widehat{\Delta}(U_{j},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l})
is a quasi-isomorphism, and therefore
(r_{\widehat{\Delta}})^{1}(e_{x_{l}})\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{l},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

10.5.
Proof of Lemma
10.1
: Step 3

As the final step, we verify that
\mathcal{V}_{l}
is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}_{l}
. In some senses,
V_{l}
is the geometric representative of the Yoneda image
\mathcal{U}_{l}
. At this point, there is no energy filtration involved, and the
A_{\infty}
-category
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is an invariant of
\mathbf{Th}_{un}
. Consider the Yoneda embedding functor of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
composed with the restriction functor to the subcategory
\mathcal{B}
:

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\xrightarrow{r_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}}\rfmod(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow{\text{restriction}}\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B}).

This defines a chain map

r_{l}:\hom_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(U_{l},V_{l})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{U}_{l},\mathcal{V}_{l}).

By Lemma
5.10
,
\hom_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(U_{l},V_{l})
is generated by the quasi-unit
e_{x_{l}}
. Note that for any
1\leq j\leq m
, the multiplication map

[\mu_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}(e_{x_{l}},\cdot)]:H(\hom_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(U_{j},U_{l}))\to H(\hom_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(U_{j},V_{l}))

is always an isomorphism. For
j<l
, this follows from Proposition
8.18
. For
j=l
, this follows from strict unitality. For
j>l
, the domain is trivial by definition, while
H(\hom_{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(U_{j},V_{l}))
is trivial by Lemma
5.9
. This implies that
r_{l}(e_{x_{l}})\in\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{U}_{l},\mathcal{V}_{l})
is quasi-isomorphism. Thus we have completed the proof of Lemma
10.1
assuming Lemma
10.3
and
10.4
.

10.6.
Proof of Lemma
10.2

Consider the commutative diagram:

(10.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(D\hom_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{n},S_{l})\otimes\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta})^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\mapsto a\otimes e_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\cdot,r_{n})}

By Lemma
10.1
, the
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism
r_{n}
defined by (
10.9
) is a quasi-isomorphism, and so is the right vertical arrow in (
10.11
) by Lemma
6.8
. The left vertical arrow in (
10.11
) is defined by the formula
a\mapsto a\otimes e_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}}
and is also a quasi-isomorphism. Indeed, by Lemma
10.1
,

H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l},\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}))\cong H(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{U}_{l},\mathcal{U}_{l}))\cong H(\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{l},U_{l}))\cong\mathbb{K}

is generated by the identity homomorphism
e_{\mathcal{G}^{l}\mathcal{S}_{l}}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
10.2
.

Remark 10.5
.

The proof of Theorem
8.3
relies essentially only on a few formal properties of the filtered bimodule
\Delta=\Delta_{R}
, namely, Lemma
9.3
, Lemma
10.3
and Lemma
10.4
, whereas the explicit construction of
\Delta_{R}
is somewhat irrelevant. In particular, Lemma
10.3
and
10.4
are statements about the cohomological categories. We have taken a detour in Section
9
to show that the operations
\mu^{r|1|s}_{\Delta_{R}},r,s\geq 0
preserve the geometric filtration on
\Delta_{R}(U_{j},S_{k})
. Readers should feel free to propose a more direct and less technical route as long as these properties can be verified.
∎

11.
The Vertical Gluing Theorem

11.1.
The idea of the vertical gluing theorem

To finish the proof of Theorem
8.3
, we prove Lemma
10.3
,
10.2
and Proposition
8.18
(on quasi-units) in this section using a vertical gluing theorem. To illustrate, we focus on the case of Lemma
10.3
and first explain the basic idea. Consider the
A_{\infty}
-subcategory
\mathcal{E}_{0}
of
\mathcal{E}_{R}=\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{R}}
with
\Ob\mathcal{E}_{0}=(U_{j},S_{n},S_{l}),1\leq k<n\leq m
. One may apply the rotational operation
\mathcal{R}
in Remark
8.6
to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and obtain a new
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{1}
with
\Ob\mathcal{E}_{1}=(S_{n},S_{l},U_{j}^{\prime})
,
U_{j}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda_{x_{j},\eta_{j}-2\pi}
.
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is modeled on the metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}^{2,1}_{R}
. Unwinding the definition, Lemma
10.3
is equivalent to the statement that

(11.1)

\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}:\mathcal{G}^{l}\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}(S_{l},U_{j}^{\prime})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}(S_{n},S_{l})\to\mathcal{G}^{l}\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}(S_{n},U_{j}^{\prime}).

is a quasi-isomorphism for all
R\gg\pi
. The roles between stable and unstable thimbles are symmetric here. To ease our notation, we shall verify this instead for the original
A_{\infty}
-category. By rotating Figure
30
by
180^{\circ}
, it is clear that (
11.1
) is equivalent to the following lemma.

Lemma 11.1
.

For any triple of objects
U_{j}\prec U_{l}\prec S_{k}
in
\mathcal{E}_{R}=\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{R}}
and
R\gg\pi
, the map

(11.2)

\mu^{0|1|1}_{\Delta_{R}}:\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta_{R}(U_{l},S_{k})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j},U_{l})\to\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta_{R}(U_{j},S_{k}).

is a quasi-isomorphism. This lemma is non-trivial only when
k\leq l\leq j
; see Figure
34
.

.
The thimbles
U_{\star}
and
S_{\star}
.

Lemma
11.1
is a property about the cohomological category, which depends only on a single
(2+1)
-pointed disk. In this case, the choice of the perturbation 1-forms
\delta H^{R}
can be more flexible: the norms in (
9.10
) may not tend to zero as
R\to\infty
; it suffices to require that the left hand side of (
9.10
) is bounded by a fixed constant
\epsilon^{\prime}
. If
R
is chosen sufficiently large depending on this
\epsilon^{\prime}
, the continuation method can be used to show that
(
11.2
)
is independent of these perturbations up to chain homotopy (however, the phase pairs are remained fixed in this process). Lemma
11.1
is then proved by choosing some special
\delta H^{R}
so that the complex
\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{k})
can be described rather concretely. To set the stage, consider the thimbles

U_{\star}=\Lambda_{x_{l},\pi}\text{ and }S_{\star}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda_{x_{l},2\pi}.

Choose admissible Floer data
\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un}=(\pi,\alpha_{j}^{un},\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H_{j}^{st})
and
\mathfrak{a}^{st}_{k}=(\pi,\alpha_{k}^{st},\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H_{k}^{st})
, one for each pair
(U_{j},U_{\star})
and
(S_{\star},S_{k})
, where
\alpha_{j}^{un}(s),\alpha_{k}^{st}(s)
are given as in (
9.7
) and

\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*}
as in (
9.9
). The perturbation 1-forms
\delta H_{j}^{st},\delta H_{k}^{un}
are supported on
[0,\pi]_{s}
as usual. Then these Floer data can be concatenated to give a Floer datum
\alpha_{jk}^{R}=(R+\pi,\alpha_{jk}^{R},\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H^{R}_{jk})
for the pair
(U_{j},S_{k})
with

(11.3)

\delta H^{R}_{jk}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\delta H_{k}^{st}&\text{ if }s\in[\pi,R+\pi]_{s},\\
\delta H_{j}^{un}&\text{ if }s\in[0,\pi]_{s},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Under these assumptions, Lemma
9.1
can be refined as follows.

Lemma 11.2
.

For all
k\leq l\leq j
and
R\gg\pi
, there is a gluing bijection

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})\times\mathfrak{C}(U_{j},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un})

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{C}^{l}(U_{j},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}^{R}_{jk})

\displaystyle(p^{st}_{k},p^{un}_{j})

\displaystyle\mapsto p^{st}_{k}\circ_{R}p^{un}_{j}

such that
p^{st}_{k}\circ_{R}p^{un}_{j}\to(p^{st}_{k},p^{un}_{j})
in the sense of Lemma
9.1
as
R\to\infty
and
\gr(p^{st}_{k}\circ_{R}p^{un}_{j})=\gr(p^{st}_{k})+\gr(p^{un}_{j})
. Thus there is an isomorphism between graded vector spaces

(11.4)

\widehat{\Phi}_{jk}:\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{j},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un})\to\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{k}).

.
Gluing Floer differentials.

Theorem 11.3

(The Vertical Gluing Theorem I)
.

The map
\widehat{\Phi}_{jk}
is also an isomorphism between complexes. This means that for any solitons
p_{k,\pm}^{st}\in\mathfrak{C}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})
and
p_{j,\pm}^{un}\in\mathfrak{C}(U_{j},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un})
, there is a gluing bijection between finite sets

(11.5)

\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{k,-}^{st}\circ_{R}p_{j,-}^{un},p_{k,+}^{st}\circ_{R}p_{j,+}^{un};\mathfrak{a}_{jk}^{R})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{k,-}^{st},p_{k,+}^{st})&\text{ if }p_{j,-}^{un}=p_{j,+}^{un},\\
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(p_{j,-}^{un},p_{j,+}^{un})&\text{ if }p_{k,+}^{st}=p_{k,+}^{st},\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise }\end{array}\right.

where
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}(\cdots;\mathfrak{a}_{jk}^{R})
is the moduli space contributing to the differential map (
2.14
) for the associated graded complex
\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{k})
(
so the expected dimension of
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}=\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{R}

=0)
.

Theorem
11.3
says that not only
\alpha
-solitons can be glued vertically in the direction of
s
but so are
\alpha
-instantons. This is possible because the drop of the action functional along such an
\alpha
-instanton does not increase as
R\to\infty
, so we have uniform energy control. This implies that any
\alpha^{R}_{jk}
-instanton must decay exponentially towards the critical point
x_{l}\in\Crit(W)
as
z\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[\pi,R]_{s}
approaches the middle line
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{\frac{R+\pi}{2}\}
, and this convergence is uniform in the time variable; see Lemma
11.6
below. Thus one may establish a compactness theorem and compare the moduli space for a finite large
R
with that of
``R=\infty"
, i.e., when the neck is completely stretched.

Now we explain the case for cobordism maps. The
(2+1)
-pointed disk
S
carries a unique
S
-compatible quadratical differential
\phi
with
\mathcal{T}_{\phi}=\mathcal{T}^{2,1}_{R}
. In terms of the decomposition
S=S^{st}\cup Z_{R}\cup S^{un}
in Figure
31
, we label
S^{st}\cong\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,2\pi]_{s}
by
(S_{\star},S_{k})
and the
(2+1)
-pointed disk
S^{un}
by
(U_{j},U_{l},U_{\star})
. When restricted on
S^{un}
, the horizontal foliation of
\phi|_{S^{un}}
is modeled on
\mathcal{T}^{2,1}_{\pi}
. Fix an admissible continuation datum
\mathfrak{c}
on
S^{st}
and a cobordism datum
\mathfrak{b}
on
S^{un}
to define the maps

\displaystyle\Cont:

\displaystyle\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})\to\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st}),

\displaystyle\mu^{2}:

\displaystyle\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{l},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{l}^{un})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j},U_{l})\to\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{j},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un}).

One may simply take
\mathfrak{c}
be the identity cobordism, so
\Cont=\Id
; but this is unnecessary for the next gluing theorem. Concatenate
\mathfrak{c}
and
\mathfrak{b}
to obtain a rigid cobordism datum on
(S,\phi)
, denoted by
\mathfrak{c}\circ_{R}\mathfrak{b}
. The next theorem says that any moduli space contributing to (
11.2
) is also obtained by a gluing construction when
R\gg\pi
.

Theorem 11.4

(The Vertical Gluing Theorem II)
.

There is a gluing bijection between finite sets:

\mathcal{M}(p_{k,0}^{st}\circ_{R}p_{j,0}^{un},\ p_{1},\ p_{k,2}^{st}\circ_{R}p_{l,2}^{un}:\mathfrak{c}\circ_{R}\mathfrak{b})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{M}(p_{k,0}^{st},p_{k,2}^{st};\mathfrak{c})\times\mathcal{M}(p_{j,0}^{un},p_{1},p_{l,2}^{un};\mathfrak{b})

where
\mathcal{M}(\cdots;\mathfrak{c}\circ_{R}\mathfrak{b})

(
resp.
\mathcal{M}(\cdots;\mathfrak{c})
and
\mathcal{M}(\cdots;\mathfrak{b}))
is any moduli space contributing to the map
\mu_{\Delta}^{0|1|1}

(
resp. to
\Cont
and
\mu^{2}
)
; see Figure
36
below. Thus the chain map (
11.2
) fits into a commutative diagram

(11.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{l},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{l}^{un})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j},U_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{k}^{st})\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{j},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{j}^{un})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{l},S_{k})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{B}}(U_{j},U_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{l}\Delta(U_{j},S_{k}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cont\otimes\mu^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}_{lk}\otimes\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}_{jk}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{0|1|1}_{\Delta}}

Theorem
11.4
implies
[\mu_{\Delta}^{0|1|1}]=[\Cont]\otimes[\mu^{2}]
. Since
[\Cont]=\Id
, and
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(U_{l},U_{\star})=H(\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(U_{l},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{l}^{un}))
is generated by the quasi-unit
e_{x_{l}}
at
x_{l}
,
[\mu^{2}]
is an isomorphism by Proposition
8.18
, and so is
[\mu_{\Delta}^{0|1|1}]
. This proves Lemma
11.1
and so Lemma
10.3
.

Lemma
10.4
is in fact the special case of Lemma
11.1
with
j=l
. Finally, Proposition
8.18
(the property about quasi-units) is proved by another application of the vertical gluing theorem and is reduced the easier case, Lemma
8.20
, which has been verified directly.

The proof of Theorem
11.4
is almost identical to that of Theorem
11.3
and is omitted in this paper. In fact, Theorem
11.4
is slightly simpler, since we are gluing moduli spaces of index
0
, in which case the equation does not carry a translation symmetry.

The rest of this section is therefore devoted to the technical proof of Theorem
11.3
. Readers who feel comfortable with this heuristic may proceed directly to the next part.
