.
The dashed curves are the projection of some
\alpha_{12}^{R}
-solitons. There are two filtration levels now.

Suppose that this critical value is associated to
q_{3}\in\Crit(W)
. Choose an angle
\theta_{3}
such that

\theta_{-1}-\pi<\theta_{2}<\theta_{3}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{-1}

and let
\Lambda_{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda_{q_{3},\theta_{3}}
. Figure
39
is then replaced by Figure
41
above. One can also choose a function
\alpha_{3}:\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for some
0<\delta\ll 1
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}(s)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta_{-1}-\pi&\text{if }s\geq\pi-\delta,\\
\theta_{3}-\pi&\text{if }s\leq\delta,\end{array}\right.

to define a filtered complex
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})
for
R\gg\pi
(replace
\Lambda_{0}
by
\Lambda_{3}
throughout the construction of Section
11.6
). In this case, there are two filtration levels for
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})
:

\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})=\mathcal{G}^{0}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})\oplus\mathcal{G}^{1}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})

where
\mathcal{G}^{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}^{1}
) is associated to
q_{3}
(resp.
q
). The summand
\mathcal{G}^{0}
has higher energy level. The geometric filtration on
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{02}^{R})
then gives a short sequence sequence of chain complexes:

(11.37)

0\to\mathcal{G}^{0}\to\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{02}^{R})\to\mathcal{G}^{1}\to 0.

Meanwhile,

\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})=\mathcal{G}^{1}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{02}^{R})\text{ and }\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})=\mathcal{G}^{0}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R}).

The chain map induced by the
(2+1)
-disk preserves the filtration, so we arrive at a diagram

(11.38)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{0}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{3};\mathfrak{a}_{13})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{0}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{1}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{32}^{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{1}\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\mathfrak{a}_{12}^{R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}(\cdot,e_{q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}(\cdot,e_{q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the right column is given by (
11.37
). As in the case of (
11.36
), the bottom horizontal arrow of (
11.38
) is a chain isomorphism, whereas the top arrow is only a quasi-isomorphism by the vertical gluing theorem and Proposition
8.18
. Nevertheless, this proves that the left column forms an exact triangle and hence recovers a special case of Seidel’s long exact sequence
[
Sei03
]
.

Proposition 11.17
.

Suppose we are in the scenario of Figure
41
, then there is a long exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2})\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(e_{q},\cdot)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}

Thus the group
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2})\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{3})
measures the failure of
m_{*}(e_{q},\cdot)
being an isomorphism.

Remark 11.18
.

The fact that the composition
m_{*}(e_{q},m_{*}(\cdot,\cdot)):\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{3},\Lambda_{2})\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{3})\to\HFF_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{2})
is trivial be also seen as follows: since
m_{*}
is associative, this map factorizes through the group
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{3})
, which is trivial, since
\Lambda_{0}\cap\Lambda_{3}=\emptyset
; see Figure
41
.
∎

Part IV
Lagrangian Submanifolds

Having verified the Koszul duality pattern between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, our next step is to explore its implication for a pair of
(X,Y)
of Lagrangian submanifolds. Section
12
is devoted to a generalization of
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
where one entry is replaced by an exact graded Lagrangian submanifold, which allows us to define the
A_{\infty}
-modules
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
and
Y_{\mathcal{B}}
. In Section
13
, we prove our main result Theorem
1.9
by verifying the geometric filtration on the Floer complex of
(X,Y)
is isomorphic to the algebraic filtration of Seidel
[
Sei08
, Corollary 18.27]
. This proof will follow the same argument as in Theorem
8.3
.

12.
Floer Cohomology for Lagrangian Submanifolds

12.1.
The setup

Our proof of the compactness theorem so far is based on the energy method; in this regard, it is not realistic to work with arbitrary
non-compact
Lagrangian submanifolds. We have to make some mild conditions on the projection
W(X),W(Y)\subset\mathbb{C}
in order to make this energy method work, which are spelled out as follows.

Assumption 12.1
.

In this paper, we shall only consider Lagrangian submanifolds
X
of
M
satisfying the following properties:

(
L1
)

X
is exact, i.e., there exists a smooth function
h_{X}:X\to\mathbb{R}
such that
dh_{X}=\lambda_{M}|_{X}
;

(
L2
)

X
is graded, i.e., the Maslov class
\mu_{X}\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
vanishes, and a grading
(X,\xi_{X}^{\#})
is already fixed.

(
L3
)

there exist some
\beta_{X}\in\mathbb{R}
and
C_{X}>0
such that

(12.1)

\re(e^{-i\beta_{X}}W)|_{X}<C_{X},

i.e., the projection
W(X)\subset\mathbb{C}
is bounded above in the direction of
e^{i\beta_{X}}
. If
W(X)
is compact, then the angle
e^{i\beta_{X}}
in (
12.1
) can be arbitrary. But we may also see the case when
e^{i\beta_{X}}
is unique. We assume that a choice of such
\beta_{X}\in\mathbb{R}
has been chosen, once and for all, for this
X
. Unlike the case of thimbles, the grading of
X
is not related to
\beta_{X}
.

(
L4
)

X
has bounded geometry as a totally real submanifold of
(M,J_{M},g_{M},\omega_{M})
. ∎

Let
\Lambda_{q,\theta},q\in\Crit(W)
be any thimble satisfying (
2.8
) and with

(12.2)

e^{i(\theta-\beta_{X})}>0.

Under this assumption, one can construct the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})
following the scheme in Section
2
. In this case, a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=(R,\alpha_{X}(s),\beta_{X},\epsilon_{X},\delta H)
consists of a constant
R\geq\pi
, a real-valued function
\alpha_{X}:\mathbb{R}^{+}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
, an angle
\beta_{X}\in\mathbb{R}
as in

(

L3

)

,
\epsilon_{X}>0
, and a perturbation 1-form
\delta H=\delta H_{s}ds\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+};\mathcal{H})
, which satisfy the following properties:

•

for some
0<\delta\ll 1
,
\alpha_{X}(s)=\theta
for all
s\geq R-\delta
;

•

\re(e^{i(\alpha_{X}(s)-\beta_{X})})\geq\epsilon_{X}
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{s}
(this condition requires (
12.2
));

•

\delta H
is supported on
[0,R]_{s}
and satisfies (
2.7
).

In practice, one may simply take
\alpha_{X}(s)\equiv\theta
to be constant, but it is harmless to make the setup a bit more flexible. Let
p_{\model}:\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}\to M
be a reference map such that
p_{\model}(s)\equiv q
when
s\geq R
. Consider the path space

\mathcal{P}_{k}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{p\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+};M):p(0)\in X,\ p\text{ has finite $L^{2}_{k}$-distance with }p_{\model}\},k\geq 1.

Then any Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
defines an action functional
\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}
on
\mathcal{P}_{k}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})
by the formula:

(12.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}(p)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-h_{X}(p(0))+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}}-p^{*}\lambda_{M}+\delta H_{s}(p(s))ds

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}}\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha_{X}}(W\circ p(s)))-\chi_{[R,+\infty)}\im(e^{-i\theta}W(q))\big)ds

whose gradient vector at
p\in\mathcal{P}_{k}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})
is an
L^{2}
-section of
p^{*}TM
:

\grad\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}(p)=J\partial_{s}p(s)+\nabla\im(e^{-i\alpha_{X}(s)}W)\circ p(s)+\nabla(\delta H_{s})\in L^{2}\cap C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+},p^{*}TM).

\begin{overpic}[Pic51.png]
\put(45.0,31.0){\small$S_{4}$}
\put(45.0,34.0){\small$S_{3}$}
\put(45.0,37.0){\small$S_{2}$}
\put(45.0,40.0){\small$S_{1}$}
\put(-10.0,22.0){\small$H(x_{4})$}
\put(-10.0,16.0){\small$H(x_{3})$}
\put(-10.0,9.0){\small$H(x_{2})$}
\put(-10.0,3.0){\small$H(x_{1})$}
\put(35.0,5.0){\small$X$}
\put(48.0,10.0){\small$e^{i\beta_{X}}$}
\put(60.0,35.0){\small$\re\langle w,e^{i\beta_{X}}\rangle=C_{X}$}
\put(95.0,40.0){\small$l_{q,\theta_{0}}$}
\put(102.0,24.0){\small$l_{q,\theta_{1}}$}
\put(66.0,6.0){\small$W(q)$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 42
.
The left
\mathcal{A}
-module
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
(left) and quasi-units (right).

Let
\mathfrak{C}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
denote the space of critical points of
\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}
, also called
\alpha_{X}
-solitons (this is an abuse of notations, because they also rely on the perturbation 1-form
\delta H
). The Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
is defined by counting the
\alpha_{X}
-instantons on the upper half plane
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}
with a boundary condition along
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{0\}
.

(12.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}P:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}&\to M,\\
\partial_{t}P+J(\partial_{s}P+\nabla\re(e^{-i\alpha_{X}(s)}W))+\nabla(\delta H)_{s}&=0,\\
\lim_{s\to\infty}P(t,s)&=q,\\
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}(P,s)&=p_{\pm}(s),\\
P(t,0)&\in X.\end{array}\right.

with
p_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{C}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
. The boundary condition should be interpreted as in (
2.13
). A solution of (
12.4
) is formally a downward gradient flowline of
\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}
.

12.2.
Compactness

We explain quickly the necessary adaption in order to prove the compactness theorem in this context. Let
\gamma_{X}:\mathbb{R}^{+}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
,
\partial_{s}\gamma_{X}(s)=-e^{i\alpha_{X}(s)}
be a characteristic curve of
\alpha_{X}(s)
. To drive the energy estimate, consider the phase function
\Xi

\Xi(s,t)=-i\epsilon_{X}e^{i\beta_{X}}\cdot t+\gamma_{X}(s),\ (t,s)\in\mathbb{H}^{+}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}

and set
\delta\kappa=\im(i\epsilon_{X}e^{-i\beta_{X}}dt\cdot W)
. For any
\alpha_{X}
-instanton
P
, apply the energy estimate of Lemma
5.2
first to the rectangle
[t_{0},t_{1}]_{t}\times[0,R_{1}]_{s}
,
R_{1}\gg R
and then let
R_{1}\to\infty
to obtain that

(12.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}\big(P(t_{0},\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}\big(P(t_{1},\cdot)\big)+\text{ some terms involving }\delta H

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{X}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\re\big(e^{-i\beta_{X}}\big(W(p(t,0))-W(q)\big)\big)\geq\epsilon_{X}^{\prime}\mathcal{E}_{an}(P;[t_{0},t_{1}]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}^{+}).

for a constant
\epsilon_{X}^{\prime}>0
. This is the analogue of Lemma
2.8
in the presence of a Lagrangian boundary condition. The condition (
12.1
) is used here to upper bound
\re(e^{-i\beta_{X}}W(p(t,0)))
on the left hand side of (
12.5
). At this point, we need a boundary version of Lemma
3.1
. For future reference, we record this as follows.

Lemma 12.2

(Local Compactness II: the Boundary Case)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{H}^{+}
denote any bounded open subset and
C>0
. Suppose that
\xi:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
is any smooth function such that
|\xi(z)|<C
, and
H^{t}_{z},H^{s}_{z}:M\to\mathbb{R},\ z\in\Omega
are families of Hamiltonian functions on
M
with

\|\nabla H^{s}_{z}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)},\|\nabla H^{t}_{z}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}<C

for all
z\in\Omega
. Then any sequence of solutions
P_{n}:\Omega\to M
to the Floer equation

(12.6)

(\partial_{t}P_{n}-J\nabla H_{z}^{t})+J(\partial_{s}P_{n}-\nabla\re(\overline{\xi(z)}W)-J\nabla H_{z}^{s})=0

subject to the boundary condition
P(\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{0\})\subset X
and the energy bound

(12.7)

\mathcal{E}_{an}(P_{n};\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla P_{n}|^{2}+|\nabla H\circ P_{n}|^{2}<C,

has a subsequence that converges in
C^{\infty}_{loc}
-topology on
\Omega
.

This lemma is deduced again using a diameter estimate for “almost”
J
-holomorphic curves in an almost Hermitian manifold with boundary lying in a totally real submanifold.

Proposition 12.3
.

Suppose that
(M,J_{M},g_{M},\omega_{M})
is an almost Hermitian manifold with bounded geometry
(
\omega_{M}
is not necessarily closed
)
, and
X\subset M
is a totally real submanifold with
2\dim_{\mathbb{R}}X=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M
. Denote by
\bar{\partial}_{J}
the operator
\partial_{t}+J\partial_{s}
and by
B^{+}(0,R)=B(0,R)\cap\mathbb{H}^{+},R>0
the upper half disk. If
X
also has bounded geometry, then for any
r>2
, there exists a function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
C>0
and for any map
P:B^{+}(0,1)\to M
if

\int_{B^{+}(0,1)}|\nabla P|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}\leq C\text{ and }P(t,0)\in X\text{ for all }t\in(-1,1)_{t},

then
\diam(P(B^{+}(0,\frac{1}{2})))\leq\varphi(C)
.

The proof of this proposition is by repeating the argument in Appendix
A
and using the reflection principle; the complete argument is omitted in this paper. With this diameter estimate in hand, one can deduce the compactness theorem of (
12.4
), both the local and global versions, following the argument in Section
3
. Hence the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
is well-defined if the perturbation 1-form
\delta H
is chosen generically, in which case we say that
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
is admissible. Finally, the grading function
\gr
introduced in Section
4.4
is generalized rather trivially to this case, so
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
is canonically
\mathbb{Z}
-graded.

12.3.
Continuation Maps

Given any pair of admissible Floer data

\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{\pm}=(R^{\pm},\alpha_{X}^{\pm},\beta_{X},\epsilon_{X}^{\pm},\delta H^{\pm}),

the continuation method in Section
5.2
shows that
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{\pm})
are canonically isomorphic, but there is a caveat about the proof: the energy inequality (
5.18
) is replaced in this case by

(12.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}^{-}_{X}}(P(0,\cdot))-\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}^{+}_{X}}(P(K,\cdot))+\text{ some terms involving }\delta H

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{K}\im(W(P(t,0))\cdot\overline{\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)})dt-\int_{0}^{K}\im(W(q)\cdot\overline{\partial_{t}g_{1}(t)})dt>\epsilon_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}\int_{[0,K]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}}u.

Thus for the embedding
\Xi^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\pi]_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
considered in Section
5.2
(again we have assumed that
R^{\pm}=\pi
), we have to insist that
\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)
is always in the direction of
-ie^{i\beta_{X}}
, i.e.,

(12.9)

g_{0}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{\dagger}(t,0)=-ie^{i\beta_{X}}\cdot h(t)+\gamma^{-}(0),

for a real-valued function
h:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\partial_{t}h(t)\equiv\epsilon_{X}^{-}
for
t\leq 0
and
\equiv\epsilon_{X}^{+}
for
t\gg 0
. Otherwise, we can not estimate the term

\int_{0}^{K}\im(W(P(t,0))\cdot\overline{\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)})dt

on the left hand side of (
12.8
) using (
12.1
). It is for the same reason that
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
is defined only when the condition (
12.2
) holds. This limitation may be get rid of using more sophisticated analytic tools in the future, but in that case the invariance of this Floer cohomology can not be verified using the continuation method. From now on we shall use
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})
to denote the isomorphism class of
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})
for any admissible
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
.

12.4.
Stable- and Unstable-type Lagrangian submanifolds

We say that a Lagrangian submanifold
X
is of
unstable-type
(resp.
stable-type
) if
\re(e^{i\beta_{X}})>0
(resp.
<0
), in which case we shall always assume that
\beta_{X}\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
(resp.
\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2})
). Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
denote the Fukaya-Seidel category of
(M,W)
and its dual category respectively. If
X
is of unstable-type, then we require further that
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{0})
is defined using an admissible set of thimbles
\mathbf{Th}_{0}=(S_{1},\cdots,S_{m})\in\Gamma_{0},\ S_{k}=\Lambda_{x_{k},\theta_{k}},1\leq k\leq m
such that

(12.10)

0<\theta_{m}<\cdots<\theta_{2}<\theta_{1}<\min\{\theta_{\star},\beta_{X}+\frac{\pi}{2}\}.

Under this condition the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,S_{k})
is well-defined for all
S_{k}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
. Choose admissible Floer data
\mathfrak{a}_{X,k}
, one for each pair
(X,S_{k})
. Then there is a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{X}
with

\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}:X\prec S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m},

containing
\mathcal{A}
as a full
A_{\infty}
-subcategory and with

\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{k})=\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(X,S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{X,k}),

which is the Floer complex associated to
(X,S_{k};\mathfrak{a}_{X,k})
for all
k
. The construction of
\mathcal{E}_{X}
is identical to the one in Section
8
: we shall use
\mathcal{T}^{m+1}
as the metric ribbon tree; if a
(d+1)
-pointed disk
S
is equipped with a compatible quadratic differential
\phi
and labeled by
(X,S_{k_{1}},\cdots,S_{k_{d}})
, then we remove the lower half plane
\mathbb{H}^{-}_{0}
from
\hat{S}
and consider the partial completion:

\hat{S}_{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hat{S}\setminus\sigma_{0}(\mathbb{H}_{0}^{-})=S\ \bigcup\ \mathbb{H}^{-}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbb{H}^{-}_{d}.

To specify the phase pair on
\hat{S}_{X}
, pretend that
X
is a thimble
\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}}
with
\theta_{0}=\pi
and use
\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}}\sqcup\mathbf{Th}_{0}
as the set of labels to determine a phase pair
(\Xi,\delta\kappa)
on
\hat{S}
(as in Section
8.2
); then restricts this pair to the partial completion
\hat{S}_{X}\subset\hat{S}
. As we already noted, in order to make the energy method work when replacing the planar end
\mathbb{H}^{-}_{0}
in
\hat{S}
by a Lagrangian boundary condition along
\partial\hat{S}_{X}
, we have to require that

(12.11)

g_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{\dagger}\circ\sigma_{0}(\cdot,0):\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{C}

be a straight line in the direction of
-ie^{i\beta_{X}}
; compare (
12.9
). Figure
43
below illustrates such a phase function for a cobordism map

\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}},\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}})\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q_{0},\theta_{0}})\to\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q_{1},\theta_{1}})

with
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
. With this convention understood, one can prove the energy estimate as usual. Finally, suppose that this construction is carried out for another
\mathbf{Th}_{0}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{0}
which satisfies (
12.10
) and
\mathbf{Th}_{0}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{s}\mathbf{Th}_{0}^{\prime}
, then the
A_{\infty}
-category defined using
X\sqcup\mathbf{Th}_{0}^{\prime}
, which we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{X}^{\prime}
, is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{E}_{X}
. This is seen by building up a larger category
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{X}
with

\Ob\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{X}:X\prec S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m}\prec S_{1}^{\prime}\prec S_{2}^{\prime}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m}^{\prime}.

and by repeating the proof of Proposition
8.24
. Thus the left
\mathcal{A}
-module
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
associated to
\mathcal{E}_{X}
, which assigns to each
S_{k}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
the Floer complex
\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{k})
, is well-defined up to canonical quasi-isomorphism. The following property about quasi-units is used in this proof.

\begin{overpic}[Pic52.png]
\put(-5.0,22.0){\small$-e^{i\theta_{1}}$}
\put(2.0,-2.0){\small$-e^{i\theta_{0}}$}
\put(101.0,10.0){\small$-ie^{i\beta_{X}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 43
.
The phase function on a pair-of-pants cobordism map. The green curve is
g_{0}(t)
, while red curves are the images of
g_{1}(t)
and
g_{2}(t)
. The blue region corresponds to the incoming end, while the red ones correspond to outgoing ends.

Proposition 12.4
.

Let
(\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}},\Lambda_{q,\theta_{1}})
be any thimbles with
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
and such that (
12.2
) holds with
\theta=\theta_{0},\theta_{1}
. Suppose in addition that the triangle bounded by
l_{q,\theta_{0}},\ l_{q,\theta_{1}}
and the equation
\re\langle w,e^{i\beta_{X}}\rangle\leq C_{X}
contains no critical values of
W
other than the vertex
W(q)
, then the multiplication map

m_{*}(e_{q},\cdot):\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}})\to\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta_{1}})

is an isomorphism.

The proof of this result follows the same line of arguments in Section
11.6
(using the vertical gluing theorem) and is omitted here.

In the same vein, for any stable-type Lagrangian submanifold
Y=(Y,h_{Y},\xi_{Y}^{\#},\beta_{Y})
, one may construct a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{Y}
with

\Ob\mathcal{E}_{Y}:Y\prec U_{m}\prec U_{m-1}\cdots\prec U_{1}.

if the admissible set of thimbles
\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}\in\Gamma_{\pi}
satisfies the relation

(12.12)

\pi<\eta_{1}<\eta_{2}<\cdots<\eta_{m}<\min\{\pi+\theta_{\star},\beta_{Y}+\frac{\pi}{2}\},\beta_{Y}\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}),

and the left
\mathcal{B}
-module
{}_{\mathcal{B}}Y
associated to
\mathcal{E}_{Y}
is well-defined up to canonical quasi-isomorphism.

12.5.
Lower half planes

As an alternative route, one may switch the role of
X
and
\Lambda_{q,\theta}
and define the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X)
by counting instantons on the lower upper plane
\mathbb{H}^{-}
. As the theory is identical to the previous one, we only explain the basic setup. In this case, a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}
is a quintuple
(R,\alpha_{X}^{\prime}(s),\beta_{X}-\pi,\epsilon_{X},\delta H^{\prime})
as usual, but the function
\alpha_{X}^{\prime}
is now defined on
(-\infty,R]_{s}
such that for some
0<\delta\ll 1
,

\alpha_{X}(s)\equiv\theta-\pi\text{ for all }s\leq\delta,

and
\beta_{X}
is replaced by
\beta_{X}-\pi
. The reference path
p_{\model}:(-\infty,R]_{s}\to M
satisfies that
p_{\model}(s)\equiv q
when
s\leq 0
. Consider the path space

\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{p\in C^{\infty}((-\infty,R]_{s};M):p(R)\in X,\ p\text{ has finite $L^{2}_{k}$-distance with }p_{\model}\},k\geq 1.

Then any a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}
defines an action functional on
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X)
by the formula:

(12.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}}(p)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}h_{X}(p(R))+\int_{-\infty}^{R}-p^{*}\lambda_{M}+\delta H_{s}(p(s))ds

\displaystyle+\int_{-\infty}^{R}\big(\im(e^{-i\alpha_{X}(s)}(W\circ p(s)))+\chi_{(-\infty,0]}\im(e^{-i\theta}W(q))\big)ds.

If
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}
is admissible, then the Floer cohomology
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X;\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X})
is defined as the Morse cohomology of
\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}}
. This construction is not new. Given any such
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X}
, one can construct a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{X}=(R,\alpha_{X},\beta_{X},\epsilon_{X},\delta H)
for the pair
(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta})
by taking

\alpha_{X}=\tau^{*}\alpha_{X}^{\prime}+\pi,\delta H=\tau^{*}\delta H^{\prime}

with
\tau:\mathbb{R}^{+}_{s}\to(-\infty,R]_{s}
defined by
\tau(s)=R-s
. Thus
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X;\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}_{X})
is isomorphic to the Morse homology of
\mathcal{A}_{X,\mathfrak{a}_{X}}
up to a degree shifting

\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X;\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{\prime})\cong\HFF^{\natural}_{\mathfrak{n}-*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X}),

or equivalently,
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{q,\theta},X;\mathfrak{a}_{X}^{\prime})\cong D\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta};\mathfrak{a}_{X})[-\mathfrak{n}]
, where
DV
stands for the dual of a finite graded vector space
V
and
\mathfrak{n}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(TM,J_{M})
. If
X
is of unstable-type, then one can construct a right
\mathcal{A}
-module
X_{\mathcal{A}}
using
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{k},X;\mathfrak{a}_{k,X}^{\prime})
,
1\leq k\leq m
, which is obtained from
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
by applying the duality functor
\mathcal{D}:\mathcal{P}_{l}^{\opp}\to\mathcal{P}_{r}
in (
6.13
).

13.
Seidel’s Spectral Sequence

13.1.
Filtered bimodules

In the most ideal case, one would like to establish Seidel’s algebraic spectral sequence for any pair of Lagrangian submanifolds
(X,Y)
, where
X=(X,h_{X},\beta_{X},\xi_{X}^{\#})
is of unstable-type, and
Y=(Y,h_{Y},\beta_{Y},\xi_{Y}^{\#})
of stable-type. In this section, we shall assume something stronger to make our method work:

(S1)

X
is of unstable-type, so
\beta_{X}\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
, and
H|_{X}\geq C_{X}
;

(S2)

Y
is of stable-type, so
\beta_{Y}\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2})
, and
H|_{Y}\leq C_{Y}
.

See Figure
44
below. Choose admissible sets of thimbles
\mathbf{Th}_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
and
\mathbf{Th}_{\pi}\in\Gamma_{\pi}
, which satisfy the conditions (
12.10
) (
12.12
), and denote by
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{0})
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{Th}_{\pi})
the Fukaya-Seidel category and its dual category respectively. Under
(S1)
and
(S2)
, for some
C^{\prime}>0
, we have

(13.1)

\displaystyle\re(e^{-\beta_{X}^{\prime}}W)|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\text{ for all }\beta_{X}^{\prime}\in[-\frac{\pi}{2},\beta_{X}]

\displaystyle\re(e^{-\beta_{Y}^{\prime}}W)|_{Y}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\text{ for all }\beta_{Y}^{\prime}\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\beta_{Y}].

\begin{overpic}[Pic64.png]
\put(95.0,60.0){\small$S_{k}$}
\put(0.0,0.0){\small$U_{j}$}
\put(20.0,45.0){\small$Y$}
\put(75.0,15.0){\small$X$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 44
.
The projection of thimbles,
X
and
Y
onto
\mathbb{C}
(with
\beta_{X}=0
and
\beta_{Y}=\pi
).

Thus one can also define a left
\mathcal{A}
-module
{}_{\mathcal{A}}Y
for
Y
and a
\mathcal{B}
-module
X_{\mathcal{B}}
for
X
. In fact, there is a finite directed
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
with

\Ob\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}:\underbrace{Y\prec U_{m}\prec\cdots U_{2}\prec U_{1}}_{\Ob\mathcal{E}_{Y}}\prec\underbrace{X\prec S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m}}_{\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}},\ S_{k}\in\Ob\mathcal{A},U_{j}\in\Ob\mathcal{B},

and which contains
\mathcal{E}_{X},\mathcal{E}_{Y}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta}
as full
A_{\infty}
-subcategories. This
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
depends on an extra stretching parameter
R\gg\pi
(which is omitted from our notations) and is defined using the metric ribbon tree
\mathcal{T}^{m+1,m+1}_{R}
. For
R\gg\pi
, the induced
(\mathcal{E}_{X},\mathcal{E}_{Y})
-bimodule
\Delta_{*}=\Delta_{*,R}
carries an increasing filtration:

0=\Delta^{(0)}_{*}\subset\Delta^{(1)}_{*}\subset\cdots\subset\Delta^{(m)}_{*}=\Delta_{*},

which is induced by the action functionals and agrees with the increasing filtration (
9.2
) on the bimodule
\Delta
when restricted to
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
.

We elaborate on the construction of
\Delta_{*}
. Suppose that the functions
\alpha_{j}^{un},\alpha_{k}^{st}
have been chosen as in (
9.2
); take
\frac{\pi}{2}<\beta_{*}<\frac{\pi}{2}+\min\{\theta_{m},\eta_{1}-\pi\}
and
\epsilon_{*}>0
as in (
9.9
). Combined with (
12.10
)(
12.12
), this implies that

0<\beta_{*}-\frac{\pi}{2}<\theta_{m}<\cdots<\theta_{1}<\beta_{X}+\frac{\pi}{2}<\pi<\beta_{*}+\frac{\pi}{2}<\eta_{1}<\cdots<\eta_{m}<\beta_{Y}+\frac{\pi}{2}<2\pi.

with
S_{k}=\Lambda_{x_{k},\theta_{k}}
and
U_{j}=\Lambda_{x_{j},\eta_{j}}
. We require further that

(13.2)

0<\epsilon_{*}<\min\{\re(e^{i\beta_{X}}),-\re(e^{i\beta_{Y}})\}.

\begin{overpic}[Pic63.png]
\put(10.0,5.0){$Y$}
\put(45.0,5.0){$U_{j_{2}}$}
\put(85.0,5.0){$U_{j_{1}}$}
\put(85.0,40.0){$X$}
\put(5.0,40.0){$S_{k_{3}}$}
\put(35.0,40.0){$S_{k_{2}}$}
\put(60.0,40.0){$S_{k_{1}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 45
.
The diagonal bimodule
\Delta_{*}.
The green region is stretched vertically.

As a label, we think of
X
as
\Lambda_{q,\pi}
and
Y
as
\Lambda_{q,2\pi}
for any choice of
q\in\Crit(W)
. This helps us determine Floer data as well as cobordism data on pointed disks. Now for any
R\geq\pi
, consider the functions

\alpha^{R}_{Y,k}(s)=\alpha_{k}^{st}(s-R),\ s\in[0,+\infty)_{s}\text{ and }\alpha^{R}_{j,X}(s)=\alpha_{j}^{un}(s),\ s\in(-\infty,R+\pi]_{s}.

Then for any
s\in[\pi,R]_{s}
,
\alpha^{R}_{Y,k}(s)=\alpha^{R}_{j,X}(s)=\pi
. The Floer data are then defined as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{Y,k}^{R}

\displaystyle=(R+\pi,\alpha^{R}_{Y,k}(s),\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H^{R}_{Y,k})

\displaystyle\text{ for }(Y,S_{k}),1\leq k\leq m,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{j,X}^{R}

\displaystyle=(R+\pi,\alpha^{R}_{j,X}(s),\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H^{R}_{j,X})

\displaystyle\text{ for }(U_{j},X),1\leq j\leq m,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{R}_{Y,X}

\displaystyle=(R+\pi,\alpha^{R}_{Y,X}(s),\beta_{*},\epsilon_{*},\delta H_{Y,X})

\displaystyle\text{ for }(Y,X)

with the usual assumptions on perturbation 1-forms; cf. Section
9.2
. For simplicity we shall take the function
\alpha^{R}_{Y,X}(s)\equiv\pi
to be constant.

Let
S
be any
(d+1)
-pointed disk labeled by a subset
A\subset\Ob\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
with
|A|=d+1\geq 3
, and let
\phi
be any compatible quadratic differential which is
\epsilon
-close to
\mathcal{T}^{d_{1},d_{2}}_{R}
with
d_{1}=|A\cap\Ob\mathcal{E}_{Y}|
and
d_{2}=|A\cap\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}|
. If
Y\in A
, then
Y
is attached the first component
C_{0}\subset\partial S
. Similar to (
12.11
), the curve
g_{0}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Xi^{\dagger}\circ\sigma_{0}(t,0):\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{C}
must satisfy some additional properties:

•

If
d_{1}=1
, then
A\setminus\{Y\}\subset\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}
, and
\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)\equiv-i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{*}}
;

•

If
d_{2}=0
, then
A\subset\Ob\mathcal{E}_{Y}
, and
\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)\equiv-i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{X}}
;

•

If
d_{1}\geq 2
and
d_{2}\geq 1
, then
\partial_{t}g_{0}(t)
is in the direction of
-ie^{i\beta_{*}}
for
t\leq 0
and
-ie^{i\beta_{Y}}
for
t\gg 0
. In general, this direction lies in the interval
[\beta_{*}-\frac{\pi}{2},\beta_{Y}-\frac{\pi}{2}]
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
. The condition
(S2)
ensures that
\re(e^{-i\beta}W)|_{Y}
is bounded above for any
\beta\in[\beta_{*},\beta_{Y}]\subset[\frac{\pi}{2},\beta_{Y}]
.

If
X\in A
, then
X
is attached to the
d_{1}
-th component
C_{d_{1}}\subset\partial S
, and for the curve
g_{d_{1}}(t)\equiv\Xi^{\dagger}\circ\sigma_{d_{1}}(t,0):\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{C}
, we require that

•

If
d_{2}=1
, then
A\setminus\{X\}\subset\Ob\mathcal{E}_{Y}
, and
\partial_{t}g_{d_{1}}(t)\equiv i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{*}}
;

•

If
d_{1}=0
, then
A\subset\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}
, and
\partial_{t}g_{d_{1}}(t)\equiv-i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{X}}
;

•

If
d_{2}\geq 2
and
d_{1}\geq 1
, then
\partial_{t}g_{d_{1}}(t)
is in the direction of
i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{*}}
for
t\ll 0
and
-i\epsilon_{*}e^{i\beta_{X}}
for
t\gg 0
. In general, this direction lies in the interval
[\beta_{*}-\frac{3\pi}{2},\beta_{X}-\frac{\pi}{2}]
. The condition
(S1)
ensures that
\re(e^{-i\beta}W)|_{X}
is bounded above for any
\beta\in[\beta_{*}-\pi,\beta_{X}]\subset[-\frac{\pi}{2},\beta_{X}]
.

With these conventions understood, one can repeat the argument in Section
9
to construct the filtered bimodule
\Delta_{*}=\Delta_{*,R}
. See Figure
46
below for a typical phase function on the partial completion of a
(6+1)
pointed disk (compare Figure
45
). The green region is the image of the strip
Z_{R}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[2\pi,R-\pi]_{s}
, which is stretched as
R\to\infty
. The blue (resp. pink) regions are associated to the incoming end and the
d_{1}
-th outgoing end (resp. other outing ends). Each gray curve is the translated copy of some characteristic curves. The two red curves are the images of
g_{0}(t)
and
g_{d_{1}}(t)
respectively.

\begin{overpic}[Pic65.png]
\put(10.0,50.0){$Y$}
\put(38.0,60.0){$U_{j_{2}}$}
\put(60.0,60.0){$U_{j_{1}}$}
\put(90.0,10.0){$X$}
\put(20.0,-2.0){$S_{k_{3}}$}
\put(43.0,-2.0){$S_{k_{2}}$}
\put(60.0,-2.0){$S_{k_{1}}$}

\put(75.0,25.0){$g_{d_{1}}(t)$}

\put(72.0,5.0){$-ie^{i\beta_{X}}$}
\put(0.0,30.0){-$ie^{i\beta_{*}}$}

Figure 46
.
A typical phase function on a
(6+1)
-pointed disk with
d_{1}=3
and
d_{2}=4
(
\beta_{X}=0
and
\beta_{Y}=\pi
).

Remark 13.1
.

Readers may wonder how these properties about
g_{0}
and
g_{d_{1}}
are certified when we construct the family of phase functions on
\mathcal{S}^{|A|}\to\mathcal{R}^{|A|}
using Lemma
9.14
, or Lemma
8.13
. The problem may occur in the proof of Lemma
8.7
as we “push” these curves outwards. Since one may always increase the constant
K
to construct the null-homotopy, these properties can be preserved if this push-out is designed carefully enough (the proof outlined in
Step
2 of Lemma
5.6
is just one possible way for this push-out). We leave the details to interested readers.
∎

13.2.
Statements

Now we are already to state the algebraic formula to rebuild the Floer cohomology
\HFF^{*}_{\natural}(X,Y)=H(D\Delta_{*}(Y,X)[-\mathfrak{n}])
in terms of the
A_{\infty}
-modules
{}_{\mathcal{A}}X
,
Y_{\mathcal{B}}
and the bimodule
{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}
. Applying the cyclic operation
\mathcal{C}
defined in (
6.15
), one can transform
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
into another category with

\Ob\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}:U_{m}\prec\cdots\prec U_{2}\prec U_{1}\prec X\prec S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m}\prec Y.

This defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor into
\mathcal{Q}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{B})
:

(13.3)

r_{X,Y}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}\xrightarrow{r_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}}}\rfmod(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}})\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}.

such that
r_{X,Y}|_{\mathcal{A}}
is the functor
r_{\Delta}
in Theorem
8.3
. While
r_{X,Y}
is not cohomologically full and faithful in general, one can still prove the following result.

Theorem 13.2
.

For any
V\prec V^{\prime}\in\Ob\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
, the first order map of
r_{X,Y}

r^{1}_{X,Y}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}(V,V^{\prime})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(r_{X,Y}(V),r_{X,Y}(V^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism.

If
V\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
, then this statement follows from the Yoneda embedding theorem. If
V,V^{\prime}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
, then this follows from Theorem
8.3
. Let
X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{S}_{k}\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}
denote the images of
X,Y,S_{k}
under
r_{X,Y}
respectively. Thus the non-trivial part of Theorem
13.2
is when the map
r^{1}_{X,Y}
is between

(13.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}(X,Y)=\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}(X,Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D(\Delta_{*}(X,Y))[-\mathfrak{n}]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}(S_{k},Y)=\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}}),

(13.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}(S_{k},Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D(\Delta_{*}(S_{k},Y))[-\mathfrak{n}]

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{S}_{k},Y_{\mathcal{B}}),

(13.6)

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}(X,S_{k})

\displaystyle\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{S}_{k}),

Note that for
R\gg\pi
, the right
\mathcal{B}
-module
X_{\mathcal{B}}
inherits an increasing filtration from
\Delta_{*}
:

0=X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}\subset\cdots X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(m)}=X_{\mathcal{B}},

while the dual complex
\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}(X,Y)
inherits a decreasing filtration

(13.7)

0=(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(m+1)}(X,Y)\subset\cdots\subset(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(1)}(X,Y)=(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(X,Y)

with

(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(n)}(X,Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D(\Delta_{*}(Y,X)/\Delta_{*}^{(n-1)}(Y,X))[-\mathfrak{n}],\ 1\leq n\leq m+1.

While
X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n)}
involves the first
n
-critical points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
of
W
,
(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(n)}(X,Y)
involves
x_{n},\cdots,x_{m}
. Theorem
13.2
can be refined as follows.

Theorem 13.3
.

Since the geometric filtration is preserved under the bimodule action of
\Delta_{*}
,
r^{1}_{X,Y}
restricts to a chain map for all
0\leq n\leq m
:

(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(n)}(X,Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n-1)},Y_{\mathcal{B}}).

This map is a quasi-isomorphism. The same holds if
X
is replaced by any stable thimble
S_{k}\in\Ob\mathcal{A}
.

The setup we have developed so far is symmetric between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, so one may work instead with the
(\mathcal{E}_{Y},\mathcal{E}_{X})
-bimodule
\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}
, which is the dual of
\Delta_{*}
; see for instance Lemma
6.11
.
\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}
is defined by the relation
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{D}\Delta^{*}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{C}^{m+1}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta^{*}}
and is filtered as in (
13.7
) where
X
is replaced by any
S_{k}
and
Y
by
U_{j}
in general. Thus
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}}=\mathcal{C}^{m+2}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
has objects ordered by

\Ob\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}}:S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\cdots\prec S_{m}\prec Y\prec U_{m}\prec\cdots\prec U_{2}\prec U_{1}\prec X.

Let
r_{Y,X}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}}\to\mathcal{P}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{A})
denote the induced functor. At this point, there are four
A_{\infty}
-modules associated to
X
(and resp. to
Y
)

\displaystyle{}_{\mathcal{A}}X=\mathcal{D}X_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\text{ and }{}_{\mathcal{B}}X=\mathcal{D}(X_{\mathcal{B}})\text{ (with filtrations)},

\displaystyle{}_{\mathcal{B}}Y=\mathcal{D}Y_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\text{ and }{}_{\mathcal{A}}Y=\mathcal{D}(Y_{\mathcal{A}})\text{ (with filtrations)},

where
\mathcal{D}
denotes the duality functor defined by (
6.13
). Then Theorem
13.2
and
13.3
implies the following.

Corollary 13.4
.

By (
13.6
), the
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism

t^{l}_{X}:{}_{\mathcal{A}}X\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}})

induced by
\Delta_{*}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathcal{P}_{l}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\lfmod(\mathcal{A})
. Indeed, its first order map is given precisely (
13.6
). Similarly, Theorem
13.3
(with
X
replaced by
S_{k}
) implies that for any
0\leq n\leq m
,

t^{r}_{Y}:Y_{\mathcal{A}}^{(n)}\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}/{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta^{(n-1)}_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow[\cong]{\text{ duality }}\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{B}}Y,{}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{D}\Delta)_{\mathcal{A}}^{(n)})

is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathcal{P}_{r}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rfmod(\mathcal{A})
.

Corollary 13.5
.

Switching the roles of
\Delta
and
\mathcal{D}\Delta
in Corollary
13.4
, we obtain that

t_{X}^{r}:X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n)}\to\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}X,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n)}),

is a quasi-isomorphism between finite right
\mathcal{B}
-modules for all
0\leq n\leq m
.

Corollary 13.6
.

By Lemma
6.9
,
t_{X}^{r}
admits a quasi-inverse
(t_{X}^{r})^{-1}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. By composing
r^{1}_{X,Y}
with
\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\cdot,(t_{X}^{r})^{-1})
, we obtain a quasi-isomorphism:

\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}(X,Y)\xrightarrow{r^{1}_{X,Y}}\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow{\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\cdot,(t_{X}^{r})^{-1})}\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}\big(\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{A}}X,{}_{\mathcal{A}}\Delta_{\mathcal{B}}),Y_{\mathcal{B}}\big).

Switching the roles of
X
and
Y
, we also have

\Delta_{*}(Y,X)\xrightarrow{r^{1}_{Y,X}}\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{r}}(Y_{\mathcal{A}},X_{\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{P}_{r}}(\cdot,(t_{Y}^{r})^{-1})}\hom_{\mathcal{P}_{r}}\big(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}({}_{\mathcal{B}}Y,{}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{D}\Delta)_{\mathcal{A}}),X_{\mathcal{A}}\big).

13.3.
Proof of Theorem
13.2
and
13.3
: Step 1

Theorem
13.3
is proved by a few quick reductions. First, since the chain map
r^{1}_{X,Y}
preserves the filtrations, it induces a map between two spectral sequences. To show that
[r^{1}_{X,Y}]
is an isomorphism between
E_{\infty}
-pages, one can work instead with the
E_{1}
-page. To put it another way, for any
0\leq n\leq m-1
, we have a map between exact triangles, which goes vertically in the diagram below,

(13.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big((\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(n+1)}(X,Y)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big((\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})^{(n)}(X,Y)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big(\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(X,Y)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n)},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n-1)},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big(\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\big)}

where
\mathcal{G}^{n}
denotes the associated graded complexes/modules. Thus it suffices to verify the quasi-isomorphism for each associated graded component:

(13.9)

r^{1}_{n}:\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(X,Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}}),\ 1\leq n\leq m.

13.4.
Proof of Theorem
13.2
and
13.3
: Step 2

For the next reduction, we apply Lemma
6.1
to the
A_{\infty}
-functor
r_{X,Y}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}
and to the triple
(X,S_{n},Y)\subset\Ob\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
. Then (
6.4
) says that the following diagram is commutative up to chain homotopy (take the
n
-th graded component
\mathcal{G}^{n}
):

(13.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(S_{n},Y)\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}}).}

The next lemma implies that the vertical arrows in (
13.10
) are quasi-isomorphisms.

Lemma 13.7
.

The
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism

\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n}\otimes D\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{n})

induced by
r_{X,Y}^{1}:\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{n})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n})
is a quasi-isomorphism meaning that for any
U_{j}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
, the cobordism map induced by a pair-of-pants

\mathcal{G}^{n}\Delta_{*}(U_{j},X)\to\mathcal{G}^{n}\Delta_{*}(U_{j},S_{n})\otimes D\hom_{\mathcal{E}_{X}}(X,S_{n})

is a quasi-isomorphism. The same holds if
U_{j}
is replaced by
Y
.

Remark 13.8
.

Lemma
13.7
is the analogue of Lemma
10.3
where
S_{n}
is replaced by
X
and
S_{l}
by
S_{n}
.
∎

Thus it suffices to show that the top row of (
13.10
) is a quasi-isomorphism, so we may replace
X
by
S_{n}
in (
13.9
) and verify instead that

(13.11)

r^{1}_{n}:\mathcal{G}^{n}(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(S_{n},Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}}).

is a quasi-isomorphism. From now on the problem is not related to
X
any longer.

13.5.
Proof of Theorem
13.2
and
13.3
: Step 3

For the third reduction, let
V_{n}=\Lambda_{x_{n},\eta_{n}^{\prime}}
denote the thimble in the direction of
\eta_{n}^{\prime}\in(0,\eta_{1})
. As in
Step
2 of the proof of Lemma
10.1
, we consider an enlargement of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}}
whose objects are ordered as

Y\prec U_{1}\prec U_{2}\prec\cdots\prec U_{m}\prec V_{n}\prec S_{n}.

where we have omitted
X
and other stable thimbles for simplicity. This defines a filtered
(\mathcal{E}_{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}_{Y}^{\prime})
-bimodule
\Delta_{*}^{\prime}
, where
\Ob\mathcal{E}_{X}^{\prime}=(S_{n})
and
\mathcal{E}_{Y}^{\prime}
consists of all other objects of
\mathcal{E}_{\Delta_{*}^{\prime}}
. Then the cyclic rotation
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
has objects ordered by

U_{1}\prec U_{2}\prec\cdots\prec U_{m}\prec V_{n}\prec S_{n}\prec Y

and defines an
A_{\infty}
-functor

r^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{r_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}}\rfmod(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\text{restriction}}\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\rfmod(\mathcal{B}).

Apply Lemma
6.1
to
r^{\prime}
and the triple
(V_{n},S_{n},Y)
; then (
6.5
) says that the following diagram is commutative up to a chain-homotopy (take the
n
-th graded component
\mathcal{G}^{n}
):

(13.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(S_{n},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},Y)\otimes D\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},S_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\otimes D\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},S_{n}).}

The next lemma says that the vertical arrows in (
13.12
) are quasi-isomorphisms.

Lemma 13.9
.

The
A_{\infty}
-homomorphism

\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},S_{n})\otimes\mathcal{V}_{n}\to\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n}

induced by
(r^{\prime})^{1}:\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},S_{n})\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{n})
is a quasi-isomorphism meaning that for any
U_{j}\in\Ob\mathcal{B}
, the cobordism map induced by a pair-of-pants

\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},S_{n})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{j},V_{n})\to\mathcal{G}^{n}\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{j},S_{n})

is a quasi-isomorphism. The same holds if
U_{j}
is replaced by
Y
.

Remark 13.10
.

The first part of Lemma
13.9
is exactly Lemma
10.4
. Only the case when
U_{j}
is replaced by
Y
is new.
∎

13.6.
Proof of Theorem
13.2
and
13.3
: Step 4

Finally, it remains to verify the second row of (
13.12
)

r^{\prime}:\hom_{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},Y)\to\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}})

is a quasi-isomorphism. This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{V}_{n}
is quasi-isomorphic to the Yoneda image
\mathcal{U}_{n}
of
U_{n}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{r}
. At this point, there is no energy filtration involved in the chain map. Apply Lemma
6.1
again to the functor
r^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Q}_{r}
but now to the triple
(U_{n},V_{n},Y)
. Then (
6.4
) implies that the following diagram is commutative up to a chain-homotopy:

(13.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(V_{n},Y)\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{n},V_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{V}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}})\otimes\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{n},V_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{n},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(\mathcal{U}_{n},Y_{\mathcal{B}}).}

Note that
\hom_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(U_{n},V_{n})
is 1-dimensional and is generated by the quasi-unit
e_{x_{n}}
(the constant soliton at
x_{n}
). By Proposition
12.4
and
Step
3 in the proof of Lemma
10.1
, the vertical arrows of (
13.13
) are quasi-isomorphisms. By the Yoneda embedding theorem, the second row of (
13.13
) is a quasi-isomorphism, and so is the first row.

Lemma
13.7
and
13.9
are proved in the same way as Lemma
10.3
and Lemma
10.4
using the vertical gluing theorem (the proof is identical and hence omitted). This completes the proof of Theorem
13.3
. Note that (
13.4
) and (
13.5
) follow from Theorem
13.3
by taking
n=0
.

Finally, (
13.6
) is proved by repeating the argument in Theorem
8.3
with
S_{1}
replaced by
X
and Lemma
10.1
by Lemma
13.7
. This completes the proof of Theorem
13.2
.

13.7.
Identifying the geometric/algebraic filtrations

We would like to compare the geometric filtration on
(\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*})(X,Y)
with the algebraic filtration on
\hom_{\mathcal{Q}_{r}}(X_{\mathcal{B}},Y_{\mathcal{B}})
as defined in (
6.26
). By Theorem
13.3
and the argument in Section
6.6
, it suffices to show that the ladder

(13.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(m-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{m}X_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{3}X_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{2}X_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{B}}=X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{1}X_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}

defines a Postnikov decomposition of
X_{\mathcal{B}}
; cf. (
6.25
)
(L)
. Combining Lemma
13.7
and Lemma
13.9
, we have
\mathcal{G}^{n}X_{\mathcal{B}}\cong\mathcal{U}_{n}\otimes D\hom_{\mathcal{S}_{X}}(X,S_{n})
. An inductive argument using the exact triangles in (
13.14
) shows that
X_{\mathcal{B}}/X_{\mathcal{B}}^{(n)}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,r}
and the property (
6.23
) holds for every
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n,r},0\leq k\leq m
. Although we have worked constantly with chain complexes in Section
6.6
, this spectral sequence relies essentially on the cohomological category
H(\mathcal{Q}_{r})
, especially if one thinks in terms of exact couples
[
GM03
, P.263]
. Thus we have proved that

Theorem 13.11
.

The spectral sequence induced by the geometric filtration on
\mathcal{D}\Delta_{*}(X,Y)
is isomorphic to Seidel’s algebraic spectral sequence defined in Theorem
6.12
. This means that there is an isomorphism between each page of the spectral sequences, starting from
E_{1}
, which commutes with the differential maps.

13.8.
Seidel’s long exact sequence: revisited

We end this section with some applications of the vertical gluing theorem. Proposition
12.4
is concerned with the case when the thimble
\Lambda_{q,\theta_{1}}
can be continuously deformed into
\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}}
without violating the condition (
2.8
). As in the case of thimbles, if the triangle in Proposition
12.4
(compare Figure
42
with Figure
47
below) contains a single critical point, then the multiplication map
m_{*}(e_{q},\ \cdot\ ):\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}})\to\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(X,\Lambda_{q,\theta_{1}})
might not be an isomorphism and will fit into a long exact sequence, as first proved by Seidel
[
Sei03
]
. The next proposition generalizes Proposition
11.17
to the case of Lagrangian submanifolds. To see their resemblance, we now put
X
in the second entry.

Proposition 13.12
.

Choose angles
\theta_{3}<\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{3}+\pi
such that
e^{i(\theta_{j}-\beta_{X})}>0
for all
j=0,1,3
. Let
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{q,\theta_{j}},j=0,1
,
q\in\Crit(W)
, and suppose that the closed triangle bounded by
l_{q,\theta_{0}},l_{q,\theta_{1}}
and the equation
\re\langle w,e^{i\beta_{X}}\rangle\leq C_{X}
only contains the critical value
W(q)
on the boundary and a critical value
W(q_{3})
in the interior. Let
\Lambda_{3}=\Lambda_{q_{3},\theta_{3}}
. Then we have a long exact sequence :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{3},X)\otimes\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{1},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\HFF_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{*}(\cdot,e_{q})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1]}

The proof of Proposition
13.12
follows the same line of arguments as in Proposition
11.17
with
\Lambda_{2}
replaced by
X
and hence is omitted here.

\begin{overpic}[Pic62.png]
\put(35.0,5.0){\small$X$}
\put(100.0,5.0){\small$X$}
\put(60.0,35.0){\small$\re\langle w,e^{i\beta_{X}}\rangle=C_{X}$}
\put(-5.0,35.0){\small$\re\langle w,e^{i\beta_{X}}\rangle=C_{X}$}
\put(95.0,38.0){\small$\Lambda_{0}$}
\put(92.0,43.0){\small$S_{\star}$}
\put(58.0,0.0){\small$U_{\star}$}
\put(66.0,6.0){\small$q$}
\put(1.0,6.0){\small$q$}
\put(14.0,20.0){\small$q_{3}$}
\put(30.0,38.0){\small$\Lambda_{0}$}
\put(35.0,21.0){\small$\Lambda_{1}$}
\put(45.0,9.0){\small$\Lambda_{3}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 47
.
Seidel’s long exact sequence (left) and the isomorphism between of
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
and
\HFF^{*}
(right).

13.9.
The isomorphism between
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}
and
\HFF^{*}

Let
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda_{q,\theta_{0}}
. Suppose that
W(X)\subset\mathbb{C}
is bounded above in the direction of
e^{i\beta_{X}}
and
e^{i(\theta_{0}-\beta_{X})}>0
. Another application of the vertical gluing theorem is to show that
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},X)
is isomorphic to the
\HFF^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X)
, the Floer cohomology defined using Lagrangian boundary condition (counting solutions on strips). We used an underline here to emphasis that the Lagrangian boundary condition along
\underline{\Lambda}_{0}
is used here to define this group. At this point, we have to assume that the thimble
\Lambda_{0}
has bounded geometry as a totally real submanifold of
(M,J_{M},\omega_{M},g_{M})
. This is a technical point which we will not address in this paper.

Suppose that we are in the scenario of Figure
47
, and
\theta_{-1}=\theta_{0}+\epsilon,0<\epsilon\ll 1
is a slight perturbation of
\theta_{0}
. Let
U_{\star}=\Lambda_{q,\theta_{-1}-\pi}
and
S_{\star}=\Lambda_{q,\theta_{-1}}
. Choose a function
\alpha_{0}(s):\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
as in (
11.6
) to define an admissible Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
for the pair
(\Lambda_{0},U_{\star})
. If
\mathfrak{a}_{X}
is any admissible Floer datum for
(S_{\star},X)
, then they can be concatenated to give a Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R}
for
(\Lambda_{0},X)
(cf. Section
11.1
). There are two ways to define the Floer cohomology of
(\Lambda_{0},X)
, either by counting instantons on the lower half planes,
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(-\infty,R+\pi]
, which gives
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\Lambda_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R})
, or by restricting the Floer datum
\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R}
on
[0,R+\pi]_{s}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
and counting instantons on strips, which gives
\Ch^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R})
. For any
R\gg\pi
, the Floer complex
\Ch^{*}(\Lambda_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R})
has only one filtration level. The proof of Theorem
11.3
is adapted to this case to show that

\Ch^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R})\cong\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(S_{\star},X;\mathfrak{a}_{X})\otimes\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{0}),

that is, they are isomorphic as chain complexes). If
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
is chosen properly,
\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},U_{\star};\mathfrak{a}_{0})
has rank 1 and is generated by the constant soliton
e_{q}
. This proves

H(\Ch^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0};\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R})))\cong\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(S_{\star},X)\cong\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},X)\ (\text{by Proposition }\ref{FL.P.4})

Finally, the cohomology group
H(\Ch^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R}))
is independent of
R>\pi
. We can always pull back the solution (cf. (
1.6
)) and think of the Floer equation as defined on the same domain
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}
, but the Floer datum may change as
R
varies. Even in the worst case that
W(X)
is only bounded in the direction of
e^{i\beta_{X}}
, since
W(\Lambda_{0})
is the ray
l_{q,\theta_{0}}
, one may still use a cobordism datum on the strip
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}
to construct continuation maps (cf. Section
5.2
) and verify this invariance.

In this paper the construction of Floer cohomology always relies on a perturbation by the superpotential
W
. If one wishes to compare
H(\Ch^{*}_{\natural}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X;\mathfrak{a}_{0,X}^{R}))
with the version without such a perturbation, extra assumptions on
(M,W)
are required to verify the compactness theorem, and we leave this task to interested readers. One may also replace
X
by a thimble
\Lambda_{1}
to obtain the following result.

Proposition 13.13
.

Suppose that
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{q,\theta_{j}},j=0,1
has bounded geometry and
\theta_{1}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
, then
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1})\cong\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},\underline{\Lambda}_{1})\cong\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},\Lambda_{1})\cong\HFF^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},\underline{\Lambda}_{1})
.

Moreover, for any Lagrangian submanifold
X=(X,h_{X},\beta_{X},\xi_{X}^{\#})
satisfying the conditions in Assumption
12.1
and with
e^{i(\beta_{X}-\theta_{0})}>0
, we have
\HFF_{\natural}^{*}(\Lambda_{0},X)\cong\HFF^{*}(\underline{\Lambda}_{0},X)
. Here
\HFF^{*}
is defined by counting instantons on infinite strips
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,1]_{s}
, but the equation is still perturbed by the superpotential
W
using a suitable Floer datum.

Appendix A
The Diameter Estimate

This appendix is devoted to the proof of the diameter estimate in Proposition
3.3
. This result concerns only the almost Hermitian geometry of
(M,J_{M},\omega_{M},g_{M})
and has played an essential role in the proof of the Local Compactness Lemma
3.1
. When
P:B(0,1)\to M
is
J
-holomorphic, this estimate follows from
[
IS00
]
; we shall only explain the necessary adaptation. In what follows, we shall always assume that
(M,J_{M},\omega_{M},g_{M})
has bounded geometry.

A.1.
The first step

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, let
B(z,R)
denote the disk centered at
z
of radius
t
. Fix some
r>2
. We begin with the small energy estimate.

Lemma A.1
.

There exist
\delta_{1},C_{1}>0
with the following property. Let
B(z,R)\subset B(0,1)
. If
P:B(0,1)\to M
is any smooth map such that
\diam P(B(z,R))\leq\delta_{1}
, then

\diam\big(P\big(B(z,\frac{R}{2})\big)\big)<C_{1}\big(\|dP\|_{L^{2}(B(z,R))}+\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(B(z,R))}\big).

Proof of
A.1
.

This follows from the proof of
[
IS00
, Lemma 1.1, Step 2]
. For
0<\delta_{1}\ll 1
, we may assume that the image
P(B(z,R))
lies in a geodesic ball, then the estimate follows from the case when
M=\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
J=J_{M}
is
C^{0}
-close to the standard complex structure. See
[
IS00
, Lemma 1.2]
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

There exist
\epsilon_{2},C_{2}>0
with the following property. If
P:B(0,1)\to M
is any smooth map such that

(A.1)

\int_{B(0,1)}|dP|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}<\epsilon_{2},

then

(A.2)

\diam\big(P\big(B(0,\frac{1}{2})\big)\big)<C_{2}\big(\|dP\|_{L^{2}(B(0,1))}+\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(B(0,1))}\big).

Proof of
A.2
.

This follows from Lemma
A.1
and by repeating the argument in
[
IS00
, Lemma 1.1, Step 3]
. Alternatively, it can be organized as follows. Let
\delta_{1}
be the constant from Lemma
A.1
. Consider the continuous function

(A.3)

f:B(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{+},\ f(z)=\max\{0\leq R\leq 1-|z|:\diam(P(B(z,R)))\leq\delta_{1}\},

and let

\alpha_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\min_{|z|<\frac{3}{4}}\frac{f(z)}{\frac{3}{4}-|z|}.

We claim that if
0<\epsilon_{2}\ll 1
is chosen sufficiently small, then
\alpha_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. If not, suppose that this minimum is attained at
z_{0}\in B(0,\frac{3}{4})
, set
r_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{3}{4}-|z_{0}|
, and consider the map

g:B(0,1)\to B(z_{0},r_{0}),\ w\mapsto z_{0}+r_{0}w.

Define
P_{0}(w)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P(g(w)):B(0,1)\to M
, and denote by
f_{0}
the function (
A.3
) associated to
P_{0}
. Then
f_{0}(0)=\alpha_{0}
. In general, since

f(g(w))\geq\alpha_{0}\bigg(\frac{3}{4}-|g(w)|\bigg)\geq\alpha_{0}\bigg(r_{0}-|g(w)-z_{0}|\bigg),

we have
f_{0}(w)=\min\{f(g(w))/r_{0},1-|w|\}\geq\min\{\alpha_{0}(1-|w|),1-|w|\}\geq\alpha_{0}(1-|w|)
. Hence, for all
|w|\leq\alpha_{0}
,
f_{0}(w)\geq\alpha_{0}/2
. Moreover, under this rescaling, the quantity (
A.1
) associated to
P_{0}
gets smaller. Since
B(0,\alpha_{0})
can be covered by finitely many disks
B(w_{i},\alpha_{0}/4)
with each
|w_{i}|\leq\alpha_{0}
, the diameter estimate from Lemma
A.1
applied to each
B(w_{i},\alpha_{0}/2)
shows that
\diam(P_{0}(B(0,\alpha_{0})))<\delta_{1}
if
\epsilon_{2}
is chosen sufficiently small. This contradicts our choice of
\alpha_{0}
.

We have verified that
\alpha_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}
, and so
f(z)\geq\frac{1}{8}
for all
|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Now we cover
B(0,\frac{1}{2})
by finitely many disks
B(z_{i},\frac{1}{16})
with each
|z_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. The estimate (
A.2
) now follows from Lemma
A.1
.
∎

A.2.
The second step

Lemma
A.2
is almost enough to deduce Proposition
3.3
. But when the energy is highly concentrated near a point, we need another lemma to control the diameter over an annulus. Let
A(R_{1},R_{2})
denote the annulus
B(0,R_{2})\setminus B(0,R_{1})
for
R_{1}<R_{2}
.

Lemma A.3
.

There exists
\epsilon_{3},C_{3}>0
with the following property. Suppose that
P:B(0,1)\to M
is any smooth map such that for some
0<R<\frac{1}{16}
,

(A.4)

\int_{A(R,1)}|dP|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}<\epsilon_{3},

then

(A.5)

\diam\big(P\big(A(2R,1/2)\big)\big)<C_{3}\big(\|dP\|_{L^{2}(A(R,1))}+\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(A(R,1))}\big).

The best way to understand Lemma
A.3
is to think of cylinders instead of annuli. Let
Z(a,b)
denote the cylinder
[a,b]\times S^{1}
equipped with the product metric for
a<b
, and
Z_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Z(n-1,n)
. The proof of
[
IS00
, Lemma 1.4]
can be adapted to show the following.

Lemma A.4
.

There exists
\epsilon_{4},C_{4}>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
with the following property. Suppose that
P:Z(0,5)\to M
is any smooth map with

\|dP\|_{L^{2}(Z_{n})}+\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(Z_{n})}<\epsilon_{4}

for all
1\leq n\leq 5
, then

\|dP\|_{L^{2}(Z_{3})}^{2}\leq\frac{\gamma}{2}\bigg(\|dP\|_{L^{2}(Z_{2})}^{2}+\|dP\|_{L^{2}(Z_{2})}^{2}\bigg)+C_{4}\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(Z(1,4))}^{2}.

We prove Lemma
A.3
given Lemma
A.4
.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

Consider
P
as a map on
(-\infty,0]_{\rho}\times S^{1}_{\theta}
with
z=\exp(\rho+i\theta)
. Define

\displaystyle a_{k}

\displaystyle=\|dP\|_{L^{2}(Z_{-k})}^{2},

\displaystyle b_{k}

\displaystyle=\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(Z_{-k})}^{2}.

If
\epsilon_{3}
is sufficiently small, then Lemma
A.4
implies that

a_{k}\leq\frac{\gamma}{2}(a_{k+1}+a_{k-1})+C_{4}\cdot b_{k}

for all
1\leq k\leq n-1
with
n=\lfloor-\ln R\rfloor
. Let
c_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\|dP\|_{L^{2}(A(R,1))}+\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(A(R,1))}
. Then
a_{k}\leq c_{0}^{2}
and

\displaystyle c_{0}^{r}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{A(R,1)}|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}dtds=\int_{[\ln R,0]\times S^{1}}|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}e^{(2-r)\rho}d\rho d\theta\geq\int_{Z_{-k}}e^{(r-2)k}|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{r}d\rho d\theta,

which implies that
b_{k}=\|\bar{\partial}_{J}P\|_{L^{r}(Z_{-k})}^{2}\leq c_{0}^{2}e^{-2k(r-2)/r}
, so
b_{k}
decays exponentially as
k\to\infty
. Now we can repeat the argument of
[
IS00
, Corollary 1.5]
to show that both
\sum a_{k}^{1/2}
and
\sum b_{k}^{1/2}
are bounded by a constant multiple of
c_{0}^{2}
. Now apply Lemma
A.2
to each
1\leq k\leq n-1
to obtain the diameter bound.
∎

A.3.
The third step

Finally, we prove Proposition
3.3
. Let
\epsilon_{\star}
be the minimum of the constants
\epsilon_{2}
and
\epsilon_{3}
in the Lemma
A.2
. Then the proof proceeds by induction for
C\leq n\epsilon_{\star}
. The base case when
n=1
is Lemma
A.2
. For any
n\geq 2
, consider the continuous function
h:B(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{+}

h(z)=\max\{0\leq R\leq 1-|z|:\int_{B(z,R)}|dP|^{2}+|\bar{\partial}_{J}P|^{2}\leq(n-1)\epsilon_{\star}\},

and let

\beta_{0}=\min_{|z|<\frac{3}{4}}\frac{h(z)}{\frac{3}{4}-|z|}.

If
\beta_{0}\geq\frac{1}{16}
, then for all
z\in B(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
h(z)\geq 1/64
. Then one can cover
B(0,\frac{1}{2})
using finitely many disks
B(z_{k},\frac{1}{32})
with each
|z_{k}|\leq\frac{1}{2}
and use the induction hypothesis to estimate
\diam(P(B(0,\frac{1}{2})))
.

If
\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{16}
, then the energy concentrates at a smaller scale. Suppose that this minimum is attained at some
z_{0}\in B(0,\frac{3}{4})
. Let
r_{1}=\frac{3}{4}-|z|
and
r_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}h(z_{0})=\beta_{0}\cdot r_{1}<r_{1}/16
. Consider the inclusion

B(z_{0},r_{0})\subset B(z_{0},2r_{0})\subset B(z_{0},\frac{r_{1}}{2})\subset B(z_{0},r_{1}).

First, the diameter of
P
on
B(z_{0},\frac{r_{1}}{2})\setminus B(z_{0},2r_{0})
can be estimated using Lemma
A.3
. Second, the region
B(0,\frac{1}{2})\setminus B(z_{0},\frac{r_{1}}{2})
can be covered using finitely many disks of radius
\frac{1}{32}
which are all disjoint from
B(z_{0},r_{0})
, then the diameter of
P
on
B(0,\frac{1}{2})\setminus B(z_{0},\frac{r_{1}}{2})
is estimated using Lemma
A.2
. Finally, for any
z\in B(z_{0},2r_{0})
, we have

h(z)\geq\beta_{0}\bigg(\frac{3}{4}-|z|\bigg)\geq r_{0}\cdot\frac{r_{1}-2r_{0}}{r_{1}}\geq\frac{r_{0}}{2}.

Now we cover
B(z_{0},2r_{0})
by finitely many disks of radius
\frac{r_{0}}{4}
, then the diameter of
P
on
B(z_{0},2r_{0})
can be estimated using the induction hypothesis. This finishes the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Appendix B
A Well-Known but Technical Lemma

This appendix is devoted to a well-known but technical lemma used in the proof of Lemma
11.16
.

Lemma B.1
.

Under the assumption of Lemma
11.16
, we have the following pointwise estimate for the
n
-th derivative of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
n\geq 1
. For any
c_{2}>0
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any smooth
\xi_{0},\xi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(B(0,1);P^{*}TM)
,
1\leq j\leq n
with
\|\xi_{0}\|_{\infty}<c_{2}
, we have

(B.1)

|(d_{n}\mathcal{F}_{0})(\xi_{0};\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{l})|<C(1+|dP|+|\nabla\xi_{0}|+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\nabla\xi_{j}|}{|\xi_{j}|})\prod_{j=1}^{n}|\xi_{j}|.

on
B(0,\frac{1}{2})
. This constant
C
depends on
c_{2}
and the
C^{n}
-norm of
\mathcal{X}
on a slightly larger compact subset of
M

(
which depends on
c_{2})
.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{r}_{1}(S;M)
denote the space of
L^{r}_{1}
-maps
P:S\to M
, and consider the Banach bundle:

\mathcal{E}^{r}\to\mathcal{B}^{r}_{1}(S;M),r>2,

whose fiber at
P
is
L^{r}(S;\Lambda^{0,1}S\otimes TM)
. If
M
and
S
are compact, then Lemma
B.1
implies that
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(P)=(dP-\mathcal{X})^{0,1}
defines a smooth section of
\mathcal{E}^{r}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is the expression of
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
in a local trivialization of
\mathcal{E}^{r}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

We decompose
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(P)
as

\frac{1}{2}(dP+J_{P}\circ dP\circ j_{S})+\mathcal{X}^{0,1}

where
j_{S}
is the complex structure on
S
. The estimate (
B.1
) is linear in
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
, so one may prove (
B.1
) for each of the three pieces separately. To start, suppose that
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(P)
takes the form
\mathcal{G}(P)\circ dP
, where
\mathcal{G}:TM\to TM
is any smooth bundle map. We are mostly interested in the case when
\mathcal{G}=\Id
or
J_{M}
, but the proof below works for a general
\mathcal{G}
.

Let
\exp:TM\to M
denote the exponential map and
\pi:TM\to M
the project map. Then the parallel transportation
\Psi
can be viewed as a smooth bundle isometry:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

Given any smooth sections
\xi_{0},\xi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(B(0,1);P^{*}TM)
,
1\leq j\leq n
, consider a bundle map
\hat{P}
which covers
P:B(0,1)\to M
:

(B.2)

\hbox to172.29pt{\vbox to52.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.14343pt\lower-25.19502pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{38.6539pt}{19.60913pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-25.95822pt}{-14.7381pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\hat{P}:S\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to TM,\\
\\
(z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})\mapsto(z,\sum_{j=0}^{n}t_{j}\xi_{j}).\end{array}

Now we consider the differential of the top row of (
B.2
) along
S
then apply
\mathcal{G}
:

(B.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(TM)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\hat{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

where
d\hat{P}
is given explicitly by the formula:

(B.4)

\displaystyle d\hat{P}:TS\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\to T(TM)=T^{h}(TM)\oplus T^{v}(TM)

\displaystyle(Y,z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto(dP(Y),\sum_{j=0}^{n}t_{j}\nabla_{Y}\xi_{j}),\ Y\in T_{z}S

Here the vertical and horizontal sub-bundles of
T(TM)
are defined using the Levi-Civita connection. One may view
d\exp
as a bundle map
T(TM)\to(\exp)^{*}TM
over
TM
and compose with
\Psi^{-1}
to obtain another digram

(B.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(TM)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp^{*}(TM)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp^{*}(TM)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\hat{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{P}}

The composition of the first row is the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\sum_{j=0}^{n}t_{j}\xi_{j})
, whose output is a smooth section of
P^{*}TM\to S
. Now fix some
(Y,z)\in TS
and let
t_{0}=1
. We shall differentiate
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\sum_{j=0}^{n}t_{j}\xi_{j})
in the variables
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}
and set them to be zero. In this case, the image of
d\hat{P}
lies in

T_{\hat{P}(z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})}(TM)\cong T_{\hat{P}(z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})}^{h}(TM)\oplus T_{\hat{P}(z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})}^{v}(TM)

which is isomorphic to
T_{P(z)}M\oplus T_{P(z)}M
canonically, so
\Psi^{-1}\circ d\exp
is a smooth family of endomorphism
T_{z}M\oplus T_{z}M\to T_{z}M
parametrized by
T_{\hat{P}(z,t_{0},\cdots,t_{n})}^{v}(TM)\cong T_{z}M
. Thus
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\sum_{j=0}^{n}t_{j}\xi_{j})
takes the form

(B.6)

(\Psi^{-1}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ d\exp)(t_{0}\xi_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\xi_{j})(dP(Y),t_{0}\nabla_{Y}\xi_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\nabla_{Y}\xi_{j}).

Since
\|\xi_{0}\|_{\infty}<c_{2}
, all
n
-th derivatives of
(\Psi^{-1}\circ\mathcal{G}\circ d\exp)
are bounded uniformly. It is clear that if (
B.6
) is differentiated along
t_{j},1\leq j\leq n
and evaluated at the origin, the final result is bounded by

C_{1}|Y|(|dP(z)|+|\nabla\xi_{0}(z)|+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{|\nabla\xi_{j}(z)|}{|\xi_{j}(z)|})\prod_{j=1}^{n}|\xi_{j}(z)|.

for some
C_{1}>0
. By taking
\mathcal{G}=\Id
and
J_{M}
, we obtain the desired estimate for
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(P)=dP
and
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(P)=J_{M}\circ dP\circ j_{S}
respectively (the complex rotation
j_{S}
is irrelevant for this estimate).

Finally, if
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{X}^{0,1}
, then the diagram (
B.3
) is replaced by

(B.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{*}TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{*}TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\hat{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\exp\circ\pi_{2})}

where
\pi_{1}:S\times M\to S
,
\pi_{2}:S\times M\to M
are projection maps. The diagram (
B.5
) is replaced by

(B.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{*}TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{*}TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\exp\circ\pi_{2})^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi\circ\pi_{2})^{*}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{X}^{0,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\hat{P})}

For any fixed
(Y,z)\in TS
, this diagram defines a smooth function
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to T_{z}M
which can be differentiated along
t_{j},1\leq j\leq n
and evaluated at the origin. This derivative is then bounded by

C_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{n}|\xi_{j}(z)|.

and this
C_{2}
depends at most on the
C^{n}
-norm of
\mathcal{X}
on a slightly larger compact subset of
M
(which depends on
c_{2}
). This completes the proof of Lemma
B.1
.
∎
